# Decisions

From 2026-11-06 each pull request adds a file to `decisions/` instead of lines here; this file is the record before that.

One line per choice the brief left open, with its reason. The brief and the owner's later instructions win over this file; the decision record (rationale) is background.

## Process

- The installed-app driver clicks an element once no outcome frame in its card is still loading (`data-sized`, set by the app once a frame has its height) and the element is where it was a tenth of a second before (2026-11-05, #207). Master runs 41969612463 and 42105845733 lost the review's Accept after the update test. The kept screenshot (`~/magnus-ci/shots/42105845733`) shows the card two minutes later with Accept enabled, and the task was still waiting on review. The card's outcome frame sits above the buttons and takes its height only once loaded, which moves Accept down. The driver had clicked within a quarter second of the card appearing, so on a busy runner the click landed on the frame. Raising that step's wait to 120 seconds (run 41969612463) treated the same bug as slowness. Test: `desktop_app.rs` `the_app_driver_clicks_a_button_once_its_card_has_stopped_moving`, against a stand-in WebDriver whose card frame loads 400 ms after the button is found.
- Only a runner with the release key and the appliance publishes, and master and tags run on the build box (2026-11-05, #204). Master run 42097633195 landed on `build-box-beta`, which now has `~/.magnus-release/update.key` and no `~/.magnus-appliance`. The key alone set `MAGNUS_PUBLISH=1`, so it published the test build into strix-beta's own updates directory, which no app reads, and failed at `Master on the build box's appliance`. `release/can-publish.sh` now requires the key and `MAGNUS_APPLIANCE_CONFIG`, and for a push (master, a tag) it fails at once without them. Pull requests still run on any `magnus-linux` runner. Master, tags and `appliance.yml` run on `[magnus-linux, magnus-light]`, which only build-box carries today (the earlier "build-box does not carry magnus-light" no longer holds). A label of its own (`magnus-appliance`) was not added: changing runner labels is a shared setting, and the pair already picks the box. Reason: publishing only counts where the owner's app reads, and a red master holds up the 0.2.5 release.
- The console's browser sign-in script opens each page through `goto()` (`desktop/tests/goto.mjs`), which loads it again, up to three times, only when Chromium answered `net::ERR_NETWORK_CHANGED` (2026-11-05, #171, release run 42032928589). Reason: appliance-v0.2.4's release failed two console tests at their first page with that error, 34 seconds apart, while every other test passed. Chromium aborts a load in flight when the machine's addresses change and leaves the retry to the caller; nothing in our tests adds an interface on the host (the mail sinks share Keycloak's network), so the change came from the shared build box. Any other failure still fails the step at once. A test-harness fix; no product change. Test: `the_console_sign_in_script_loads_a_page_again_when_the_network_changes` (`admin_sign_in.rs`), which fails while the script calls `page.goto` directly. The other desktop test scripts load pages the same way and are not changed here. No release run is rerun: appliance-v0.2.4's tag holds the old script, so the release goes out as the next version once this lands.
- The console's browser sign-in script waits 120 seconds for each step, not 60 (2026-11-05, run 41960251290). The password was accepted and the heading "Only admins can enter" was not visible before the old limit. Reason: the console was right and the build box was busy drawing it. Test: `the_console_sign_in_script_waits_two_minutes`.

- A schedule's own end notice is waited for for 180 seconds, not 90 (2026-11-05). The test's own minute budget is 100 seconds, and the notice wait was the shared 90-second patience, so a run that finished just after the minute was reported as missing. It failed that way on build-box three times (runs 41949527809, 41957157578, 41960121035). Reason: the schedule is due within a minute and then has to run. Test: `a_schedule_notice_outlasts_a_quiet_minute`.
- After an installed app updates, the test waits 120 seconds for "Accepted.", not 60 (2026-11-05, run 41969612463). The updated app was the new version and showed the release notes. The task that had waited through the update was approved, and the review's "Accepted." did not appear before the old limit while the other installed-app tests were still running. Reason: the update had worked. Test: `the_update_script_waits_two_minutes_to_accept_the_task_that_waited`.
- `graphify-out/` is not tracked. Reason: the graphify git hooks regenerate it on every commit and checkout.

- Built independently of other `~/magnus*` attempts on this machine; no code or decisions are copied. Reason: this workspace was started fresh for this brief.
- The invariant tests drive the appliance as a real process through its outside surfaces only (config file, `migrate`/`serve` CLI, HTTP API, Postgres log) plus the external protocols it speaks. Reason: the tests are a fixed contract that must survive internal rewrites, and crash tests need a process that can be SIGKILLed.
- Every external system in the invariant tests is a fake: a model provider speaking the Anthropic Messages API, MCP servers for the person's apps, and a website. Reason: the tests exercise the real adapter and MCP client paths without live accounts.
- The fake model is scripted and often scripted to misbehave (lie about completion, follow injected text, exfiltrate). Reason: the invariants must hold whatever the model decides, so the tests cannot rely on a well-behaved model.
- Edits to `tests/invariants/` are denied in `.claude/settings.json`. Reason: the owner approves changes there, and a permission rule enforces it structurally (X3).
- Dev and test Postgres is the `magnus-claude-pg` container on 127.0.0.1:54340. Reason: other projects on this machine use 54329 and 54330.
- Rust toolchain pinned to 1.99.1. Reason: the system cargo is 1.75, too old for current crates.
- I write the agent loop, because the brief asks me to build the whole system (the record's X14 says the principal writes it). It lives in one module (`crates/magnus/src/run/agent_loop.rs`); once the owner has reviewed it, edits to it are denied like the tests. Reason: the brief overrides the background record, and X14's protection still applies after review.

## Event log and state

- `public.events` is the only source of truth. Every other table is a derived projection that can be truncated, and `serve` rebuilds it from the log on boot. Reason: invariant 3 says the log is the source of truth, and truncation tests it directly.
- The kernel connects as a role that may only INSERT and SELECT on `events`; `migrate` creates that role and the schema with an admin URL. Reason: append-only is enforced by the database, not by kernel code.
- `migrate` gives a role the password in its URL percent-decoded, as the kernel sends it when it connects (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: it stored the URL's escaped text, so a password with `@`, `:`, `/` or `%` locked the kernel (or the console's root role) out of its own database. Test: `migrate_sets_the_password_the_kernel_signs_in_with` (log_review.rs).
- The vault is an encrypted file in the appliance state directory, not a database table. Reason: secrets must stay out of the log, and dropping derived tables must not lose them.

## Boundary and policy

- Under both taints, a send whose every recipient is an address the person wrote themselves passes the Rule of Two with no card (2026-11-05, #176; approvals spec, recipient provenance). The recipients are the ones the gate reads from the draft (`destinations()`), never the model's words. "Wrote themselves" means the task they started or their chat message, or their own messages in a chat task's conversation, never a tool result, the model's text (a chat task's text is the model's) or a schedule's text. Injected text can choose neither who they named nor who the draft goes to. That the content is what they wanted is theirs to judge (owner, 2026-11-04: the accepted risk that wrong content can reach an allowed recipient). Anything else asks as before: a send with recipients the gate cannot read, the audience rule for private data from another app, and policy asks. Addresses are read from free text by `tools::addresses_in`, each as a whole run of the characters an address can hold, so none is a piece cut from a longer one (`o'connor@` never reads as `connor@`; review of #213). Sent mail and the directory as sources are #210 and #211. Content provenance waits for scoped drafting (#178), since taint is kept per app. Tests: `inv4_taint.rs` `an_allowed_recipient_passes_under_both_taints` and `a_new_address_in_the_same_run_asks` (protected; approved 2026-11-04, applied with the owner's OK 2026-11-05).
- A web lookup judges an IPv4 address written as IPv6 (`::ffff:a.b.c.d`) as that IPv4 address, and takes an IP written in the URL as that address (2026-11-05, #196). `::ffff:127.0.0.1`, `::ffff:10.0.0.1` and `::ffff:169.254.169.254` passed the private-address check, and a name resolving to one would have been fetched. An IPv6 literal kept its brackets in `host_str`, so it failed DNS lookup before any check. It is now checked like any address, which is what made the full fetch reproducible. Redirect hops and the pinned connection go through the same check. `allow_private_networks` still allows them. Test: `web.rs` `a_mapped_ipv4_address_is_refused_like_the_address_it_maps`, a real fetch of `http://[::ffff:127.0.0.1]:port/` from a local server, which was fetched before the fix.
- A connector read through the person's own sign-in (`auth = "oauth"`, `Risk::Read`) does not count as sending, under any taint (2026-11-05, #175; approvals spec, build order 3). Before, any read of an app whose audience is wider than private was sending, so after one email every further Gmail, Slack or Calendar read asked a once-only Rule of Two card. A read's query goes only to the provider that already holds the person's data, through their own account, and reaches no one there, so the card guarded nothing. It also kept the verifier, which cannot wait for a card, from reading mail. A read through the organisation's key, web lookups and the browser keep the rule: their inputs can reach others. Owner, 2026-11-05: remove approvals wherever a system can decide without giving up security; Muse asks nothing for reads. Test: `gmail.rs` `having_read_mail_gmail_searches_again_without_asking_and_a_web_lookup_still_asks`.
- Browser navigation accepts only HTTP and HTTPS, and snapshots/screenshots refuse non-web pages except the initial blank page (2026-11-04, `browser-local-access-fix`). Reason: a model could navigate to an appliance-host file and read it as ordinary untrusted web content; executable and browser-internal URLs are not website navigation. The brokered-credential workspace redesign is separate work, not a reason to leave this direct read open.

- Cedar actions: `execute` (act without asking) and `request_approval`. Outcome: Allow if `execute` is permitted, Ask if only `request_approval` is, otherwise Deny. Reason: Cedar has permit and forbid but no ask effect.
- Cedar entities: `Person` in `Group`; `Tool::"<app>__<tool>"` in `App`, with attributes `risk` and `audience`; context carries the run's `untrusted` and `private` taint. Reason: these are the facts the boundary decides on (B4, B9, A6).
- Layering: an action is permitted only if the organization layer permits it and no layer (org, each of the person's groups, user, task) forbids it. A permit in a lower layer never widens. Reason: E1 makes the organization the ceiling and lets lower layers only narrow; deny wins.
- The task layer is `task/<task id>`, set with `PUT /v1/policies/task/<id>` by the owner or the task's own person (it only narrows), and evaluated last for that task's actions; the console's policy test takes an optional `task` (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: the layering above names a task layer, and there was none: the kernel evaluated org, groups and user only. The task's person reads it back with the rest of their layers (`GET /v1/policies`), and the console drafts, applies and rolls it back like any set (`task:<id>`, a task that exists) (review of #157). Tests: `a_tasks_own_layer_forbids_what_it_names_for_that_task_alone` (task_policy.rs), `a_task_layer_is_a_policy_set` (admin/policy.rs).
- With no organization policy written, the default is execute for read, reversible_write and sandbox, and Ask for everything else. Reason: nothing irreversible runs without owner-written policy, and the product stays usable out of the box.
- An accepted policy proposal appends its rules to the target layer. Reason: a proposal is a change to review, not a replacement of the owner's whole policy.
- The `prohibited` class is a hard Deny that no policy or approval lifts. A tool the owner has not classified cannot be called. Reason: fail closed (B9).
- Rule of Two is fixed in the kernel, per K47. When a run holds both untrusted and private taint, every non-read action and every web fetch or search asks. Reason: a fetched URL can carry private data out (EchoLeak), and K47 allows stricter-than-K34.
- Audience rule: a run holding private taint that sends to an `internal` or `public` destination asks, even when the destination is allowlisted. Reason: B4.
- Approvals lift Ask only, never Deny. Approvers need the `owner` role or `approver:<risk class>`. Scope is once, task, time window or standing, and every approval is bound to the exact action. Reason: B5, E7.
- Where an action goes is read from what it acts on, never from what the model wrote (2026-11-04, `send-reads-the-draft`; approvals spec step 2). A connector declares it per tool (`[destinations.<tool>]`: the read tool, the id field, JSON pointers to recipients and content); Gmail's `send_draft` is read through `get_draft` (To, Cc, Bcc; subject, text, attachments). The kernel reads it with the person's own sign-in after the budget and before policy, puts it on the approval card, and binds a once approval's hash to it, so a draft edited after approval (one added Bcc included) asks again. If it cannot be read, the action is refused and nothing is sent. Addresses are lowercased exact matches; a mailing list is one address. What is read never reaches the model. Reason: the card showed only a draft id, and an approval of a draft id covered whatever the draft said when sent. Left open: the draft can still change in the moment between that read and the send.
- A person's rights come from the groups the identity provider gives them; an app lists `access_groups`. Missing rights is Deny, not Ask. Reason: E2; an approval must not get around the person's own access.
- Tool definitions are pinned by hash on first use; a changed definition is disabled and hidden from the model until the owner re-approves it. Reason: B10.
- The idempotency key reaches apps as an `Idempotency-Key` HTTP header (and in MCP `_meta`). After a crash, an app without idempotency support gets no resend: the intent is marked unknown and the person is asked. Reason: B7.

## Runs and verification

- The agent must record a task contract (`task_contract`) before any non-read action and before completing. Reads are allowed before it so the agent can scope the task. Reason: A1 says contract before acting; reading to write a good contract is not acting.
- Contract checks may read or run in the sandbox (tests, demos), the same class the verifier has; never write, send or touch memory. The kernel runs them as written when the agent reports completion, and records the result as evidence. Supersedes "checks must call read-only tools". Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28), K26, K39, K42; a test run is the natural check for built work.
- A sandbox check runs in a VM of its own: booted from the image, with a throwaway copy of the run's workspace (the run's VM is stopped first so the disk is consistent). In a check VM the workspace is mounted noexec, HOME is a fresh /tmp/home, Python ignores user site-packages (`PYTHONNOUSERSITE`) and the working directory (`PYTHONSAFEPATH`), and the image's `sitecustomize.py` puts every path from outside the image after the image's own, with the working directory last. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28), after the rerun passed its pytest check with a stand-in pytest on the user site; the working directory goes last rather than away because `python3 -m pytest` and `unittest discover` need it to import the work.
- A check's programs come from the sandbox image, and nothing may change where programs and modules are looked up. Each check command is resolved when the contract is recorded, by `check_tools.py` in the image, in a fresh VM with an empty workspace; it refuses a bare program the image lacks, variables that move lookups (PATH, PYTHON*, LD_*, HOME and the like), `python3 -E`/`-S`, `source`/`.`/`eval`/`alias`/`hash`, and a workspace file executed directly (the check VM mounts the workspace noexec; the refusal says to run it with its interpreter). Workspace runners are not refused. Supersedes "a check may run only tools in the image; workspace files only as what a tool runs". Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28): "Don't refuse workspace runners. Label instead."
- Each criterion with a check is labelled by the kernel when the contract is recorded (so the frozen contract carries it): `self-checked` when its check executes any workspace file (a script given to an interpreter or shell; `python3 -m` of a module not in the image or of a test runner such as pytest or unittest; `python3 -c` code that imports a module from outside the image or can launch code (subprocess, os.system, exec, eval, importlib, pickle, …); a program that runs code from the folder (make, npm, node, git, xargs, …); `find -exec`; `awk`/`sed`/`jq -f`), with the reasons; otherwise `appliance-verified` (image tools such as test, grep, diff, `python3 -m json.tool`, and read-only tool checks, all judged by the kernel against the contract's expected values). Doubt counts as self-checked. Labels the model sends are discarded. The contract card and contract-change card show the label, the agent is told which of its checks are self-checked, and a finished task reads "Done: N appliance-verified, M self-checked" (plus how many a person confirmed). Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28), mechanical at freeze; a verdict from code the agent wrote is the agent's own word.
- Magnus's instructions name no workspace runner as an allowed check; they describe the labels. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28); the earlier example endorsed a change the owner declined.
- Of the sandbox tools, only `sandbox_exec` and `sandbox_read_file` may be checks. Reason: `deliver_file` and the desktop tools act on the run's own VM and deliver output; a check only observes.
- pytest is in the sandbox image. Reason: it is the usual checking tool, and its absence is what led the agent to fake one.
- A check's result is evidence, not model input: reading it does not taint the run, and the check is judged by the taint the run had when the contract was written. A contract change a person approved keeps the run's taint at approval; it no longer clears it (review, 2026-11-04): approving the criteria is not the Rule of Two's one-action approval of each send a check makes, and a run that had read private data and untrusted content could approve its way to a `web_fetch` carrying the data. Test: `an_approved_contract_change_keeps_the_taint_its_checks_are_judged_by` (contract_freeze.rs). The model sees only which expectation failed (pointer and expected value), never what the check observed. Reason: owner instruction "taint limits authority, not reading"; the model never reads it, so nothing can be injected.
- The contract freezes when work starts: the first model action that is not a read, or the first `task_complete`. Before that the agent revises freely. After it, a changed contract goes to the inbox as a `contract_change` card with old and new side by side; until approved the frozen criteria decide Done, and a declined change leaves them in force. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28), after the "build your replacement" run reached Done by rewriting its contract to a trivial criterion.
- A `sandbox_exec` check that names the exit code it expects (`/exit_code`) is decided by that code, not failed for exiting nonzero (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: the command ran; `grep -q TODO` exiting 1 is the criterion holding, and the contract validator accepted such checks that could then never pass, so the agent looped until lost-detection or the budget stopped it. A command that could not run (no exit code) still counts as an error. Test: `a_check_expecting_a_nonzero_exit_passes_when_the_command_exits_so` (contract_freeze.rs).
- A criterion whose check cannot run (refused by the boundary, or the tool failed) goes to the person on the review card with the reason, unchanged; it is never replaced or counted as passed. Reason: K37; owner instruction.
- Once a task has ended, plan units still marked active or pending show as "not marked done". Reason: the plan is the agent's claim; a finished task cannot have work in progress.
- A criterion without an executable check goes to the person as a review card when the verifier is off or cannot tell; the run suspends. With the verifier on, its verdict decides: false goes back to the agent, true counts as verified. Reason: K37; the verifier is quality only (R1), and the owner turns it on.
- Removable modules (verifier, lost-reset, escalation) each declare what they compensate for and their ablation suite, are switched for every model and per model in `[modules]`, and are pinned in the run's configuration; experiment trials can switch them, and their ablation is the same cases with a module on and off. Lost-reset and escalation are on by default (they act only on a run already going in circles); the verifier stays off unless `[verifier] enabled`. Reason: the record's Modules section and the alpha gate (item 3); the verifier costs model calls on every task.
- Lost-detection uses OpenHands' stuck-detector signals and defaults (software-agent-sdk `StuckDetectionThresholds`, commit 095883242a0b), configurable in `[modules.lost]`. A first detection resets (the loop leaves the context, a note names what still happened), the next escalates to `escalate_to`, the next suspends for a person. Escalation does not rewrite the run's pinned configuration; it is an event. Reason: a published, tested detector instead of invented thresholds; experiments group runs by the configuration they started with.
- The verifier reads through `execute()` as its own issuer: read-only tools or the check VM, never an approval wait, and its reads taint the run. Reason: the record's verifier rows; its words reach the agent, so what it read must count as read.
- A model reply with no tool call is a malformed step. It goes back to the model as an error and does not end the run. Reason: K29; only the model's `task_complete` or `task_fail`, or the principal, ends a run (K40).
- An exhausted budget or a persistent provider failure suspends the run with an inbox notice. The principal resumes it on the pinned model. Reason: K24, K12.
- A rate limit (`usage_limit`) is retried like an outage, up to the model's `max_retries`, waiting the longer of the backoff and the provider's `retry-after`, unless the provider asks for longer than the longest backoff (32 seconds), which counts as persistent (a spent quota) and suspends as before (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: every 429 suspended the run at once, pausing the task and its schedule until a person resumed it, for a per-minute limit that would have passed in seconds; the decision suspends only on a persistent failure. Test: `a_rate_limit_is_retried_rather_than_pausing_the_task` (models.rs).
- A model call the provider charged for and then failed (its reply could not be used, or the stream broke after the cost was reported) counts its dollars and tokens against the run, like a reply's: the adapter's error carries the usage it had reported, and `ModelError` records it (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: the cost was dropped with the error, so repeated unusable replies were paid for but never reached the run's dollar budget. Each attempt already counted against the model-call limit. Tests: `a_failed_turn_keeps_what_it_cost` (adapters.rs), `a_charged_call_that_failed_counts_against_the_budget` (state.rs).

## Identity

- In v1 the appliance config lists people with their groups and roles (`owner`, `approver:<risk>`, `policy:user`, `policy:group:<id>`); each person has an API token for the desktop app. Reason: K46 requires groups from an identity provider, the appliance needs a local one to start, and OIDC can replace it behind the same interface.
- The appliance is reachable on all interfaces at port 7700, now over HTTPS (`tls_listen = "0.0.0.0:7700"`); plain HTTP moved to loopback (`listen = "127.0.0.1:7701"`) for scripts on the appliance itself. Its helpers (browser sidecar, search, Postgres) stay on loopback. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-27) that people's apps reach the appliance over the network; HTTPS on the same port keeps the firewall rule and stops passwords and tokens crossing the LAN in clear.

## Models

- Model providers are separate adapter processes behind one versioned contract the kernel speaks over a local socket. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-27).

## Sandbox

- Guest kernel: Firecracker CI `vmlinux-6.1.155` (v1.15 artifacts). Reason: upstream-tested with Firecracker 1.16 and has vsock and virtio-blk built in.
- The jailer is used when the appliance runs as root. Tests run Firecracker unjailed as an unprivileged user in the `kvm` group. Reason: the jailer needs root for chroot and cgroups.

## Evaluation

- TheAgentCompany: held out. No task is run against Magnus before one full run of all 175 for the reported score, and nothing is tuned on it; supersedes "a handful of tasks while developing". Spend cap asked once per-task cost is measured. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28) that it is held out; published scores are over all 175, and at a ~30% pass rate a 40-task sample has a ±14-point 95% interval against ±7 for the full set.
- Live provider sign-in happens through the app (API-key entry, OAuth links); tokens go straight to the vault. Reason: owner's choice (2026-10-27).
- Two adapters cover the five providers: `anthropic` (Messages API) and one OpenAI-compatible adapter configured per provider (Chat Completions or Responses, API key or OAuth, base URL, extra headers) for GLM (api.z.ai), Muse (api.meta.ai), Codex (chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex) and Grok (cli-chat-proxy.grok.com). Reason: owner instruction that OpenAI-compatible providers share one adapter; research in docs/research/providers-and-tac.md.
- Codex and Grok sign in with device codes, using their own CLIs' public clients: Codex as `codex login --device-auth` does it (OpenAI's `deviceauth` endpoints, then a code exchange), Grok as `grok login --device-auth` does it (RFC 8628 at auth.x.ai, client `b1a00492-…`, scopes `grok-cli:access api:access`). The person approves on the provider's site and the appliance polls until they have; nothing is pasted back. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-27); a browser redirect lands on the person's own machine, not the appliance.
- Provider client versions are looked up, never pinned: a preset names where the provider publishes its own client's current version (`client_version_url`: npm for the Codex CLI, `x.ai/cli/stable` for the Grok CLI) and how often to read it again, and the adapter fills `{client_version}` in the model-list path and headers. Reason: measured 2026-10-27, Codex filters its model list by `client_version` (0.0.0 lists 6 older models, 0.1.0 none) and Grok refuses versions below 1.0.13; a pinned number goes stale; owner instruction that variables are never hardcoded.
- A conformance run that names no model uses the appliance's task model only if this adapter serves it, otherwise the adapter's first listed model (a fixed version first); the CLI requires `--model` in that case. Reason: testing one provider with another's model fails every check.
- The Settings model picker lists the chosen adapter's catalog; a current model the provider does not offer is shown as such. Reason: owner instruction; a typed id like `4.7` silently broke new tasks.
- The adapter contract adds a fifth error class, `invalid_request` (for example, context too long), to the four the owner named. Reason: the kernel must tell a rejected request (no retry) from an unavailable provider (retry).
- One connection per adapter request over a Unix socket, newline-delimited JSON. Reason: a replaced adapter takes new requests on its new socket while in-flight requests finish on the old one, with no kernel restart.
- The kernel passes the vault credential in each request and stores any refreshed credential the adapter returns; adapters keep no token state. Reason: tokens come from the vault, and a replaced adapter must not lose sign-ins.
- While the kill switch is engaged, the boundary refuses every action and the vault is sealed: nothing is read from it or stored in it, across restarts. Engaging it (one owner control, on Home) refuses every action, suspends every run, and revokes every OAuth sign-in at its provider (RFC 7009, at the revocation endpoint the provider's discovery document publishes) and takes it out of the vault. API keys and app secrets are kept, sealed: no provider can be asked to revoke them from here, and deleting the local copy would not stop a leaked one, so the owner is told to revoke a suspect key in its provider's console. Releasing it unseals the vault and resumes nothing (K53); the owner signs in again and resumes each task. Revocation at the provider runs after the vault is sealed and its answer is recorded, so a slow provider does not delay the stop. Reason: B11 as the owner restated it for the alpha gate (2026-10-29), "revoke vault tokens"; this replaces "the vault itself is kept; provider-side revocation is left to the adapters". Keeping keys and app secrets also keeps the owner-approved invariant (a resumed task's action runs after release).
- Canonical JSON (keys sorted at every level) for every hash: approval bindings, idempotency, tool pins. Reason: Postgres jsonb reorders object keys, so a call read back from the log must hash like the original.
- The audience rule asks only when private content read from a different app goes to an internal or public audience; content returning to the app it came from reaches no one new. The Rule of Two is unchanged and still gates every outbound action once untrusted and private content have both been read. Reason: B4's documented leaks (GitHub MCP, EchoLeak) all involved injected content, which the Rule of Two stops; without the exemption, posting to a team chat twice would ask because the chat's own acknowledgement carries the chat's label.
- Files attached to a task are labeled untrusted: the model sees them (images and PDFs as content, every file at /workspace/inputs/), but they instruct nothing. Reason: a document from elsewhere can carry injected text; only the person's task text instructs (invariant 4).
- Voice input becomes text on the appliance (local speech-to-text), and the person sends that text as the task, so the instruction is what the person confirmed. Reason: voice is a way to type, not a separate channel.

## Security review fixes (2026-10-27, clean-context review)

- Labels are a set: a source can be `private+untrusted` (a mailbox: the person's mail, written by others). Reason: with one label per source, an injected email plus a URL fetch passed the Rule of Two (EchoLeak).
- Memory entries keep the taint and source apps of the run that wrote them, and a search result carries the combined taint of what it returns. Reason: memory otherwise launders private or injected content into later runs (M1).
- Taint asks (Rule of Two, audience) override a policy ask and accept only a one-action approval of the exact input; task, window and standing scopes cover policy asks only. Only the owner grants standing approvals; windows last at most 24 hours. Reason: a broad approval given before an injection must not cover what the injection asks for, and standing approval is autonomy (invariant 6).
- Approvals record why they were asked for; an approval for an unknown outcome is used only to resend that call. Reason: otherwise approving a resend could be consumed as a policy approval, and the person would be asked forever.
- With both taints, a call to any app with an audience wider than the person counts as transmitting, reads included. Reason: a search query carries its inputs to the app.
- Web fetch follows redirects itself, checks each hop's address, and connects to the address it checked. Reason: redirects and DNS rebinding otherwise reach private and cloud-metadata addresses.
- Contract checks may not use memory tools, may not expect null or an empty substring, and fail when the pointer does not resolve; a contract has at most 20 criteria. Reason: checks must look at the world (invariant 7), and a vacuous check is no check.
- Redaction happens at one place, inside the MCP client, for every string an app returns (results, structured data, errors, tool descriptions and schemas). The kill switch is checked before any stage that touches a credential. Reason: echoed credentials leaked through error paths and tool listings.
- The text of a tool definition is shown to the model only after the owner approves it (one review item per app on first sight); until then the model sees the tool's name, risk class and a schema without descriptions. Reason: tool descriptions are third-party text (B10); pinning only caught later changes.
- Apps are not assumed to deduplicate: `idempotency` defaults to `none`, and only `header` allows a resend after a crash. Reason: a wrong assumption repeats a side effect.
- Run-record inbox items (questions, proposals, messages) take their id from the call, so a crash cannot duplicate them. Agent notes are `message` items that show the run's taint, and only the kernel's own notices can extend a budget or continue a run. Reason: an injected agent must not be able to impersonate the kernel's notices.
- Firecracker starts with an empty environment. The jailer is still to be wired for the root-run appliance (PROGRESS.md). Reason: defense in depth against a VMM escape.
- The browser is Chromium in a host-side sidecar (Node, playwright-core) reached over a Unix socket, one isolated context per run, with no saved logins. Navigation and snapshots are reads; clicks, typing and selects are reversible writes by default. Everything it returns is untrusted, and with both taints every browser call asks. Reason: the sandbox has no network, so web use must run outside it behind the boundary; with fresh contexts per run the browser holds no sessions that could pay or post as the person.
- Computer use runs in the run's own microVM: an Xvfb desktop with LibreOffice and other X apps, driven with xdotool, screenshots returned as images. Reason: desktop apps need no network, and the VM is already the isolation boundary.
- The guest agent stops collecting a command's output shortly after the shell exits, even if background processes keep the pipes open. Reason: `app &` is how the model starts desktop apps, and it must not hang the call.
- Files leave a run only through `deliver_file`, which copies them into the log as outputs the person downloads from the task view; the workspace is deleted when the run ends. Reason: K19, and a non-technical person needs the spreadsheet, not a path inside a VM.
- Recurring tasks are created by people; the agent can only propose one (`propose_schedule`), which starts only if the person accepts. A scheduled task acts for the person who set it up. Reason: a schedule is a standing instruction, and an injected agent must not be able to plant future tasks (invariant 4); K54.
- The desktop app is a Tauri 2 app for Linux and Windows (replacing the Electron shell). Its Rust side holds the session token in the OS keyring, pins the appliance's certificate, and proxies only `/v1/*` calls; the page never sees the token. Reason: the kernel is Rust, the installers are small, and the WebKit development packages are now on the build box.
- The app and the appliance's browser page share one frontend (plain JavaScript, no build step) behind a small bridge: Tauri commands in the app, `fetch` with a session cookie in the browser. Reason: one set of views to test, and no bundler to keep current.
- People sign in to apps with a name and password; Argon2id hashes and session-token hashes live in `state/accounts/` (mode 0600), never in the log, which records only that a session opened or closed. The owner sets passwords with `magnus password` on the appliance. Repeated failures lock the name for a while; unknown names cost the same work as known ones. Config tokens still work for scripts. Reason: a session token is a credential (invariant 2), and the owner should not have to hand out long-lived keys.
- Browser sessions use an HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict cookie, and any change made with the cookie must carry an `x-magnus-client` header. Reason: a cookie is sent by the browser on its own; another site's page cannot set that header.
- The HTTPS port answers plain HTTP with a 308 redirect to the same host and path over https; it tells the two apart by the first byte (a TLS handshake record is 0x16). The host comes from the request only when it looks like a host, else the address the connection arrived on. Reason: the owner typed the address without `https://` on Windows and the browser showed the TLS alert bytes as strange characters (2026-10-28); a redirect is what every web server does here.
- People sign in through an OpenID Connect provider when `[sign_in.oidc]` is set (the dev appliance uses the existing Keycloak, realm `magnus` at identity.pyrallon.ai, client `magnus-claude`). The flow is the device-code grant (RFC 8628): the appliance asks the provider for a code, the app opens the provider's page, the person approves there, and the appliance collects the ID token itself and opens an ordinary Magnus session. The ID token's `preferred_username` (configurable) must be a configured person's id: who may sign in at all stays with the owner's `[[people]]`. Where the owner names the claims (`groups_claim`, `roles_claim`, JSON pointers; Keycloak: `/groups`, `/realm_access/roles`), the groups and roles in the ID token replace the config's at each sign-in, are recorded in the log, and every decision about that person uses them (the boundary, policy layers, approvals) until their next sign-in. Keycloak group paths lose their leading slash. Endpoints come from the provider's discovery document, and the poll interval and expiry from its answers. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28) to use the installed Keycloak; device code is the flow the owner asked for elsewhere, needs no redirect to the person's machine, and keeps passwords out of Magnus. Rights from the provider: E2 as the owner restated it for the alpha gate (2026-10-29).
- The ID token is not signature-checked: it comes straight from the provider's token endpoint over verified TLS, which OpenID Connect Core 3.1.3.7 (step 6) allows instead. Issuer, audience, `azp` and expiry are checked. No provider access or refresh token is kept. Reason: the smallest correct check; a JWT library would add nothing in this flow.
- The Keycloak client is public, with only the device grant on (no redirects, no direct password grant). `config/keycloak-client.py` creates or updates it through Keycloak's admin API, run by the owner as root. Reason: the appliance needs no client secret to read who signed in, and the admin credential stays with the owner (the agent's sudo use of it was refused by the safety check).
- Passwords stay open when an identity provider is set, as the backup way in. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28) for a backup way in on this dev server, so a Keycloak or Cloudflare outage does not lock everyone out.
- At most `sign_in.oidc.max_pending` (default 100) provider sign-ins wait at once. Reason: starting one needs no credential, so this bounds what strangers can make the appliance hold; expired ones are dropped first.
- The appliance serves HTTPS when `appliance.tls_listen` is set, with the owner's certificate or a self-signed one it makes and keeps; apps pin the certificate's SHA-256 on first connection, and the fingerprint is printed at start so the owner can compare. Password sign-in over plain HTTP is refused except from the appliance itself. Reason: passwords and session tokens must not cross the network in clear, and a home appliance has no public name to get a certificate for.
- The developer view (runs, raw log, experiments, live panels, policy history, playbook) needs a `developer` role; the operator view shows only derived views. Reason: K10, K45.
- The agent keeps a plan with `task_plan`: units with id, goal, done-when and status; each call is a new version, checked for shape only. Statuses are the agent's own account; the contract's checks are what the kernel trusts. Reason: K36.
- The agent's playbook is per person, changed only through `playbook_update` (add, edit, retire), and each change is labeled with the taint of the run that made it; a run's taint starts with the labels of the items it is given. The playbook version is part of the run configuration. Reason: I2, I3; an item written while reading untrusted content must not launder it into later runs.
- An outcome may carry a composition of fixed components (heading, text, list, table, stat, bar chart, line chart, image of a delivered file), checked for shape by the kernel and drawn by the app's own code in a sandboxed frame, and a separate script for reading aloud. Reason: U7, U8: the agent chooses what to show, never how it runs.
- Experiment trials are tasks tagged with experiment, case and trial; graders post verdicts as `Graded` events; pass@k and pass^k use the unbiased estimators over settled trials. A trial passes when every grader passed it, or, with no grader, when the kernel completed it, and the report says which. Reason: V2; one log for both views (V5).
- Failure tags come from the log: grader disagrees, verification, boundary, provider, agent, budget, waiting on a person, kill switch, cancelled, stalled (no event for `observability.stall_minutes`). Reason: V2 asks where failures happen; each tag is a fact in the log, not a guess.
- An inbox item is blocking when a run is paused waiting on it; apps interrupt only for those and batch the rest. Approval cards state the consequence of irreversible, financial and repeat-unknown actions on the card itself. Reason: U3, U6.
- Version changes (harness, instructions, model, playbook, each policy layer) are listed at `/v1/versions` from the log; apps notify when they change. Reason: U5.
- Where the OS has no keyring (no Secret Service), the app keeps the session token in a file readable only by the user, in its settings folder. Reason: a session must survive a restart, and headless or minimal desktops have no keyring; the token is revocable by signing out.
- The app makes an OS notification only for blocking inbox items and for version changes; everything else waits in the inbox. Approve buttons exist only on the inbox card, never in a notification or the task view. Reason: U3, U8.
- A contract check observes a tool's structured result with the result's text beside it (`/text`). Reason: a fetched page's structured result holds only its content type, so a check could never see the page.
- Windows installers are cross-built on Linux for `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu` and bundled with NSIS (per-user install, WebView2 bootstrapper); Linux ships as .deb and AppImage. Reason: one build box, no MSVC toolchain or licence download.
- The app is tested by behavior: the shared frontend in Chromium against a real appliance (`desktop_flow`), and the built Linux app through tauri-driver over HTTPS (`desktop_app`). The Windows build runs its unit tests under Wine; its WebView2 window cannot run there, so the owner's recorded run is its end-to-end test. Reason: U7; test what a person does, not what the page looks like.
- Web search is a local SearXNG instance. Reason: no API key or third-party search account, and queries stay under the appliance's control.
- Chat: a person's message is a chat turn, a run without a contract (`TaskCreated.chat`, turn = true) that ends with its first reply that calls no tool; that text is the answer, with no Done check. It runs under its own versioned instructions (`instructions/chat.md`, `chat.md@<hash>` in the run's config), which leave the model to decide what each message is: answer, `create_task`, `create_schedule`, or a question back. No code classifies messages. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28).
- A chat turn may only read, plus `create_task` and `create_schedule`; those two exist only in chat. The boundary enforces it (a tool not offered is still denied), and run records (contract, plan, complete, ask) are refused in chat. Writing memory is a task's job too. Reason: owner instruction: any tool use goes through execute() and the log with taint and the Rule of Two; changes happen where they are checked and approved.
- `create_task` starts the task at once, for the person, with ids derived from the call's idempotency key (a resend after a restart finds the same task), the turn's taint as its starting taint, and the conversation in its context and on its contract (`contract.conversation`). It is not counted as sending data out: nothing leaves the appliance, and the inherited taint makes the Rule of Two apply to what the task sends. Reason: owner instruction; I2.
- `create_schedule` only shows a card in the thread (timing, scope, per-run budget, who it acts for); only the thread's person can confirm or decline it, once. A confirmed schedule acts for its creator (K54), with the card's budget (the appliance's, tightened only) and the turn's taint on each run. Reason: owner instruction; K54.
- A thread is derived from the log: its turns, the tasks they started with their results, and the cards with their decisions. Each turn records the thread as it stood when the person wrote (`ChatOrigin.conversation`), so its context is fixed and only grows. Threads are their starter's alone; chat turns are not listed in Tasks; approvals and questions stay in the inbox, and the thread links to them. Reason: owner instruction; C1.
- A conversation goes on until the person starts a new one: Chat opens their latest thread, a sidebar lists their threads, and only the "New conversation" button starts another. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28); opening Chat to a blank composer made every visit a one-turn thread.
- Anyone signed in may choose the model for a task or a chat message from `GET /v1/models`, which lists each adapter's catalog as its provider reports it at that moment; a choice is checked against that catalog when sent, and the appliance's default needs no check. The run pins it (K9). A task started from chat runs on the turn's model; schedules use the appliance's default. The app remembers each viewer's last choice in local storage. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28); budgets still bound every run, and the list comes from the providers, not from the app.
- A credential an adapter refreshes while listing its models goes to the vault, like one refreshed during a model call, and the catalog returns only the list. Reason: Grok's refresh made its catalog answer with the token itself, so its models were missing from the list and the owner-only catalog endpoint handed the token to the caller.
- Each desktop build handed out gets its own version and its own pre-release (`desktop-v<version>`); a release with new features bumps the minor version. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28); the installer date should not be the only way to tell builds apart.
- Tasks are grouped by the request that started them: a chat message, or a schedule; a typed task is a group of one. Sections come in the order Needs you, Running, Waiting, Done, Failed, and a group sits in the section of its most pressing task (a group with a failure and nothing active is under Failed). A task needs you when it waits on an open inbox item you may answer, the inbox's own rule. Cancelled tasks count under Failed. Done and Failed show their 15 most recent groups, the rest on request. No projects, tags or folders. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28), for volume.
- The model titles what it starts: `create_task` requires a short `title` (refused without one), `task_contract` and `create_schedule` take one, and lists fall back to the start of the person's text. `create_task`'s description also says to start one task per independent piece of work. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28); a title is what a list of dozens of tasks can show.
- In chat, the tasks one message started are one card with each task's live status, and each result is a folded one-liner placed after the reply to that message. Scheduled runs are listed under their schedule in Schedules (next run, last result, runs folded beneath), and in Tasks grouped under the schedule. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28).
- "One screen" in tests is the desktop app's window as `tauri.conf.json` sets it (1200 × 840). Reason: that is the screen the person has; the number is read from the app's config, not repeated.
- A check may only read fields its tool's result has. Built-in tools have a fixed shape (sandbox_exec: /exit_code, /stdout, /stderr, /timed_out, /truncated, /text; sandbox_read_file and web_search: /text; web_fetch: /text, /content_type; browser: /text, /image_b64), listed in `task_contract`'s description, and a contract or contract change whose check reads any other field is refused when proposed, with the fields it may read, before any card reaches the person. A file read is never parsed as JSON for a check; a connector's read is its JSON, unchecked until it runs. Reason: approving a contract change "did not work" (2026-10-28): each approval took effect, but the check read /content, which a file read never has, so it failed, the model asked again, and the person was asked again.
- The owner's request set is the 20 numbered requests they typed into chat on 2026-10-28 (22:58-23:00), taken from the log into `eval/requests/requests.json` with the list numbers removed. Request 8 ("Send that reply.") continues request 7's conversation, since it refers to it. Reason: owner instruction to run "my 20-request set"; the log is where it is.
- The request set runs on four models (gpt-6.1-sol, gpt-6-luna, grok-4.7, grok-4.7-build-fast), each request as a chat message that is a trial of one experiment, each model as its own eval person (`eval-1` to `eval-4`, developer role, the org policy only) so no model sees another's memory or chats. Nothing is approved; once a request has settled, what it left running is cancelled and any schedule card declined. A request settles when its reply and every task it started first ended or stopped (for a person, or to wait); its time runs to that moment, and cancelling afterwards does not change it. Policy stops are the ask and deny decisions in the reply and its tasks. A request still going after 30 minutes is stopped and marked as timed out. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-28), "flagship + small per family"; 30 minutes keeps a model's 20 requests within an evening, and nothing that needs approval may happen in a test.
- The owner's judgment of each choice is a grade from grader `choice`, set with ✓ or ✗ in the Experiments view; for every grader the latest grade counts, so a judgment can be changed. Reason: owner instruction that they supply the correctness judgments.
- TheAgentCompany, the owner's choices (2026-10-28): no environment LLM, so the NPC replies and LLM-graded checkpoints (about 90 tasks) score falsely low and the result is not comparable with published scores; strict mode (a stop for approval ends the task, graded as it stands); task containers on their own Docker bridge network with one firewall rule letting them reach the api-server on port 2999, so the agent's shell cannot reach this host's loopback.
- `task_complete` takes a `deliverable`: the work itself, in full (the answer; findings with figures and sources; a plan item by item, a trip day by day with places, times and costs), built from the fixed components. Its schema requires it, and the kernel refuses a completion whose deliverable is missing, empty or blank (a shape check like the plan's; content is not judged); a task finished before this shows its summary in its place. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29); the San Diego trip (`task_fabe3c64e383746302c134f88aabb46a`) did produce a full itinerary, but only in a delivered file, so its page showed a price, a chart and three check lines. Only the model can tell a deliverable from a summary of it, so the kernel checks the shape alone.
- A `file` component shows a delivered file in full, drawn by the app from the same fixed components: Markdown as headings, paragraphs, lists and tables; CSV as a table; other text as text; an image as an image. Every delivered file the deliverable does not show follows it inline (the latest file of each name). A preview holds at most what a composition may (40 components × 4,000 characters) and 200 table rows; the rest is in the file. PDF and office files are not drawn; the page says to download them. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29), "files previewed inline, not only linked".
- The outcome page reads: the deliverable, then the summary, the evidence, the cost, and the trace, folded at the end of the same page (the Full trace tab is gone). Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29).
- The library is derived from the log: every upload (`FileUploaded`) and every delivered file (`Output`), each linked to the task or chat it came from. Files sent with a task are uploads too. Search matches every word against the name, where it came from, its project, and a text file's content (up to the preview length). Uploads are limited to 25 MB, like delivered files; the upload route's body limit fits that much base64. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29).
- An upload's content is private and untrusted (no authority) wherever it is used: a task or message given it starts with both labels. A delivered file carries the taint its run had when it delivered it. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29); M1.
- Projects group chats, tasks, schedules and files. Only the person writes a project's name and instructions (`ProjectSet`, through the API); no tool can (I1). Items move with `Moved`; an item is in the project it was last moved to, else where it began (a task where it started, a thread where its first message was, a delivered file with its task, a file uploaded in chat with its thread). Projects and libraries are their person's alone. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29). This replaces "no projects" in the tasks-at-volume decision.
- A task started in a project carries the project as it stood then (`ProjectContext`: instructions and reference file ids), so its context only grows (C1, K5); new instructions apply to tasks started after. The instructions reach the model as the person's own words; the reference files (every file in the project) are listed by name and read on demand with `project_read` (M2), whose result carries the file's taint. Chat turns in a project's thread get the same, tasks from chat get the thread's project and the message's files, and a schedule's runs get the schedule's project when they start. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29).
- K34 amended (owner, 2026-10-29): a web lookup the model chooses (search or fetch) is external communication, since its address can carry data out. After a task has read private and untrusted data, its first web lookup asks one question ("This task reads your project files and searches the web — allow web lookups for this task?"), and approving it for the task covers all its web reads (B5); in chat the approval covers the conversation. Every other send is still approved one action at a time. It is asked at the first lookup, not on the contract, because only then does the kernel know the task holds both kinds of data. Once allowed, a lookup's URL could carry private data out; the owner accepted this.
- The web-lookups question is an inbox card like every approval, and the chat thread links to it, rather than a button in the thread. Reason: approve buttons exist only on inbox cards (U3, U8); the owner asked for it "in the thread", so this is flagged for the owner.
- When a task's result is an action, its deliverable is the record of what was done (what was sent, changed or created, to whom and where). The invariant tests' example tasks are such actions: their shared `complete` helper sends the summary as one text component, and no assertion changed. Reason: owner instruction and approval of that invariant-test change (2026-10-29), after reviewing the diff (`docs/proposals/invariant-deliverable.patch`).
- A library file opens on its own page (`#/file/{id}`): drawn in full the way the outcome page draws delivered files, with where it came from, its project, and Download. Wherever a file is listed, its name opens it and a Download button saves it. The desktop app fetches bytes from a delivered output or a library file (`/v1/files/{id}`) and nothing else. Reason: owner report (2026-10-29), "library has no ability to open or download files": the app's byte fetch allowed only task outputs, so every library download was refused there (the browser test did not see it), and there was no way to open a file.
- Every feature that crosses into the desktop shell (downloads, uploads, sign-in links, notifications, file access) has at least one test against the installed app (`tests/scratch/tests/desktop_app.rs`, scripts in `desktop/tests/app*.mjs` sharing `appdriver.mjs`), and installers are published only when those tests pass (on Linux; see below). Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29), after 0.4.0 shipped with every library download refused in the app while all browser tests passed. The library-download test fails on the released 0.4.0 (the app shows an empty message and saves nothing) and passes on 0.4.1.
- A desktop shell command that fails reaches the page as an `Error` with the shell's message. Reason: found by the library-download test; the shell rejects with a bare string, so every shell error (downloads, previews, connecting) showed as an empty toast.
- Desktop installers are published only by `.github/workflows/desktop.yml`, for a `desktop-v<version>` tag, and only when the installed-app tests passed on Linux; the release notes are the tag's message. The jobs run on a self-hosted runner on the build box (`magnus-linux`: KVM, the guest image, Postgres). The Windows build is not tested separately. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29) and choice of a self-hosted runner; then "don't run windows tests. It's the same functional app on linux and window or the web browser."
- Every outcome takes a one-tap verdict from the person it is for: right, partly or wrong, with an optional note (at most 4,000 characters, as a deliverable text), stored in the log as `verdict` and only once the task has ended; the latest counts. It sits on the outcome page, on each chat answer and on each chat result. A chat request's verdict is the one on its answer; if the message started tasks and the answer has none, it is theirs once every task has one (right only if all are right, wrong if any is, otherwise partly). The old ✓/✗ choice grades on request trials are replaced, not converted. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29).
- Experiments compares a run of a request set with an earlier one (the latest earlier experiment sharing a request, or the one picked), pairing each request on each model and trial. It flags a change in verdict, the model's choice (answer, ask, task, schedule), policy stops, the tasks' end states, or the instructions, harness or playbook, and shows the cost and time difference beside each pair without flagging it (every run's cost and time differ a little). Once every trial of the rerun has settled, one summary counts improved, unchanged and regressed by verdict, with pairs missing a verdict on either side counted apart as not judged yet. Reason: owner instruction (2026-10-29).
- The desktop release checks are one job on the build box, and they build every workspace program before the installed-app tests. Reason: GitHub's artifact storage for the private repository is full, so jobs could not hand installers to each other; and a fresh checkout showed `cargo test` builds only the test package's programs, not the appliance and adapters the tests launch.
- The owner adds the person's apps (MCP servers) from the app; they live in the log beside the config file's (which stay read-only there). Whether an app needs a sign-in is asked of the app (401 at initialize). Sign-in follows MCP's authorization spec: protected-resource metadata, the authorization server's metadata, dynamic client registration (or a client id the owner gives), authorization code with PKCE S256 and the `resource` parameter, the code returning to a loopback address on the person's computer (RFC 8252), which the desktop app catches. Reason: the record's product scope (the user's own apps through MCP connectors); MCP servers support the authorization code, not the device grant, and the loopback keeps it free of pasting.
- An app's tools are offered only once the owner classifies them; the app's annotations only suggest a class (read-only hint: read; not destructive: reversible write; otherwise irreversible). Reason: annotations are the app's own claims (B10 treats app text as untrusted).
- Signed-in apps' tokens are refreshed a minute before they expire, one refresh at a time (rotating refresh tokens are good once); a failed refresh means signing in again. The kill switch takes and revokes them at the app's revocation endpoint (RFC 7009), like the model providers' sign-ins; removing an app revokes and deletes its sign-in. Reason: B11, K53.
- The injection suite (`tests/scratch/tests/injection.rs`, X16) drives the kernel with a model that obeys every instruction it reads (the adaptive attacker, against whom model-side defenses fail), so it measures the boundary alone: four places an attacker can plant text (mail, web page, CRM record, team chat) × six goals (send private mail out, carry it in a web address, delete records, pay, post in team chat, plant a playbook item) × tasks with and without a private read. It fails on any breach: private data leaving, or an irreversible or financial change, without the exact action approved; or an attacker's playbook item losing its untrusted label. CI runs it (`.github/workflows/injection.yml`) on every change to the boundary's files. Reason: X16; a deterministic attacker makes it a regression test, not a model benchmark.
- Backups (K10) are one encrypted file each (XChaCha20-Poly1305 under a backup key that is not the vault's): every event and the vault's key and contents. Each is restored in memory as soon as it is written (every event replayed, every secret decrypted), and the result is recorded; that is the scheduled restore test. Daily by default, kept 30 days (the record keeps key-store backups no longer than the legal deletion deadline; the GDPR gives a month to act on an erasure, Art. 12(3)). `magnus restore` puts one into an empty log and an empty vault only. By default they sit on the appliance's disk, and Home says so until `[backup] dir` names another disk. Reason: K10; a restore that is only tested by hand is not tested on a schedule.

- Appliance updates (gate item 5): releases arrive in the appliance's updates directory (`[updates] dir`, default `<state_dir>/updates`); on strix-alpha CI writes them there, and the appliance is the update host for itself and for the desktop apps (owner, 2026-10-29: "strix alpha appliance is the update"). A package is installed only when its minisign signature verifies with the release key and binds the announced version (`tauri signer sign --app-version`: one key and one tool for app and appliance), the version is newer than the running one and was not rolled back from, and the new programs' `magnus check` reads this appliance's log and vault. Reason: a manifest alone cannot pair a new version number with an old signed package; a release that cannot read the log is found before any downtime.
- The switch: running tasks are suspended ("update"), get up to a minute to finish their current step, the programs' directory is swapped (the old one kept as `<dir>.prev`), and the process replaces itself (`exec`, same pid and arguments; no supervisor needed). The new release resumes the tasks once serving. A release that fails to serve swaps back and starts the old one with its original program and arguments; a second failure stays down for a person. The owner goes back one release the same way. Releases rolled back from are not installed again by themselves. Reason: every step survives a restart (invariants), so the minute only saves repeating an in-flight model reply.
- Replay skips events of a kind the running release does not know (written by a later release it was rolled back from); any other unparseable event still stops the boot. Reason: one-step rollback must be able to read the log the newer release wrote.
- Appliance releases are checked hourly (`check_minutes`, one local file read) and installed automatically (`automatic`). Reason: owner's request for automatic updates; a release reaches the appliance within the hour.
- Versions both ways: every answer carries `x-magnus-appliance`; an app sends `x-magnus-app`, and one older than `MIN_APP` (in `update.rs`, raised with any API change an older app would get wrong) gets 426 "update required" on every API call except version, health and its own update files. `GET /v1/version` is public. Reason: owner's request; an app too old to sign in must still be able to update.
- The release signing key is on the build box only (`~/.magnus-release/update.key`, not in GitHub); its public half is `release/update.pub`. Losing it means installed apps and appliances accept no further update until reinstalled by hand, so the owner should keep an offline copy.
- Bug (owner, 2026-10-29), a scheduled task failed silently. Cause in the live trace (run_f3110cba, seq 6452–6470): the model sent `create_schedule` with `every_minutes: 5, daily_at: ""`, the empty field was read as a second timing and refused three times, and the chat's "I couldn't" was the only record. Fix: an empty or zero timing field counts as absent. Rule (K24): no run ends badly without a visible record. A failed task posts "Failed: …" with its reason, a chat turn whose requested tasks or schedules never came about posts "Not done: …" with the refusals, a driver error suspends with a notice, and a schedule that cannot start its task records `schedule_failed` with a notice. The Schedules view shows every firing's result (`last`, `last_failed`, `failures`). Reason: owner's rule; each path was found by auditing where a run can end.
- Bug (owner, 2026-10-29), memory was not written. Causes: chat turns were offered only read tools, so memory_write was never available in chat; the Rule of Two counted a memory write as sending data out; and across 159 tasks the model wrote memory twice. Fix: chat may write the person's memory and nothing else ("remember that …" is written in the turn); a write to the appliance's own memory is not a transmission and needs no contract (it leaves nothing outside the appliance and carries the run's taint label); memory search matches word stems ("allergy" finds "allergic"). Reason: owner's request; the label, not a refusal, is what keeps an untrusted fact from being trusted.
- The memory pass (module `memory_pass`, on by default; ablation suite `tests/scratch/tests/memory.rs`): after each chat turn and run ends, one separate model call is shown the exchange and what is already remembered, offered only memory_write, and its writes go through `execute()` as `kernel:memory_pass`, recorded with time, run and the run's labels (`by: "memory_pass"`). It is charged to the run's budget (`memory_pass` event). Reason: owner's request (K23, M1, M3); the model doing the work rarely stops to remember.
- Scratch tests changed with the memory change (owner rule: tests change only for bugs). chat.rs: chat now offers memory_write and its write succeeds — the owner's spec change, not a test bug. modules.rs picked "the agent's last request" as any request with tools but no verdict, which now picks the memory pass's; it now picks requests offering task_complete (test bug: wrong selector). update.rs counted every model request as the task's; it now leaves out the memory pass's (test bug: counted the wrong thing).
- An inbox item that stops a run (approval, question, review, contract change, notice) is written in the same statement as the suspension (`Kernel::suspend_on`, one multi-row insert, applied under one state lock). Reason: found by a scratch test failing under load: a reader could see the review card while the task still showed as running.
- Desktop updates: the app asks its appliance (`/v1/updates/desktop/latest.json`, over the pinned certificate) when a session begins and hourly, downloads and verifies in the background (`requireSignedVersion`), and installs only on "Restart to update"; after the restart it shows the release's notes once ("What's new"). On Windows the NSIS installer updates in place (passive); on Linux the AppImage updates itself and a .deb says the package manager updates it. An appliance that answers 426 shows "Update required", and the updater still works there. Reason: owner's request; the appliance is the update host (owner, 2026-10-29).
- Desktop 0.5.0 is the first app with the updater, so moving from 0.4.5 is one install by hand; the appliance's minimum is 0.5.0 (0.4.5 lacks the updater and the Apps screen), and 0.4.5 says so on every screen. Reason: an app without an updater cannot be updated any other way.
- CI publishes a tagged release into the build box's appliance updates directory (`release/publish.sh`, repository variable `MAGNUS_UPDATES_DIR`) only after its tests pass: desktop tags `desktop-v*` after the installed-app tests; appliance tags `appliance-v*` (new workflow `appliance.yml`) after the invariants and scratch suites. The installed-app update test uses two AppImages signed with a key made for the run, never the release key. Reason: nothing the tests sign may install anywhere.
- Bug (found on strix-alpha, 2026-10-29): Postgres dropped every connection at 11:15 UTC ("terminating connection because of crash of another server process"); the appliance never opened its log again, so every write failed for forty minutes while `/v1/health` said ok. Now a lost connection is opened again on the next use with the single-writer lock taken again (refused if another kernel took it); health opens it too and answers 503 `log: down` while it cannot; and if the log holds an event this process did not apply (an append whose answer was lost), the appliance starts again in place from the log. Reason: K24, no silent failures; the in-memory state must never run ahead of or behind the log. Test: `tests/scratch/tests/log_connection.rs`.
- Harness bug, fixed with the owner's approval (2026-10-29): `free_port()` bound port 0 and let go before the appliance started, so under parallel tests another appliance could take the port and answer the health check (appliance 0.2.2's release run got 401 from the wrong appliance). Now the appliance's first start listens on port 0, the appliance logs the address it bound (not the configured one), and the harness is ready only once this start's own "listening on" line names its port; that port is written into `magnus.toml` so restarts keep it. Owner's conditions: only harness setup changes, no assertion; 20 concurrent runs of the invariant suite pass (900 of 900). The dev Postgres container's `max_connections` was raised from 100 to 1000 for that check, since 20 suites at once exhausted the default.
- The desktop library is built as an rlib only. Reason: the Windows (mingw) link of the template's cdylib failed once the updater was added ("export ordinal too large: 65662"; a DLL exports at most 65535 names); the cdylib and staticlib were for mobile builds, which Magnus does not have.
- Changes reach master only through pull requests the owner merges; agents never push to master directly, and protected paths (`tests/invariants/`, `.github/`, `.claude/`) are never unlocked locally, even for an approved change (owner, 2026-10-29). Reason: on 2026-10-29 an agent emptied the `tests/invariants/` deny rule to apply an approved fix; while a lock is off any session can change what it guards. `.claude/` itself is now in the deny list.
- HTTPS offers only HTTP/1.1 in ALPN (2026-10-29). Reason: since 85c2091 it offered h2 first, but axum is built without `http2`, so any client that agreed to h2 (every browser, curl) had its connection closed; strix-alpha looked unreachable over Tailscale while HTTP/1.1 worked. Serving HTTP/2 would need axum's `http2` feature and its own tests; nothing needs it. The scratch tests' client now offers h2 (`reqwest` `http2`), so this cannot hide again (`a_client_that_prefers_http2_is_answered_over_https`).
- Correction (2026-10-29): the `max_connections` change (100 to 1000) is not dev-only in effect. `magnus-claude-pg` is also the database of the alpha appliance on strix-alpha, and restarting it at 12:49 UTC cut that appliance's connection; 0.2.0 does not reconnect, so it could not record anything until restarted by hand.
- Connectors were not removed (2026-10-29): they were built on 2026-10-28 (b9f035e) as the owner-only "Apps" page, shipped in desktop 0.5.0 and appliance 0.2.1+, and no later change touched them. They were easy to miss under that name. They are now "Connectors" in Settings, with connect, disconnect, status, and re-approval of changed tool definitions on each connector (the decision is the inbox item's, shown again there). The installed-app test reaches them only by clicking Settings, then Connectors, so it fails if either disappears.
- Settings holds everything a person can set (owner, 2026-10-29): Models and Connectors (owner only, as their APIs are), Account (who you are, sign out, devices with "sign out" per device; for the owner, people and roles), Notifications, Updates (this app's and the appliance's versions, What's new, restart to update, install now), Data and privacy (consent to send problem reports, a request to delete your data, and for the owner, backups). Developer views stay in their own group. `#/models` and `#/apps` lead to their Settings sections.
- Roles set in Settings are events (`RolesSet`) and replace the config's; roles the identity provider names win over both, and the owner cannot set those. Someone must keep the owner role. Reason: the owner needs to give roles (E7) without editing the config, and the provider stays the source of truth where it speaks.
- Notification and privacy choices are per person on the appliance (`PreferencesSet`), so they follow the person to every device. Defaults match what the app did before: notified when a task needs you now and when the agent changes, not for what can wait. Feedback consent defaults to off; nothing sends problem reports yet ("Report a problem" is gate 5), so the choice is recorded for it.
- A deletion request is an inbox item for the owner, due in 30 days (GDPR Art. 12(3)), decided "deleted" or "declined" with a note the person sees. Nothing deletes data automatically: erasing from an append-only log needs its own design (e.g. per-person keys), not decided yet.
- Bug (2026-10-29): a password sign-in opened two sessions, one in `accounts.sign_in` and one in `issue`; the first was never handed out but stayed valid until it expired. `sign_in` now only checks the password. Found while listing devices, where it would have shown a phantom device.
- Dev Postgres (`magnus-claude-pg`, 2026-10-29): `dynamic_shared_memory_type = mmap`. Reason: with `max_connections` at 1000 its dynamic shared memory outgrew Docker's 64 MB `/dev/shm`, and every new connection failed ("could not resize shared memory segment … No space left on device"), which stopped four invariant tests at setup. With `mmap` it lives in files under the data directory, with no size cap. The 6,876 test databases left by earlier runs were dropped. The invariant harness never drops the database it creates (each run leaves one per test); that fix is in `tests/invariants/` and waits for the owner. The alpha appliance on the same Postgres lost its connection at each of the three restarts and reconnected on its own (0.2.3).
- Chat message actions (owner, 2026-10-29; `crates/magnus/src/chat_actions.rs`): every entry has an id (`m:`/`a:`/`r:`/`t:`/`c:` plus its task or card); copy and delete on every message, edit and resend on the person's own, retry on Magnus's, branch, share, export, save to library, make a task. Only the thread's person may act on it (404 for anyone else, as for reading it).
- Delete is an event (`ChatHidden`) that hides entries from the thread's views and from what the model reads next; the log keeps the tasks and runs unchanged. Reason: the owner's rule that the log stays append-only; hiding is the only delete an event log allows.
- Edit and retry hide the message and everything after it, then send again with the same files and model. Reason: an answer after an edited question answers a question no longer shown, and two copies after a retry would both reach the model.
- A branch is a new thread holding a copy of the entries up to the chosen one (`ThreadBranched`), in the same project; the original thread is untouched. Reason: the snapshot is what the branch's next turn reads, and later changes to the original must not leak into it.
- Sharing sends the message to another appliance user's inbox (item kind "shared", with its taint), and is refused (403) if the run behind it used a tool of an app the recipient has no rights to. No public links; export is a markdown file. Reason: the owner's "within their rights": what an app returned must not reach someone that app would refuse.
- Security fix found while building this (2026-10-29): a chat turn read earlier answers of its thread without their taint, so a web page read in one turn reached the next as trusted. A turn (and a task made from a message) now starts with the joined taint of the runs whose words it reads (`chat::thread_label`); deleting those entries removes their taint with their words.
- The desktop shell fetches bytes only from files; a chat export (`/v1/chat/{thread}/entries/{entry}/export`) is now one of them, beside task outputs and library files. Bug found by the installed-app test: Export was refused with "not a file".
- Composed answers in chat (owner, 2026-10-29, U8). A chat turn may call `show` with components from the fixed library (`compose.rs`: table, stat, bar and line chart, map, timeline, comparison, code; image and file stay with task outcomes, which deliver files). The kernel checks each against its schema; a chat answer may not show headings, paragraphs or lists ("say that in your answer"), because prose in a component repeats the text.
