Reason: the owner's rule that components appear only where they carry what the text does not; the model is told so, and the kernel refuses what can be checked mechanically.
- The answer's text comes first and the components after it. A reply of answer text plus `show` calls ends the turn at once (no second model call that would write a second answer); the app streams the text as it is written and draws the components only when the answer is complete. Live text now replays the reply so far to a view that opens mid-reply (`kernel::Live`). Reason: a chat view refreshes every 2 seconds, so without the replay it missed what was written before it subscribed.
- Composed content is drawn by the app's own code into a frame with no scripts (`sandbox="allow-same-origin"` without `allow-scripts`, forms or popups) and its own CSP (`default-src 'none'`), inside the app's CSP. Everything the agent wrote is escaped text; nothing in a component is a control, a link or a URL that loads. The installed test forces markup into the frame and checks that a recording server receives nothing. Reason: the owner's "sandbox with no network and no access to app state", tested rather than assumed.
- The map is places by latitude and longitude on a plain projection, with no map tiles. Reason: tiles need the network, which composed content does not have; fetching them through the appliance would send the places to a tile server.
- Read aloud for a chat answer uses a script written for listening by one model call with no tools, on the first request, kept as an event (`SpokenWritten`, its cost counted with the turn). Tasks keep the `spoken` script of `task_complete`. Reason: the owner's rule that read-aloud is not the screen text; writing it on demand costs nothing for answers nobody listens to.
- The scripted test model writes text or tool calls, never both in one reply (protected harness). The scratch provider proxy turns a call to `say` into text and pauses after it (`magnus_scratch::say`, `SAID_PAUSE`), so tests can script an answer with components and see the text stream first. Reason: changing the protected harness needs the owner's approval; the proxy is scratch code.
- Company knowledge base (owner, 2026-10-29; `crates/magnus/src/knowledge.rs`). The log holds every entry (`KnowledgeAdded`, `KnowledgeEndorsed`); Postgres table `knowledge` indexes them for search and is brought up to date from the log on boot and after each change. Every entry carries readers, integrity (`company`, `verified`, `untrusted`) and provenance (source with its citations, time, author `person:`/`agent:<run>`/`kernel:extractor`, run). Reason: the owner's one store with three labels, and the log stays the only source of truth.
- Read rights are enforced by Postgres: each search runs in a transaction as `<kernel role>_reader`, which row-level security lets see only rows whose readers the asker meets (principals `person:<id>`, `group:<id>` set for that transaction). With no principals set it sees nothing, and it cannot write. Readers are clauses that must all be met, each by any one principal; none means everyone. Reason: E2 enforced before the model sees anything, in the database rather than in kernel code; clauses express "both sources' readers", which a flat list cannot.
- Readers come from the sources: each app whose private content the run read limits the entry to that app's access groups, or to the person when the app has none (their files, memory); the public web limits nothing. A person writing an entry chooses themselves (default), groups, or everyone. Reason: "inherited from the source's permissions".
- Agent research (`knowledge_add`) needs citations (source and quote). The kernel checks each quote against what that source returned in the run: found in two independent sources (web host and port, or app) or in an app marked `system_of_record`, the entry is verified, with the evidence kept. Verified never grants authority: a search returning anything but company facts is untrusted. Reason: the owner's "reliability, never authority", from the run's own results rather than the agent's word.
- Untrusted content grants no authority, everywhere (invariant 4's wording; owner, 2026-10-29): it may inform how a request is carried out, but never adds instructions, widens scope or changes policy. Enforcement stays structural, the taint labels and the Rule of Two, never intent detection. The knowledge base follows it like every other source: a search is untrusted unless every entry is a company fact, and private when an entry is not readable by everyone; its labels travel into any task the run starts. This replaces the knowledge base's earlier rule that untrusted entries never drive an action, which labelled every untrusted search private as well so that any action after it asked. Reason: one rule for all untrusted content, stated as invariant 4 states it; a stricter rule for one source would contradict invariant 4's allowed case (untrusted input with an external action and no private data).
- Endorsement (company fact) is an inbox card for people with `knowledge:endorse` or the owner who may also read the entry; someone with the role who may not read it never sees the card. The decision is logged with who, the card, and their note; the source stays attached. Anyone who may read an entry may ask; the agent may ask with `propose`. Reason: E7, and a card must not leak an entry to its decider.
- Automatic company facts only for a typed value (number, boolean, one of named options) that a model call with no tools read from the entry and its checked quotes, accepted only if its reply is exactly such a value; it is filed as its own entry pointing to the research. Reason: the owner's exception for low-capacity values, which cannot carry instructions.
- Search by meaning uses an OpenAI-compatible embedding server named in `[knowledge.embeddings]` (url, model, optional vault credential), so a local server (Ollama, llama.cpp, TEI) keeps company text on site; the vector size comes from the server. Without one, or when it fails, search is Postgres full-text. No approximate index yet: an exact scan is fast at an appliance's size. Reason: no embedding model is fixed in code, and nothing leaves the site unless the owner points it elsewhere.
- A search by meaning also returns, first, the entries not embedded with the server's model that match the query's words (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: an entry saved while the embedding server was down is indexed without an embedding, and only the next start embeds it; until then the server, once back, left it out of every search. Test: `an_entry_saved_while_the_embedder_was_down_is_still_found` (knowledge.rs).
- Dev Postgres (`magnus-claude-pg`) now runs `dev/postgres.Dockerfile`: the same `postgres:16` image by digest plus pgvector 0.8.7 from PGDG, on the same data volume (the old container is kept as `magnus-claude-pg-old`). Reason: `pgvector/pgvector:pg16` is 16.15 on Debian 12; the data was written on Debian 13, and another glibc can change collations under existing indexes.
- `magnus migrate` (with the admin URL) now also creates the vector extension, the knowledge table, its reader role and policies. Appliance updates do not migrate, so a release with the knowledge base needs `migrate` once when installed; until then knowledge calls fail and say so. Reason: the kernel's role may not create extensions or roles, by design.
- Bug (master's desktop run after #10, 2026-10-29): Settings' "What's new" made the app mark `whats-new.json` as shown instead of removing it, so the self-update test's check that the note is shown once (the file is gone after the first show) failed. Fix: once shown, the note moves to `whats-new-shown.json`, which only Settings reads. Reason: the product meets the test's contract again; the test was not changed.
- Read-aloud uses the appliance's own voice: Kokoro (kokoro-onnx, Apache-2.0), installed by `config/install-voice.sh` (newest kokoro-onnx and newest model files, read at install time) and named in `[read_aloud]`; `POST /v1/speak` turns text into MP3 on the appliance. The app asks for one sentence at a time and plays it while the next is made (about 0.26 s of work per second of speech, plus 0.8 s to start, on strix-alpha's CPU), from a `blob:` URL (`media-src blob:` in all three CSPs). With no voice installed (503), the app falls back to its system's voice. Reason: the owner asked for Magnus to ship its own engine (2026-10-29), so Linux, Windows and the browser hear the same voice and the text never leaves the appliance; the owner chose Kokoro over Piper for the more natural voice.
- Chat instructions now say when to `show` (figures, places, dates, options, code; nothing for a plain answer), with the text giving the point and the detail in the view instead of listed in both; "Reply in plain text" and the tool's "never repeat your answer in it" were dropped, as they read as text only. Reason: owner (2026-10-29) saw no composed answers. On strix-alpha, none of 28 answers had a view; the scripted tests always call `show`, so they never measured whether a real model chooses to. Measured with 5 questions that call for a view and 3 that do not, 3 times each: Codex (gpt-6.1-sol) went from 0 of 5 (one sample) to 9 of 12 (code questions it starts as tasks, not counted), Grok (grok-4.7) from 3 of 5 to 5 of 15; neither showed anything for a plain question. Grok still mostly chooses text; that is the model's decision (invariant 5).
- Component checks: an empty optional `title`, `unit`, `caption` or `language` counts as not given, `best` below 0 as no best, and a table header may be empty (over a column of row labels). Reason: Codex fills every field of the schema, sending the ones it does not use as "" or -1, and each was refused, one per retry: two or three extra model calls per answer, and the same for task deliverables.
- Bug: the developer view's run inspector compared every run with the task instructions, so it reported each chat turn as running on instructions other than the build's. It now compares a chat turn with the chat instructions, as the kernel chooses them. Test: `the_inspector_matches_chat_and_task_runs_to_their_own_instructions`.
- Deletion step 1, the content store (owner approved the plan and invariant 3's new wording, 2026-10-29): each event's content (the fields listed in `events::CONTENT`: what people and sources wrote) is written to `public.content`, one row per event, in the same statement as the event; the log keeps the rest of the body and `content: {sha256, bytes}`. Reads join the two back, so the kernel, inspector and app see events as written. Reason: content must be deletable, and the log must not be rewritten.
- The content hash is salted: SHA-256 over a random 32-byte salt kept in the content row, then the content. Reason (from a review note): an unsalted hash of short content (a memory fact, a file name) can be matched by guessing, and the log keeps it forever; with the salt deleted alongside the content, the hash left behind matches nothing.
- Kept in the log as the record of a decision, not content: an endorsement's note, an approval's reason, a policy decision's reason, a context reset's note, kernel notices, errors and suspensions. Reason: they are written by the kernel or record why something was allowed; the person-written answer to a question (`item_resolved.text`) and a verdict's note are content.
- Until `magnus migrate` has created the content store, the kernel writes events whole, as before, and warns at start. Reason: appliance updates do not migrate (above), and a release that cannot write its log stops working; step 4's migration moves such content out.
- Backups carry each event's content and salt beside its record; restore puts them back in the store. Old backups restore as before.
- Deletion step 2, the trash: a task, a conversation, one chat message or answer, a file, a project, a memory or a knowledge entry goes to the trash (`Trashed`), out of every view, and back on restore (`Restored`). It is removed (`Removed`, naming the events whose content was deleted) when the person deletes it now, empties the trash, or `[trash] keep_days` (default 30, as backups) have passed; the trash is checked hourly and at start. Reason: the owner's decisions (30-day trash, delete only the item).
- What an item holds: a task or conversation, the content of its runs' events except what they made that is an item of its own (delivered files, memories, playbook items); a message, its `task_created`; an answer, the rest of its turn; a file, project, memory or knowledge entry, its own event. Copies stay where they were made: a later turn's conversation, a branch's entries. Reason: the owner chose "delete only the item".
- A task that has not ended (running or waiting on the person) cannot be trashed: 409, cancel it first. Reason: invariant 3, a crashed run must be able to resume.
- Removal writes its record first, then deletes the content; at start, content of every removed event still in the store is deleted. Reason: a removal cut short finishes, never leaves content behind unrecorded.
- Every content field has a serde default, checked by a unit test. Reason: an event whose content was removed must still read, so state rebuilds.
- Chat "Delete" on a message or answer moves it to the trash; on a task's card or result it still hides the entry (the task stays in Tasks). `DELETE /v1/memory/{id}` moves the memory to the trash. A person reaches only their own trash; a knowledge entry may be trashed by its author (or the person whose task filed it) and by the owner.
- The kernel role gains DELETE on `content` (only); `magnus migrate` must run once after this release is installed.
- App: Delete on a task (once ended), a conversation, a file and a project; a Trash screen with Restore, Delete now and Empty trash now. Memories and knowledge have no screens yet, so they are trashed through the API only.
- Chat message actions are icons, not text links (owner, 2026-10-30): Lucide outline icons (lucide-static 0.563.0, ISC, licence kept beside them in app.js), 16px in a 28px button. Reason: the row of eleven text links was too long; 28px clears WCAG 2.2's 24px minimum target, and each keeps its name as a tooltip and hidden text for screen readers and the app tests.
- One workspace (owner, 2026-10-30; design: the "Magnus Workspace" canvas). The conversation is home: opening Magnus shows the latest conversation (a new one when there is none). What a message starts shows in its thread: tasks with live status, schedule cards, the decisions they wait on, results with inline file previews. Anything named there (a task, file, schedule, decision, project) opens in a panel beside the conversation, never instead of it. Tasks, Schedules, Library and Needs you are views in that panel over the same objects; the app reads tasks, schedules, conversations, projects and the inbox together and redraws every view from them, so acting anywhere updates everywhere. The Home, Inbox, New task, Tasks, Schedules, Library, File, Projects and Project pages are gone; their addresses open the matching panel beside the latest conversation (a task beside its own conversation). Settings, Trash and the developer views keep whole pages. The kill switch moved from Home to Settings. Supersedes: "approve buttons exist only on the inbox card, never in the task view" and "approvals and questions stay in the inbox, and the thread links to them": a decision's card is now the same card wherever it shows (its thread, the task's panel, Needs you), actionable in each. Notifications still carry no buttons. Reason: owner instruction.
- An inbox item names its task's conversation (`thread`) for the person whose task it is, so Needs you can say where it came from. `GET /v1/search?q=` matches every word against the person's conversations (title and everything said), tasks (title, request, summary) and files (as the library matches them). Reason: the workspace's sidebar search and Needs you.
- U7 amended (owner, 2026-10-30): themes are one component set with swappable tokens: mode (light, dark, match computer) × material (solid, glass) + accent. Glass is for chrome only (sidebar, toolbars, panels, floating cards), using native window materials where the system has them and a drawn backdrop elsewhere; reading surfaces (messages, results, approval text) stay solid. Text on glass must reach 4.5:1 against the worst-case backdrop, checked by an automated test. Motion is full, reduced (fades) or off, and the system's reduce-motion and reduce-transparency settings are honoured. Supersedes "solid surfaces only".
- Ease of use is a first-class requirement (owner, 2026-10-30): installed-app tests count the steps and navigations for starting work, approving, reading a result, finding a file and creating a schedule, against recorded budgets; a count above its budget fails the test, and so blocks the release.
- Appearance is kept per device (localStorage `magnus.appearance`), not on the appliance, and is applied before anything is drawn (theme.js loads before app.js). Reason: the right look depends on the screen and the room it is in, and the sign-in screen must already have it. Default: Match computer, solid, Blue, full motion.
- Glass tints are set by the contrast test, not by eye: light sidebar/panel at 84%/86% opacity, dark at 88%/90%, and muted text a step stronger on glass (light #45454A, dark #C2C2C8); light accent text darkened (Blue #2541A8, Teal #145247, Clay #7D3618), dark lightened (Blue #AEBFFF, Clay #F7B394), dark muted #A8A8AF. Reason: the canvas's 58–70% tints fall to 3.5:1 over a white or black desktop, and the requirement is 4.5:1 against the worst-case backdrop (`desktop/tests/theme-check.mjs`, run by `tests/scratch/tests/appearance.rs`).
- On Windows the main window is transparent (`tauri.windows.conf.json`) and glass asks the system for Mica, then Acrylic (window-vibrancy 0.8, the version tauri already locks); Windows' "Transparency effects" off counts as reduced transparency, read from the registry because WebView2 does not report it. Elsewhere the page draws the canvas's wallpaper behind the chrome. Reason: native materials where the system has them (U7, amended).
- Reduced motion from the system lowers Full to Reduced (quick fades), and never raises Off. Reason: fades carry no movement, and a person who chose Off keeps it.
- In the conversation, a request's tasks are rows in one "From this request" card; the decisions they wait on follow it as cards; a schedule to confirm offers Start schedule, Change (says what to change in the composer) and Not now; a confirmed schedule is a row. The task panel leads with the deliverable under Result, with Plan (request, contract, plan) and Steps (trace) beside it. Reason: the Magnus Workspace canvas.
- Ease-of-use budgets live in `desktop/tests/ease-budgets.json`: start work 2 steps, approve 1, read a result 1, find a file 3, create a schedule 3, each with 0 navigations; a step is a click, a typed entry or a key press. Reason: what each flow takes in the one-workspace design, measured in the installed app; lowering one is free, raising one is the owner's decision.
- The installed-app harness starts tauri-driver in its own process group and ends the group with the test, and a browser-test failure report says when no task was started instead of failing on it. Reason: each run left a driver, the app and the browser the app opened for sign-in behind; after a day they had used every inotify instance and builds failed with "Too many open files".
- Task, conversation and schedule rows lay out as flex, not a four-column grid: the title keeps at least 8rem, the reason a task waits gives way first. The conversation's scroller keeps 88px clear under its sticky header (`scroll-padding-top`), as it keeps the composer's height clear below. Reason: in the 480px panel the grid left titles three letters wide, and an action scrolled into view sat under the header.
- Review builds: every pull request's CI run, once the installed-app tests pass, publishes its signed installers to the build box's appliance as a pre-release of the next version, `<next>-test.<run>.pr.<n>` (for example `0.7.1-test.141.pr.44`; `-review.` until 2026-11-01, below), with the pull request's title as the notes. Reason: the owner reviews in the Windows app, which updates only from the appliance (owner, 2026-10-30: "if there is a rebuild it needs to update the windows app or it can't be reviewed"); a pre-release is newer than every release before it and older than the release that follows, so the apps move on to that release. The newest build to pass is what the apps get.
- Review builds are numbered by CI run, not by pull request, and `release/publish.sh` never replaces `latest.json` with an older version (2026-10-31). Reason: the first scheme, `-pr.<n>.<run>`, ranked a higher pull request number above every later build of a lower one, so one build from a newer pull request would have kept the apps from ever taking the pull request under review again; and two runs finishing out of order could move the apps backwards. CI builds a pull request merged with master (GitHub's merge ref), so each review build carries everything on master as of its run; when master moves, a pull request under review merges master to get a build that has it, and one that conflicts with master gets no build until it does.
- The trash test waits for the sidebar to drop the deleted conversation before it opens Trash. Reason: a test bug; the step clicked the sidebar's Trash link while the sidebar was redrawn after the delete, and the link it had found was gone (CI run 41075079098). The first delete step already waited for this.
- Glass uses the design canvas's tints again (light sidebar/panel 62%/70%, dark 58%/66%; dark glass search field 8% white), replacing the 84–90% tints above. Text on glass is checked against what can be behind it: the system's material over a black, grey or white desktop (its tint, `system` in `desktop/ui/theme.js`, the one Magnus gives Acrylic; Windows 11's Mica stays within that range), or the backdrop the page draws where there is no material, in both `desktop/tests/theme-check.mjs` and the installed-app looks test. Reason: the owner found the themes changed nothing (2026-10-30). Measured over a bare black or white desktop, the tints had to be nearly opaque, and glass looked like solid; but in this app a bare desktop is never directly behind the page (Linux draws its backdrop, Windows puts its material between). Every look still reaches 4.5:1.
- Settings > Appearance follows the canvas's Appearance board: sentence-case headings, full-width segmented controls, material tiles previewing each material in the current mode (from the theme's tokens), round swatches with a ring. Reason: owner, 2026-10-30, pointing at the board.
- Schedules cannot run away (owner, 2026-10-30, after a test schedule's run proposed a second schedule and both fired every ten minutes for hours while 75 runs waited). A schedule starts no run while its last one is running or waiting on someone (the firing is recorded as skipped); a scheduled run cannot propose another schedule; a schedule may end at a time (`until`) or after `max_runs`, and its own run may end it (`end_schedule`); after `[schedules] pause_after_failures` (default 3) failed runs in a row it pauses, tells its person, and waits for Resume; Schedules shows each one's end, budget per run, pause and skips, with Resume and Stop schedule. Reason: none of these limits existed, and the app had no way to stop a schedule.
- A schedule a run proposes takes that run's budget and taint when accepted, as a chat card's does. Reason: the accepted proposal had no budget of its own and ran at the appliance's ($25), more than the $2 run that proposed it.
- `[schedules] min_every_minutes` (default 5, as before) sets the shortest interval. Reason: the tests set 1 so a real appliance fires several times within a test; 5 stays the product's floor.
- A schedule whose run waits on a person (approval, question, review, contract change, or a stop for budget or an error) pauses at once and tells its person; it goes on by itself once that run no longer waits, and Resume cannot skip past it (owner, 2026-10-30: "If a schedule is left waiting on someone it would need HIL and should pause until it has it otherwise it's not acting with authority and or acting without information it needs"). A run waiting for a time it set, an update or the kill switch only holds the next firing (skipped), without a pause. Reason: a schedule that goes on without the person's answer acts without authority or without what it needs.

- Each person's tasks, schedules and task inbox items (questions, reviews, notices) are their own; the owner no longer sees or acts on anyone else's (owner, 2026-10-30: "Individual users should have their own accounts with their own tasks, conversations, schedules, memory, etc."). Approvals, tool changes, deletion requests and policy items stay role-based. Reason: the owner's Tasks and inbox filled with test accounts' tasks they could see but not trash. Org-wide reach moves to a separate admin console (owner, 2026-10-30), still to be built.
- The app signs in through the identity provider with the authorization code and PKCE (S256) in the person's own browser, which comes back to a loopback address on their computer (RFC 8252); the appliance makes and keeps the verifier, state and nonce, the app passes back the code over its pinned connection, and the appliance exchanges it, checking issuer (RFC 9207 `iss` too), audience, expiry and nonce. Replaces the app's device-code flow; the appliance's own page in a browser keeps the device grant. Reason: owner's spec (2026-10-30): Keycloak through the identity interface, passkeys and two-factor handled by Keycloak; the Linux app's webview has no WebAuthn (measured: WebKitGTK 2.52 has no `navigator.credentials`), the browser does.
- The device grant sends PKCE too. Reason: found against the real Keycloak: once a client requires S256, Keycloak refuses a device authorization without `code_challenge_method`.
- The identity interface (`identity.rs`): begin a sign-in in the person's browser, finish it with what came back; the OIDC provider implements it, and every decision still reads `Kernel::person`. SAML and SCIM come after the alpha gate. Reason: owner's spec.
- A browser sign-in waits `[sign_in.oidc] sign_in_minutes` (10): long enough to find a password or passkey and pass two-factor; the app waits as long as the appliance says.
- Cancelling a browser sign-in in the app ends its wait (`provider_sign_in_cancel`), and a sign-in the appliance finished as it was cancelled is signed out there and never stored; a new sign-in ends the one before (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: Cancel only hid the wait, so a sign-in the person then finished in the browser still made a session on the appliance and replaced the one in the keyring, the one they had chosen instead. Each sign-in is numbered from its first request (`BrowserSignIn`): a Cancel before the appliance answered still stops it, and it is kept only if it is the latest and not cancelled, checked and stored under one lock, so an older one never takes a newer one's place (review of #160). Tests: `a_cancelled_browser_sign_in_stops_waiting`, `only_the_latest_uncancelled_sign_in_finishes` (desktop lib.rs).
- Sign-in tests run against a real Keycloak (26.8.1 by digest, `dev/keycloak.sh`, `magnus-claude-kc` on 127.0.0.1:54380), a fresh realm per test set up as docs/proposals/accounts.md asks of the owner's, and people sign in on Keycloak's own pages in a real Chromium (`tests/scratch/sign-in-browser.mjs`); the installed-app tests register it as the computer's default browser. The fake identity provider is gone. Reason: owner, 2026-10-30: "There should never be a fake."
- People sign in by typing their username and password in the app; the appliance checks them with Keycloak's password grant (Direct access grants) and keeps neither. Replaces the browser as the everyday way in. Reason: owner, 2026-10-30: "This needs to be username and password just like logging into muse or grok bot"; option 1 approved the same day.
- Two-factor: the password grant answers a wrong password and a missing code alike, so the appliance asks Keycloak's admin API, as a status client that may only view users (`[sign_in.oidc] status_client`, secret in the vault named by `status_credential`), whether the person uses a code, and the app asks for it only then; it asks whether or not the password was right. Reason: measured against Keycloak 26.8.1: `invalid_grant` "Invalid user credentials" for both; the code goes as `otp`. `view-users` is the least the lookup needs and is what the status check needs anyway.
- Keycloak's own page in the browser, with the username filled in (`login_hint`), the first time someone signs in to the appliance, when Keycloak says the account is not fully set up, and when it knows no such username; also the "Use a passkey instead" link. A person with a password set on the appliance never needs it. Reason: owner, 2026-10-30: "open browser the first time or if user is not found", after a note that Keycloak runs required actions (temporary passwords, two-factor setup) only on its own page.
- Provider password failures count toward the same lockout as local passwords (`max_failures` in `lockout_minutes`), for people of this appliance only; asking for a code is not a failure. Reason: Keycloak's brute-force detection is off by default.
- With an identity provider, a password set on the appliance signs in only the owner (read from the config's roles, never from the provider), as their backup when the provider is down or refuses; `magnus password` refuses anyone else, and a password set before the provider was configured stops counting. The owner's backup is tried before the provider's "use the browser" or "enter your code" answers. Reason: owner, 2026-10-30: "The back up password is only for me. It should not be allowed for anyone else", after a note that someone turned off in Keycloak could still sign in with an appliance password.
- The owner's backup password works only on the appliance's own machine: the connection's peer is loopback and carries no proxy header (`Forwarded`, `X-Forwarded-For`, `X-Real-IP`, `CF-Connecting-IP`) (owner, 2026-10-30: "Make it available only from the appliances own machine"). Reason: from the network, a password kept on the appliance would bypass Keycloak's two-factor. Tested by headers and by the machine's own network address over HTTPS.
- When Keycloak cannot check a password typed in the app (Direct access grants off for the client, or the status client cannot say whether the person uses a code), the appliance sends the person to Keycloak's page in the browser and logs what the owner should change; it never calls the password wrong or counts it toward lockout. Reason: a right password with a code was answered "wrong" and counted toward lockout; a client without direct grants answered with an error, so the app could not sign anyone in.
- strix-alpha's Keycloak (identity.pyrallon.ai, realm magnus) changed on 2026-10-30 through a temporary admin made with Keycloak's recovery command (`kc.sh bootstrap-admin`) and deleted after (owner's choice): client `magnus-claude` has Direct access grants, Standard flow with redirect `http://127.0.0.1/*`, and PKCE S256 required (the device grant now needs PKCE too, which the appliance sends); new confidential client `magnus-claude-status`, service account with `realm-management` `view-users` only, its secret in the appliance vault as `keycloak_status`. Brute-force detection was already on. The client's previous settings are kept for a revert.
- `[sign_in.oidc] status_admin_url` and `status_admin_ca`: where the status client reaches Keycloak's admin API, and the CA to trust there. Reason: strix-alpha's Keycloak serves its admin API only on its own address (`hostname-admin`, https://127.0.0.1:19446, certificate from the Magnus production origin CA); at identity.pyrallon.ai it answers 404, so the status client could not tell who uses a code.


## Review of master fixes (2026-10-29, `master-fixes`)

- The memory pass shows a run only the remembered facts its own taint covers (an untrusted or private fact only to a run that already holds that taint). Reason: it reads them outside `execute()` and what it writes carries the run's label, so a clean run shown an untrusted fact filed it again as trusted (M1, invariant 4). Test: `runs_review.rs`.
- Each memory pass names its calls after its own `MemoryPass` event (`memory:<seq>:<n>`). Reason: a second pass on the same run (after a rejected review) reused the first pass's step ids, and the boundary deduplicated or refused its writes.
- The verifier judges only criteria without a check; a criterion whose check was refused or could not run goes to the person unchanged. Reason: K37 and the decision above; the verifier's "holds" completed such tasks.
- A reset's note (and a notice) at a reply that an earlier reset cut is shown where the context resumes. Reason: two resets in a row dropped the second note, so the stronger model never learnt its cut calls had run.
- A chat turn's failed `create_task`/`create_schedule` is reported (K24) unless a later attempt of the same thing (same title, or any title when it had none) succeeded. Reason: one success hid every other failure.
- Writing the person's memory does not start the work. Reason: it changes nothing in the world and needs no contract; it froze the first contract.
- The unknown-outcome card and its suspension are one write (`suspend_on`), like every other card.
- Each append checks that its first seq follows the last one this process applied; a gap that holds events (an append whose caller was dropped after the insert was sent, so it committed unapplied) restarts the kernel in place from the log. A gap without events is the sequence skipping a failed insert. Reason: the reconnect check compared only the highest seq, which the next append then matched, so state stayed behind the log (a kill switch engaged in the log but not in memory). Test: `log_review.rs`.
- `magnus restore` takes the log's single-writer lock and refuses while a kernel holds it, writes the vault before it commits the log, and takes the vault back out if the commit fails. Reason: a restore under a freshly started kernel left that kernel's empty state and old vault key writing over the restored ones; a vault that failed to write left a log no second restore would accept.
- One rule for events this release cannot parse, in the log's load and in a backup's restore test: skipped only when the event's kind is unknown (a later release's, after a rollback); an unknown value inside a known kind stops, as any other unparseable event. Reason: serde words both "unknown variant", so a later release's block type silently dropped model replies on rollback, and the restore test refused every backup after a rollback while pruning kept deleting the old ones.
- Scheduled backups count `every_hours` from the newest backup, with a first one ten minutes after the first start, and retry a failed one after an hour; old backups are dropped at start and on every round, whether or not a backup was made; `keep_days` must be at least 1. Reason: the interval restarted with every process start, so an appliance updated daily never backed up on schedule; backups (which hold every secret) outlived `keep_days` while backups failed; `keep_days = 0` deleted each backup as it was made.
- An app that signs in (`client: "app"`, password or device code) without naming its version in `x-magnus-app` is answered 426 "update required". Reason: desktop 0.4.5 sends no version, so the middleware's check never reached it; the browser page and API clients are not apps and sign in as before. Sessions 0.4.5 already holds keep working until they expire. Test: `update_review.rs`.
- The owner's "go back one release" first runs the kept release's `check`, as an update does, and is recorded as the owner's (`owner: true`): if that release still cannot start, the appliance goes back to the one that was running. Only an automatic rollback that fails stays down. Reason: the owner's switch was recorded like the second failure, so a kept release that could not serve left the appliance down with the working release in `.prev`. Test: `update.rs`, last step.
- A failed `exec` after the swap puts the running release's programs back, records `UpdateFailed` and resumes the suspended tasks. Reason: the tasks stayed suspended for "update" and the next check deleted the running release from `.prev`.
- Updates and rollbacks run only from an install directory holding a `VERSION` (one an installer made). Reason: the swap renames the whole directory the program runs from, such as /usr/local/bin.
- A panic while serving starts is a failed start (it rolls back like an error). A killed process is still not rolled back until the next start: there is no supervisor.
- (Superseded by "Release channels", owner 2026-10-31: a pull request's build is tested in CI before it is published, and goes to development, the owner's apps, signed with the release key by `release/publish.sh`.) A pull request's desktop build signed its installers with a key made for the run, never the release key, because PR builds were untested. Still open: PR code runs on the build box where the release key file is readable, and the signed AppImage, setup.exe and `--release` appliance programs are not themselves run before they are signed.
- The update test's broken release listens on a port the test holds, not port 1, which root may bind.
- An app's sign-in is bound to its authorization server: the protected-resource metadata must be for the app's address, the server's metadata must name the issuer it was fetched for, and the sign-in's answer must name that issuer when it names one (`iss`, RFC 9207; required when the server says it sends it). The desktop app passes `iss` on. Reason: a malicious app could have the code and PKCE verifier for another server's sign-in posted to its own token endpoint (mix-up). Test: `connectors_review.rs`.
- An app added in the app must name its label; an app that answers without asking for a sign-in has no credential unless the owner gives a key (`token`), and is listed and called without one. Reason: an empty label read as "principal" (results tainting nothing); every added app was given a credential name, so one that needs none could never be used.
- A refresh answer without `expires_in` keeps the token's last lifetime (an hour if none was ever said). Reason: the token was then never refreshed again and the app signed out at expiry.
- The kill switch seals the vault and takes out and revokes every OAuth sign-in first, then records the switch, the suspensions and the seal; engage and release run one at a time. Reason: it recorded first, so with the log down nothing was sealed or revoked, and a crash between the record and the seal left sign-ins usable after release.
- The kill switch's seal and the sign-ins it takes out are kept on disk in the vault, apart from the log: a `sealed` mark until it is released, and each sign-in taken waits in `revoking.json` until its provider has answered (review, 2026-11-04). At start, before boot returns and anything is served or scheduled, a vault sealed with no switch recorded is engaged again (recorded, runs suspended); every sign-in still waiting is revoked in the background, so no provider holds up the start (review of #151: engaging in a detached task left a window in which the schedule and action gates, which read only the recorded switch, let work run). Reason: the order above (seal and take, then record) left a crash in between with the sign-ins deleted unrevoked and, after the restart, the switch off and the vault open. The order stays: the switch still works when the log does not. Tests: `a_kill_switch_cut_short_by_a_crash_is_finished_at_the_next_start` (connectors.rs), `a_taken_sign_in_waits_until_revoked_and_the_seal_outlives_a_restart` (vault.rs).
- A window or standing approval covers an action only while the person who gave it is still the owner or holds `approver:<risk>`. Reason: a role taken away (by the owner or the identity provider) left its lasting approvals in force.
- An approval covers its tool only at the risk class the action had when approved, recorded on the `Approval` event itself (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: approvals matched on person, tool and scope alone, so after the owner reclassified a tool as riskier (say, irreversible to financial), an earlier standing, window or task approval still ran it without asking. Approvals already in the log take their class from their item; one whose task was deleted since has none and asks again. The class is on the event, not read from the item alone, because the item's details are deleted with the task, which would revoke a standing approval at the next start (review of #148). Tests: `an_approval_does_not_cover_its_tool_once_it_is_classed_riskier` and `a_standing_approval_outlives_deleting_the_task_that_asked_for_it` (connectors.rs).
- Not changed, for the owner to decide: each app has one sign-in for the whole appliance (the owner's account), used by every person's tasks unless the app is limited to groups; and a password sign-in keeps the rights the identity provider gave at its last sign-in (a person the provider demoted keeps them until they sign in through it).
- The owner gives verdicts on an experiment's trials (tasks whose run carries an experiment tag), which run as the eval people; everyone else's verdicts stay their own. Reason: trials belong to eval people who cannot sign in, so no one could judge them and every comparison stayed "not judged yet". Test: `library_review.rs`.
- The owner's task views show another person's tasks without their chat words, projects or library files: a task from chat is grouped as "A conversation of <person>", and its project and files are left out. Reason: threads, projects and libraries are their person's alone; the group heading was the person's chat message word for word.
- Downloads (library files and task outputs) are always well-formed: the name in plain ASCII and in full as RFC 6266 `filename*`, a type that is not a valid header value sent as `application/octet-stream`, and `x-content-type-options: nosniff`. Reason: a name with a control character, a quote or any non-ASCII letter ("Résumé.pdf") made the answer invalid and the handler panicked; the uploader's type was served sniffable.
- The app wires its page-wide click handlers once, and forgets unsent drafts at sign-out. Reason: every sign-in added another copy (one click downloaded, judged or moved twice), and the next person on the same window saw the last one's draft.
- A typed value's key and an enum's options are short names (a key of at most 40, an option of at most 24 letters, digits, spaces, `_ - . %`; two to eight options). Reason: they become the company fact's title and text with no person deciding, and free text there let an injected page write a "company fact" that later runs read as trusted (invariant 4). The value itself stays as decided (number, boolean, one option). Test: `knowledge_review.rs`.
- Privacy a run starts with (from its thread, a schedule's source, the playbook) counts as an unnamed private source, as a private result without sources does: entries the run files are limited to the person, and the audience rule sees it. Reason: once the run read an app limited to a group, that group alone could read an entry built on what the person's chat had read.
- Only what the world returned corroborates research: web pages by host (two ports of one host are one source), web search and apps. A quote found in the sandbox, memory, the knowledge base or the library verifies nothing. Reason: the agent could echo its own claim there and have it shown to endorsers as "checked against independent sources". The knowledge test's second web source moved to `localhost`, another host.
- After `KnowledgeAdded` is written, indexing, value reading and the endorsement request cannot turn the result into "not filed": their failure is said with the filed entry (and as a warning on the HTTP add). Reason: the model retried and filed duplicates, with duplicate company facts and cards. The index upsert never replaces a newer version.
- Sharing a chat entry checks every run whose words reach it (the turns before it and the tasks they started, as its taint does), and refuses when one of them used an app the recipient may not use, or read knowledge entries that are not for everyone. Reason: only the entry's own run was checked, so a summary turn after a CRM turn carried the CRM's content to someone the CRM refuses. Test: `chat_actions_review.rs`.
- Sharing also checks the apps those runs' private content came from, however it reached them (a recalled memory, a thread it was carried from): each must be one the recipient may use (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: only the tools each run called were checked, so a fact a person remembered from an app limited to their group, recalled in a later conversation that called only the memory, was shared with someone outside it. Test: `sharing_a_recalled_fact_keeps_to_the_rights_of_the_app_it_came_from` (chat_actions.rs).
- A shared message is its recipient's alone: the owner neither sees nor answers it. Reason: shared items fell to the default rule (the person or the owner).
- Edit and retry hide the message, its turns and what those turns started, and nothing else; they cancel the superseded turns and the tasks those turns started that have not ended; and what a hidden answer started stays hidden when it shows up later (a task's result). Reason: the superseded task ran on, its result reappeared with its taint, and the new turn could start it again (a duplicate effect); an earlier turn's later result was hidden with the retry.
- "Make a task" on a message carries that message's files. Reading aloud an answer reuses its run's script (a branch's copy included), one write at a time. Reason: the task could not read the attached file; every read-aloud of a branch's copy paid for a new model call.
- The HTTPS test checks that the protocol agreed is HTTP/1.1, not only that an answer came. Reason: the scratch client's `http2` feature unifies into the workspace build of the server, so h2 offered again would still be answered and the test would pass.
- Not changed: deleting a "started tasks" card hides later tasks from the same origin (minor).
- The memory pass of a run waiting for review reads the report on its latest review card. Reason: the run has no summary until it completes, so the pass was shown an empty answer.
- Removing a task, an answer or a conversation also removes what belongs to it but carries no run: its verdict's note and the person's answers to its cards (`item_resolved` text). Reason: both are person-written content (above), and they stayed in the store after their task was gone. Test: `trash_review.rs`.
- A knowledge entry's `source` (its citations, with the quotes from each page or app) is content, removed with the entry. Reason: the quotes are what sources wrote; only the endorsement's note is kept as a decision's record.
- Removing a memory removes where it was written too: the `memory_write` call, its result, and the reply that made the call (the run's model, or the memory pass), found by the fact's text in the memory's run. A memory can be trashed only once the run that wrote it has ended (invariant 3). Reason: the fact stayed in the run's history, shown in the developer view, after the person deleted it.
- A removal is taken out of the backups kept as well: each backup records the last removal applied to it (`scrubbed_through`); after a removal (and at start, for one cut short) every backup made before it is rewritten without the removed events' content and salt, and encrypted again. A new backup leaves out content whose removal it already holds. Reason: with 30 days of backups and 30 days of trash, removed content lived on in backups for up to 60 days, and restoring a backup from before the trash brought it back. Restoring such a backup now brings the item back without its content.
- Trash, restore and removal run one at a time. Reason: a restore between a removal's lookup and its deletion put back an item whose content was then deleted. The race test is a guard; the race could not be forced to show on the old code.
- The hourly purge removes every item it can and reports the rest, instead of stopping at the first failure.
- Read-aloud takes at most 2000 characters a request (413), makes at most two pieces at a time on the appliance (429, the app waits and asks again), and stops a piece after `[read_aloud] timeout_s` (default 60, 504). The app sends a very long sentence in pieces of about 1500 characters. Its error answers no longer carry the program's stderr. Reason: any signed-in person could start any number of model processes with any amount of text, each held open without a time limit.

## Accounts and ownership review fixes (2026-10-31, `master-fixes`)

- The owner is the admin, and each person has their own account (owner, 2026-10-31). Each person answers for their own tasks and their recovery; the admin neither sees nor resumes another person's tasks. So releasing the kill switch resumes nothing (K53) and posts each stopped run's person a notice in their own inbox ("Stopped by the kill switch"), whose Continue resumes that run as the task's Resume does (refused while the switch is on again); the release answers how many were told. A card links to its task only for that task's person. Reason: since tasks became their person's alone, the owner could no longer resume them as DECISIONS said, and a stopped scheduled run sat suspended until its person happened to look. Test: `ownership_review.rs`.
- A password typed in the app opens a session only when the status client says the account is enabled, has no security key and no step left to do; otherwise (and when the status client is unset, has no secret, or cannot be reached) the person is sent to Keycloak's page. Reason: Keycloak's password grant has no security-key step, so the password alone skipped a required key or a required setup; and a status read that failed answered "use the browser" without counting the guess. Unit test: `oidc.rs`.
- A password attempt counts against the lockout before the provider is asked, and is forgiven unless the provider refuses it (it cannot answer, or the password was right and needs the code or the browser). Reason: the check came before the round trip and the count after it, so hundreds of parallel guesses all passed the check; counting a right password that needs its code would lock out a person after a few code prompts. The tests that used the owner's backup password with Keycloak (`app.rs`, `desktop_app.rs`) now get a code from `magnus sign-in-code`.
- The owner's backup password also needs a one-time machine code, printed by `magnus sign-in-code` on the appliance (valid 10 minutes, kept hashed in the state directory), and has its own lockout counter. The app asks for it when the provider is down. Reason: "this machine only" was judged by the absence of proxy headers, which a plain relay (`ssh -L`, socat, a stream proxy) adds none of, so the backup password, with no second factor, worked from anywhere; and remote wrong passwords shared the counter, so an attacker could lock the owner out of the backup during an outage. A code only a shell on the appliance can print is what "this machine" means; the owner's answer on a loopback listener is not needed for it.
- Wrong passwords sent on the appliance's own machine (loopback, not forwarded) count apart from those sent over the network, so anyone on the network can lock a name out over the network but not on the appliance itself (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: the count was per name alone, so a stranger on the LAN locked the owner out everywhere with a few wrong passwords. Not changed: the network's count stays per name, not per address, since a LAN attacker can take any number of IPv6 addresses, and the app's LAN connection carries no device identity to count by; that trade-off is the owner's. Every counter is its scope (`net:`, `local:`, `backup:`) then the whole person id, so no person's counter is another's whatever their ids (review of #154: with the network's counter the bare id, five wrong passwords for a person named `local:carol` locked carol on the appliance). Tests: `wrong_passwords_from_the_network_do_not_lock_the_appliances_own_machine` (app.rs), `one_persons_counter_is_never_anothers` (accounts.rs).
- Starting a browser or device sign-in is limited per address: at most a quarter of the pending sign-ins (at least 5) in 10 minutes (429). Reason: starting one needs no sign-in and took one of 100 shared slots for 10 minutes, so one address could block everyone's first sign-in.
- Every 10 minutes the appliance asks the identity provider whether each signed-in person is still active, and ends the sessions of those it says are not (an unanswered question ends nothing). Reason: sessions last 30 days and were never checked again, so turning someone off in Keycloak did not shut them out.
- A schedule accepted from a proposal keeps its run's budget, each limit no more than the appliance's `[budget]`; a scheduled run's budget is likewise capped by the current `[budget]` when it starts. Reason: an extension the person gave one run (doubled each time) became every scheduled run's for good, and a lower `[budget]` later was ignored. Test: `ownership_review.rs`.
- `schedule_ended`'s reason is content, and the event names the run that ended it, so removing the task removes what the model wrote there. Reason: the model's words (an invoice amount from private mail, say) stayed in the log for good.
- A schedule's own notices (paused, failed, ended) have no Continue: the app does not offer it and the appliance refuses it (400). The schedule's Resume is what goes on. Reason: on a paused notice Continue said "Continuing." while the schedule stayed paused; on "ended itself" it resumed the run past whatever it was waiting for (its review). Test: `ownership_review.rs`.
- Dismissing the notice a stopped run waits on leaves that run waiting on no card; the schedule's Resume refusal says to answer the run, or resume or cancel the task. Reason: the run stayed tied to a card put away, the schedule stayed paused, and Resume answered 409 with nothing left to answer. Unit test: `state.rs`.
- A scheduled run counts against its schedule (`started`, `last_fired`) when its task is created by the schedule's actor, and the later `schedule_fired` does not count it again. Reason: a crash between the two let one run past `max_runs`. Unit test: `state.rs`.
- Release channels for the desktop app (owner, 2026-10-31: "I am the only developer. There will be testers and they will get updates once I approve the test release. The production releases will update production agents. No one but me should have a choice what update they are getting."; "There are testers, production release, and active development. It's that simple."; "If a release is under development it is not merged into main. If it is under testing it is merged into main. If it is a product release it would be stable and released as the authoritative code."; "If stable moves the app back to before any of the updates. That is by definition not stable."). Three channels, each filled by CI from the build box's appliance updates directory: development, each pull request's tested build (`desktop/development/`, `<next>-test.<run>.pr.<n>`); test, master's after each merge (`desktop/test/`, `<next>-test.<run>`), so merging is approving a build for testers; production, a `desktop-v*` tag (`desktop/`). The appliance decides which channel an app gets by who is signed in, never the app or its person: the owner's apps development, testers' (the `tester` role) test, everyone else's production; nothing to choose. Apps from before channels ask without a session and get the channel their version was built for (changed the same day, below: without a session a release gets production and a pre-release nothing). An app gets production's build only while its channel has none. An app only ever moves forward (Tauri's updater default, a newer version than it runs), and `release/publish.sh` never replaces a channel's build with an older one; pre-releases are numbered by CI run so each is newer than the last. Reason: owner's instructions above; one feed for every build let a pull request's build reach every app; moving forward only is how browsers' channels work and keeps an app from returning to a build from before its updates.
- One pull request at a time (owner, 2026-10-31: "There should not be multiple conflicting PR's. There should not be regressed app updates."; "There needs to be a consistent update and PR process that is not violated."). After the owner's app went from pull request #31's build to #37's, numbered later but built without #31's work. The process: (1) one pull request open at a time, from current master, plans and documents included; the next opens only after it merges; (2) its tested build goes to development, the owner's apps; (3) the owner says merge: master, and its build goes to test, the testers'; (4) a `desktop-v*` tag goes to production, everyone's. So each build holds the one before it, and no app goes back. A pull request is merged as soon as its CI passes (owner, 2026-10-31: "merge every PR when its CI passes"), so none waits to go stale and be tested again ("testing pr's then leaving them then testing them again is costing days of wasted time"). It is tested once: CI on the pull request; master's run after the merge publishes the pull request's own build, unchanged, when the merged files are exactly the pull request's that passed ("Master publishes its pull request's build"); locally, only the tests being changed run (full local runs duplicated CI on the same machine and slowed it into failures). CI runs every suite (unit, invariants, scratch, the installed app) on each pull request and master build before its build is published; before, a pull request ran only the installed-app and injection tests, and a broken sign-in passed. `release/publish.sh` enforces it: each channel's `latest.json` records the commit it was built from (a pull request's head), and a build without that commit is refused with CI failing, whatever its version. The appliance never moves an app from its channel's build to a newer production one, which need not hold the channel's work. Reason: owner's instructions above; ordering builds by run number alone let a pull request's build replace another's work.
- How work lands (owner, 2026-10-31: "verify that all future work is created according to software development best practices. The never ending PR's that go stale are wasting days of work."): seven rules in CLAUDE.md, "How work lands", which every session reads first: one pull request open at a time in the order opened; from current master into master, never stacked; kept holding master by merging it in; one concern, small (a warning past 800 changed lines outside tests and documents); done means a failing-then-passing test per fix, every suite green, DECISIONS and PROGRESS in the same pull request; the session that opens it drives it to merged or closed, and merges it itself once every check is green, it holds master and no review thread waits (owner, 2026-11-01: "yes, merge each PR yourself once green"); merged or closed within a day of going green, never left open for later. CI checks the first three on every pull request (`pr-rules`, `release/pr-rules.sh`) and comments every six hours on any with no activity for a day, saying what it waits on (`pr-stale`, `release/pr-stale.sh`); the template asks for the tests and decisions. Reason: pull requests were opened in parallel and stacked, kept open across days while review rounds were added to them (#19 for three days), and conflicted with master until their work was closed and redone (#1, #2, #19, #31). For the owner: GitHub's branch protection on master can make `pr-rules` and `release-checks` required and require branches to be up to date, which no workflow can set. Test: `pr_rules.rs`.
- Several pull requests at once (owner, 2026-11-04: the one-at-a-time rule, added by a coding agent after dozens of unmerged pull requests, now blocks finished work; replace it with something better than either extreme). Supersedes the "one open at a time, in the order opened" check and the requirement that every open branch already hold master. What stays: into master only, never stacked; one concern, small; the session that opens a pull request merges or closes it; the owner's app never goes backwards. What changes: up to six agent pull requests (a `cursor/` branch, a bot, the `agent` label, or a Claude Code description) may be open, and CI fails only a new one past that cap, not the ones already open and not a person's pull request; a branch behind master is a warning, because merging master into every open pull request retests each of them; a new push cancels that pull request's earlier run; a draft does not run the desktop or injection jobs; `pr-stale` comments after a day, labels `stale` after three and closes after five (the branch is kept, `hold` stops the close). Development publish that lacks another open pull request's head leaves the channel as it is and the job passes (test and production, and a development build that lacks master's work, still fail). The merge queue is what lands green pull requests onto current master without those retests; turning it on, and auto-merge, is an admin setting this change does not flip (allow_auto_merge is false; classic branch protection could not be read with this token). Reason: one unfinished pull request was stopping the rest, and keeping every branch current was the retesting the owner had wanted to stop. Tests: `pr_rules.rs`, `release_channels.rs`.
- Work starts from an issue (owner, 2026-11-05, #172; parent #169: random review fixes racing into master caused conflict storms, so agents share one tracker). An agent opens a product or fix pull request only for an open issue in this repository labelled `ready` (usually also `queue`), or one the owner names. Before coding it takes `ready` off, adds `in-progress` and comments the claim; one agent owns that issue. The pull request is the only one for it and carries `Fixes #N`, so the merge closes it and `in-progress` comes off. When the work cannot go on, `in-progress` comes off, `blocked` goes on, and the issue says why. One change that touches `PROGRESS.md`, `DECISIONS.md`, the release and tag paths or live OAuth is in flight; a second waits, or the agents agree on the issue who goes first. Work that leaves those files untouched may run beside it. The issues say what may be opened; the cap of six open agent pull requests still says how many. CI leaves it to the instruction (`release/pr-rules.sh` is unchanged). Reason: unsolicited pull requests edited the same files at once. It keeps "Several pull requests at once" and adds where the work comes from.
- At most one open issue carries `ready` (owner, 2026-11-05, #181; parent #169). Supersedes, in "Work starts from an issue", treating every `ready` label as a pull, and the line that agents may agree who edits the shared files first. After #172, eight issues were labelled `ready` at once and agents claimed several in parallel. `ready` is the single next pull for work that shares the hot files (`PROGRESS.md`, `DECISIONS.md`, the release and tag paths, live OAuth). The owner may mark further issues `ready` only when they do not share those files. A new finding or product idea is labelled `queue` only until the owner, or the agent finishing the one `in-progress` claim, promotes it to `ready`. `queue` alone is the backlog and is not pullable. Claiming still takes `ready` off and adds `in-progress`. Reason: several `ready` labels put shared-file work in flight together again. The claim, `Fixes #N`, the cap of six and one shared-file change in flight stay as in "Work starts from an issue". CI still leaves it to the instruction.
- Copying an answer in the installed app needs `xclip` on the runner (2026-11-05, run 41961388534). Twelve installed-app tests passed on build-box-beta; `every_message_action_works_in_the_installed_app` failed with `spawnSync xclip ENOENT`. The workflow installs `xclip` with the WebKit driver when it is missing. Reason: the copy check reads the clipboard through xclip (`desktop/tests/app-chat-actions.mjs`).
- The installed-app tests install WebKitWebDriver and tauri-driver when the runner lacks them (2026-11-05, run 41959422399 on build-box-beta). All 13 stopped in 2 seconds at `WebKitWebDriver (set MAGNUS_WEBKIT_DRIVER)`. `desktop/README.md` names `webkit2gtk-driver` and `cargo install tauri-driver --locked`. The workflow installs each when it is missing, before those tests. Reason: the Windows cross build is not the only tool the release job needs that the build box already has.
- The Windows installer build installs mingw and NSIS when the runner lacks them (2026-11-05, run 41958793139 on build-box-beta). After `rustup target add x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, the compile failed with `error calling dlltool 'x86_64-w64-mingw32-dlltool': No such file or directory`. `desktop/README.md` already names mingw-w64 and NSIS. The workflow installs `gcc-mingw-w64-x86-64`, `binutils-mingw-w64-x86-64` and `nsis` when `dlltool` or `makensis` is missing. Reason: the rustup target is not the cross linker.
- The Windows installer build installs its rustup target first (2026-11-05, run 41957162011 on build-box-beta). Linux bundles signed; `cargo tauri build --target x86_64-pc-windows-gnu` then failed because that target was not installed (installed: `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, `x86_64-unknown-linux-musl`). `desktop/README.md` already says the cross build needs `rustup target add x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`. The workflow does that before the Windows build. Reason: a runner with rustup and the release key is still not the build box until it has the target the installer uses.
- The update test's two AppImages compile after the suite, not beside it (2026-11-05, run 41949527809). `nice -n 19` did not keep them off the tests: 7 of 414 failed while they compiled (a browser click that had found its link, a sign-in whose 30-second step-up had already lapsed, a schedule notice past 90 seconds, the update switch, a console browser past 240 seconds). The installed-app tests still wait for the builds. The console sign-in tests that expect a fresh step-up use 90 seconds, then wait it out. Reason: the suite was right and the machine was busy. The same step-up window was lengthened for the other console tests on 2026-11-04.
- A magnus-linux runner without the Windows gnu target still builds the installer (2026-11-05). build-box-beta has that label and rustup, and not `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, so Installers exited before any test (master runs 41958146895 and 41955875225). `release/ensure-windows-target.sh` adds the target when it is missing, and the installer step runs it. Reason: release-checks has to reach the tests on every runner that carries `magnus-linux`. Test: `a_runner_without_the_windows_target_installs_it` (ci.rs).
- A runner without the release key still runs release-checks, and does not publish (2026-11-05). `build-box-beta` has `magnus-linux` and no `~/.magnus-release/update.key`. Tauri decoded that path as the key (`Invalid symbol 46`, run 41948685783). Requiring `magnus-light` as well matched no runner: the job sat queued (run 41949170226), so build-box does not carry that label. `release/signing-key.sh` uses the release key when the file is there and a throwaway key otherwise. Publish, the kept build, and the appliance install run only for the release key (`MAGNUS_PUBLISH=1`). Reason: the key stays on the build box, and a test run on beta must not replace the owner's app.
- Release-checks still failed after the throughput limits (2026-11-05). `build-box-beta` carries `magnus-linux` and has rustup but not `cargo-tauri`, so a job there exited at Installers with `no such command: tauri` (master run 41942081672; the same on #138, #139 and #144). `release/ensure-tauri.sh` installs tauri-cli 2 when `cargo tauri` is missing and prints `~/.cargo/bin` onto the job's PATH. A browser click, type or select waits 45 seconds: at 10 seconds `browser_navigates_clicks_types_and_screenshots` failed on a busy build box (runs 41930362272, 41902288030, 41929170386) and passed on that box when it ran one suite (#141). The console's "not on loopback" check reads that process's listen sockets, because a connect to `127.0.0.1` on the console's port also reaches another test's listener (#143). The desktop type test waits until xterm has focus. Realm cleanup reads a last line that has no newline, which its test writes. Reason: those were the failures still turning release-checks red; disabling the tests would hide them.
- CI throughput on the shared runner (owner, 2026-11-04: "investigate why this problem is screwing up all the PR's since we updated the PR process"; "Use build-box-2 for quick checks only"; "Why does every test need to run on every PR? Most of them are small changes?"). Supersedes, in "Several pull requests at once", the warning that a branch behind master should merge master in. What happened: agent sessions merged master into every open pull request each time master moved and reran failed runs; each merge cancelled and restarted that pull request's 150-minute run; one hung test (an unbounded wait for a browser sign-in that never came back) held a runner until its time ran out; and the two runners on one machine ran two full suites at once, slowing tests past their own limits. What changes: (1) behind master is a notice, not a warning, and says not to merge master in for that alone, only for a conflict (`release/pr-rules.sh`, `release/pr-stale.sh`, CLAUDE.md rule 3). (2) GitHub's merge queue cannot be turned on (the repository is private and owned by a person; the ruleset API refuses the rule), so master's own run after a merge tests everything and is the gate. (3) A pull request runs only the tests its changed paths reach (`release/ci-select.sh`): documents none, a test file itself, a connector its crate and scratch tests, the kernel every suite; the installers, the update builds, the installed-app tests, the kept build and the development publish only when `desktop/`, the build, the workflow or an unknown path changed. Master, tags, a manual run and the merge queue run everything, and master reuses a pull request's build only when that pull request ran everything. (4) Every test has a time limit: 5 minutes, 10 for the console and microVM suites (`.config/nextest.toml`); the browser sign-in wait gives up in three minutes; the update test's held reply in PATIENCE; the update builds' wait in 60 minutes and the installed-app tests in 20. (5) Each run first deletes test realms (t and a UUID) and mail sinks left on the test Keycloak for three hours (`release/ci-clean.sh`). (6) The rules and stale checks run on `magnus-light` (build-box-2, and build-box), the release and injection checks on `magnus-linux` (build-box only), so only one full suite runs on the machine at a time. Reason: the runs were restarting and hanging, not failing on their changes. Tests: `ci.rs`, `pr_rules.rs`.
- A test appliance's port stays taken across a kill (CI on #132, `a_start_waits_for_the_kernel_before_it_to_let_go_of_the_log`: two appliances bound 127.0.0.1:40059). The harness writes the bound port back into the config so a restart is the same address, then the kill frees it, and for the two seconds that test holds the log's lock another suite's port 0 is given it. The harness now binds the socket first and holds it until the test ends. The child inherits it (`MAGNUS_LISTEN_FD`), including across an update's exec, and a configured address that is not that socket is still bound by the process (the release that must fail to listen). The update test gives the port up (`release_port`) before it starts its own process on that address: a harness that kept listening after `kill` queued the health check and never accepted it, so `a_signed_release_installs_while_a_task_runs_and_goes_back_one_step` ran until the job was cancelled. Reason: binding, releasing, and binding again is the race fixed for the first start in 2026-10-29 and left open on restart. Test: `a_killed_appliances_port_stays_taken_until_it_restarts`.

- When the provider cannot say whether a person has a security key, a password typed in the app never signs them in, with or without an authenticator code: they go to the provider's page, uncounted. Reason: Keycloak's password grant ignores a code from a person without an authenticator app, so for a passkey-only account a password and any six digits would pass. The sign-in fallbacks test (#36) asked for the opposite; its intent, never locked out or called wrong for being sent to the browser, is checked once the account can be looked up again.

## Fixes from old pull requests (2026-10-31)

Ported from closed pull requests #1 (`review-fixes`), #5 (`scratch-port-zero`) and #8 (`memory-list`) onto current code; each with a test that fails without it.

- A check is `appliance-verified` only when everything it runs is on an allowlist in `sandbox/check_tools.py` (image programs known only to read what they are given, data-only Python modules, `python3 -c` code that imports only modules that run no other code and reaches `os`/`sys` only through known-safe attributes, no getattr/eval/exec/`__import__`/dunders, awk without commands, sed that only prints, deletes or substitutes, jq without files); everything else is `self-checked`. Supersedes the list of what runs code in the labelling line above. Reason: that list missed stdin-fed interpreters (`sh < run.sh`, `python3 < t.py`), `from os import system`, `getattr(os, 'system')`, `os.posix_spawn`, `unittest.main` and awk's `system()`, each labelled appliance-verified (review, 2026-10-28, PR #1). Test: `python3 -I sandbox/test_check_tools.py` (the old resolver fails 27 of its cases). The sandbox image must be rebuilt (`sandbox/build.sh`) for the appliance to use it.
- A person's decision, on an inbox item (`inbox.rs` `respond`) or a chat card (`chat.rs` `decide_card`), is checked and recorded under one lock (`Kernel.decide`), so answers that race in decide it once. Reason: two concurrent confirms made two schedules, and two answers to one item were both recorded (review, 2026-10-28, PR #1). Tests: `a_card_confirmed_twice_at_once_makes_one_schedule`, `an_approval_answered_twice_at_once_is_decided_once` (chat.rs; both fail 3 of 3 runs without the lock).
- `contract.conversation` is the kernel's: it holds the chat a task came from and is dropped from any contract the model sends (`run/mod.rs` `with_conversation`). Reason: the contract and contract-change cards show it as "From the chat", so an agent could show the person words they did not say (review, 2026-10-28, PR #1). Test: `a_contract_cannot_claim_a_conversation` (chat.rs; fails without the fix).
- A schedule confirmed from a chat card keeps the card's scope (`ScheduleSet.scope`), each run it starts carries it (`TaskCreated.scope`), and the run's context tells the model "The person confirmed this schedule for this scope; keep to it: …" after the task's text. The task's text stays the person's words. Reason: the person confirms the scope on the card, and the runs never saw it (review, 2026-10-28, PR #1). PR #1 appended the scope to the schedule's text; on current code that changes every run's task text, which `a_schedule_from_chat_fires_after_a_restart` matches exactly, so the scope is a field of its own (content, deletable with the rest). Test: `a_confirmed_schedule_carries_its_scope` (chat.rs; fails when the card's scope is not kept).
- A memory search with an empty query (or one with no word of three letters) lists the person's facts, newest first (20 at most, as any search), and `memory_search`'s description says so. Reason: bug (owner, 2026-10-29, "agent has no memory still"): asked "what do you have in memory?", the live model searched with an empty query, which matched nothing, and it said it had no memories while two were kept (PR #8). Test: `asked_what_it_remembers_it_lists_what_is_kept` (memory.rs; fails without the fix).
- A memory search takes its result's taint and sources from the same entries it returns, in one lookup (`boundary.rs`: `memory_text` and `memory_taint` over one `memory_hits`). Reason: it looked them up twice, so a fact forgotten in between could return untrusted text with a clean label (M1; review, 2026-10-28, PR #1). No test reproduces the race: nothing awaits between the two lookups, so the window is microseconds, and PR #1 had no test for it either. `memory_does_not_launder_taint` (knowledge_review.rs) covers the taint the search now takes (it fails when that taint is dropped).
- Scratch-test ports without the bind-port-0-and-let-go race: `with_tls` sets `tls_listen` to port 0, reads the bound address from the appliance's log (`cli.rs` now logs the HTTPS listener's bound address, as it already did the plain one) and writes it back so restarts keep it; the connectors tests hold their callback port for the whole test; tauri-driver cannot take port 0 (it must tell WebKitWebDriver its port and reports neither), so an installed-app test's driver counts as started only when `ss` shows both ports held by its own process tree, else it ends that driver's process group and picks again (three tries), and a failure says what happened on each try (the driver's exit and its output, or who held the port). Its ports are picked below the ephemeral range (`ip_local_port_range`), never from it: the tests' outgoing connections take their local ports there, so a port picked there could be taken before the driver bound it (master's run for #31 lost three tries in a row, 2026-10-31, with no record of why). A listener `ss` lists without naming its owner counts as not yet known, not as another process's: `ss` reads the processes' open files before it lists the sockets, so a port bound in between has no owner in its answer (the recorded reasons showed the driver's own processes on all three lost tries). The installed-app scripts find an element again when the app has redrawn it (`desktop/tests/appdriver.mjs`), as a person's click lands on the redrawn one. Reason: a port picked and released before the appliance or driver took it could be taken by a parallel test, which then answered for it (as the harness's own race did, fixed 2026-10-29; PR #5). Test: `plain_http_on_the_https_port_is_sent_to_https` (app.rs) fails without the `cli.rs` change (the log names port 0). The same race remains in `connectors_review.rs` (two binds), which PR #5 predates.
- The kill switch revokes app sign-ins at once and records each result after the record of the vault's sealing (or its failure). Reason: the sealing record starts the list of revocations, so a result recorded before it was wiped and the owner was told a sign-in was not revoked when it was; the connectors test failed 4 runs in 10 without this, none in 10 with it.
- The sandbox guest image is built from the code it runs: `sandbox/image.sh` builds it from the checkout's sandbox sources (the guest program, `check_tools.py`, the image definition) and keeps it on the build box by their hash, building only when they change; CI tests every commit on its own image, and strix-alpha's appliance is given the image of the commit it runs.
