Reason: every run used one image built on 2026-10-28, so a sandbox fix (the `check_tools.py` allowlist) would have been merged but never used.
- The agent's workspace becomes one disposable microVM per person and session (Chrome, a shell and a file manager, otherwise minimal), shared live with the person through a view with hand-off; files stay in Magnus, credentials in the vault, the website cookie jar in a boundary proxy that is the VM's only way out and logs every request. Plan: `docs/proposals/agent-workspace.md`. Implementation deferred. Reason: owner (2026-10-29), so the person and the agent can work in the same browser ("teach me" both ways) and the agent can act with the person's credentials without holding them; it needs the owner's approval of invariant 1's new wording and a choice on invariant 2 for logins.
- Master runs on the build box's appliance: every master build ends by putting its programs, the browser sidecar (`browser/`) and its sandbox image on strix-alpha's appliance as one signed appliance release numbered like the app's (`release/to-appliance.sh`, config from the `MAGNUS_APPLIANCE_CONFIG` variable), which the appliance installs by itself with rollback (strix-alpha looks every minute: `[updates] check_minutes = 1`); the step waits until the appliance runs that version. The browser sidecar runs from the install directory (`[browser] command`), never from a working checkout. The image goes in as two links in the install directory (`[sandbox] kernel_image` and `rootfs` name them) to the appliance's own copy (`sandbox/<hash>` beside it), so a release and its image switch and roll back together; the appliance keeps the images of its running release and of the one kept for rollback. The log's tables are migrated first with the Postgres admin address in an owner-only `admin-url` file beside the config (set 2026-11-01, owner-approved). The updates directory keeps the latest appliance package and the one before. Reason: merges reached the apps but not the server they talk to, which was redeployed by hand and fell behind, its browser ran from whatever branch the working checkout had, and its image lived in CI's store (owner, 2026-10-31). The appliance's own update path is used, not a restart from CI, so a release that cannot start is rolled back and CI never holds the server's process.
- Every start of the kernel waits up to 15 seconds for the event log's single-writer lock, not only a start after a switch of releases. Reason: Postgres frees the lock of a kernel that was killed (a crash, a restart) only once it sees its connection closed, so a start right after one exited with "another kernel holds this event log" (the crash invariant failed so in CI, 2026-10-31; a supervisor restarting the appliance after a crash would hit the same). A second kernel really running is still refused, after the wait. Test: `a_start_waits_for_the_kernel_before_it_to_let_go_of_the_log` (log_connection.rs) fails without this.

## Review of master fixes after #31 (2026-10-31, `master-review`)

- Only a session says which channel an app gets. Without one that still holds (signed out, ended, or an app from before channels), an app on a release gets production, and one on a pre-release gets nothing (204) until it asks signed in; a pre-release older than `MIN_APP`, which cannot sign in at all, gets production, its only way forward. Development's and test's packages are served only to the apps given that channel (the updater asks for them with the session it asked with); production's stay anyone's. Reason: with no session, the channel came from the version the request named, so anyone could choose development or test with a header ("No one but me should have a choice") and download the owner's unmerged builds; and an app whose session had ended was given production, which after a tag is newer than a pull request's build without holding its work, so the owner's app could go back. Cost: a pre-release app from before channels, which never sends its session, no longer updates and is reinstalled once (the owner's apps had already moved to #37's build, which sends it). Test: `release_channels.rs`.
- `release/publish.sh` records in `latest.json` the pull request (`pr`) and the master commit the build stood on (`base`) beside its commit. When the build in a channel is an earlier push of the same pull request, or of one closed without merging (CI asks GitHub and passes it as `MAGNUS_GIVEN_UP`), the next build need only hold that `base`: the closed pull request's work was given up on purpose, and master's still never goes back. Reason: the build of a pull request closed unmerged (or pushed again with its commits replaced) was in no later build, so every later pull request's publish failed and CI stayed red until someone edited the channel by hand. The workflow's token reads pull requests for it. Test: `release_channels.rs`.
- A card shown in several places at once (its conversation, the panel, Needs you) keeps the focus and the words in the copy being typed in: redrawing another copy neither takes the focus nor overwrites the draft. Reason: the rewrite shows one card in up to three places with one draft key, and the panel's redraw moved the focus into its empty copy, splitting an answer between two boxes. Test: `workspace_review.rs` (with `desktop/tests/workspace-review.mjs`).
- A decision is sent once however many handlers see its click (`respond` returns when its button is already disabled). Reason: in Settings > Connectors both the section's handler and the page-wide one sent it, and the person was told "already decided" after "Approved.".
- Every conversation in a project sits under it in the sidebar. Reason: `map` passed the index as the `nested` flag, so the first was drawn as a loose conversation.
- Settings > Data and privacy says when the backups are on the appliance's own disk, as Home did before the workspace removed it.
- Signing out also forgets the Library's search and project filter. Reason: the next person on the same computer saw "No file matches." under a filter they could not see.
- Not changed, for the owner: from the review of master's other fixes, `check_tools.py` rejects process substitution (`diff <(…) <(…)`) as a program it does not know (it over-refuses, never mislabels); and a bare `os` name in `python3 -c` with no import is not flagged, which cannot run (no module is loaded). The appliance's sandbox image is not yet delivered with its release; #42 does that. (Wrong about process substitution, below: inside `bash -c` it was labelled appliance-verified.)
- `check_tools.py` reads a check as the shell and each tool read it (review, 2026-11-04). The shell: `#` starts a comment only at the start of a word (shlex started one inside `x#`); a backslash keeps a quote from opening; `$(…)`, backticks and bash's `<(…)`/`>(…)` are checked as commands, their closing parenthesis found outside quotes; `$'…'` is refused. Variables that move lookups (now also `PS4`, `PROMPT_COMMAND`, `BASHOPTS`, `GCONV_PATH`) are refused however they are set or cleared: `unset`, `read`, `printf -v`, `wait -p`, a `for` loop's variable, `env -u`, `env -i`, `exec -c`, and assignments inside `$((…))`, `$[…]` and `${…}` (`${X:=…}`). A quoted command substitution where bash evaluates text as arithmetic or a variable's name (`x='a[$(cmd)]'; : $((x))`) is self-checked. A program named by a relative path (`../usr/bin/cat`, resolved from wherever the check has `cd`'d) is refused. The tools: each wrapper's own options are known, with which take a value (`env -u NAME cmd`, `xvfb-run -e FILE cmd`), and any other option, or `env -S`, makes the check self-checked; a wrapper named by its image path (`/usr/bin/env`) is the same wrapper (`env` is no longer a reader); python3's options cluster and take attached values (`-Wmod.Cat`, `-Xpycache_prefix=…`), `-X pycache_prefix` is refused and an `-X` option not known to leave lookups alone is self-checked; GNU long options are matched by any unambiguous abbreviation (`sort --com=`, `sed --fi=`, `sed --expr=`); sed and jq options are read wherever they stand, sed's and awk's clustered (`-bf`, `-e'…'`). Reason: each of these labelled a check `appliance-verified` while workspace code decided its result, against "Doubt counts as self-checked" (review of master, 2026-11-04: 31 cases). `sandbox/test_check_tools.py` now runs in CI (`desktop.yml`, "Check-tool labels"); before, nothing ran it. Test: `python3 -I sandbox/test_check_tools.py` (the old resolver fails 31 of its cases). The sandbox image must be rebuilt (`sandbox/build.sh`) for the appliance to use it.
- `check_tools.py` looks for assignments inside expansions only in text the shell expands, not in single quotes, after a backslash or in a comment, except where bash evaluates arithmetic: there a quoted value counts too, and so does every assignment in the command, since bash evaluates a variable's value as arithmetic (`x='PATH=5'; : $((x))` sets PATH). Shell code given to `sh -c` is read with `\$` and `` \` `` as the inner shell may see them. (Review of #136, 2026-11-04.) Reason: #136 refused checks that only print such text, `printf '%s\n' '${PATH:=/x}'`; and shlex keeps the backslash of `"\$"` where the shell drops it, which hid the inner script's arithmetic. A `#` after a `${` is never taken for a comment, since inside `${...}` it is text (`${x- # $((PATH=0))}` sets PATH; review of #144); finding where each `${` closes would need bash's parser, and keeping the text visible can only refuse more.

- Identity and characters (owner's behavior spec, 2026-10-31; owner: "The character is integral. It is not a switch or something to be turned off by the models. All the testing of the models with the personality is over engineering. The character exists regardless of model change."; "if there is one character there is no reason to have a switch to turn the character off."). Magnus's functional personality (ownership, one recommendation, calibrated confidence, holding evidence-based positions, deferring once the person decides, noticing unasked, honest about failure, its own identity) is written into both prompts as a description of who it is, the same for every provider. Its one character, the spec's chief of staff, is `instructions/character.md`, appended to both prompts: every run speaks in it, on every model, with no test gate, no choice in the app and no policy to take it away. Its text is part of the instructions version each run pins and each model call logs. It reads aloud in the appliance's installed voice. The kernel's own passes (filing memory) do not speak and carry neither. Reason: owner's spec and instructions above; with one character, a picker or a policy could only turn it off.
- strix-alpha's services come back by themselves: the appliance is the systemd user service `magnus-appliance` (`Restart=always`, lingering on, so it starts at boot), and the containers `magnus-claude-pg` and `magnus-searxng` restart unless stopped, as `magnus-claude-kc` did. CI starts Postgres and Keycloak and waits for both (`dev/services.sh`, two minutes at most; Keycloak starts here in 5-7 s) before any test or deploy. Reason: after a reboot on 2026-11-01 the server stayed down (it was started by hand) and the runner took a job before Postgres was back, which failed.
- The appliance's own Postgres: strix-alpha's appliance keeps its log in `magnus-appliance-pg` (`config/install-postgres.sh`: the dev image, Postgres 16 with pgvector pinned by digest; its own volume; 127.0.0.1:54341 only; scram-sha-256; data checksums; restarted unless stopped), never in the dev and test `magnus-claude-pg`. Its admin password is made on the box and kept only in owner-only files beside the config (`postgres-admin.password`, `admin-url`), never in git or printed. Moved on 2026-11-01 by dump and data-only restore after `magnus migrate` (counts checked: 11,822 events, 1,666 contents); the dump is kept owner-only in `state/db-moves/`. Reason (owner, 2026-11-01: "A single postgres database is lazy. Build it to best practices."): every test run is the test server's admin, its admin password is in git (`tests/invariants/src/pg.rs`), and one container stopping took down both CI and the live server.
- CI runs the unit, invariant and scratch suites with cargo-nextest (pinned, `NEXTEST` in the workflows), every test of every binary at once, and builds the update test's two AppImages beside them (`desktop/tests/update-builds.sh`, waited for before the installed-app tests). Reason (owner, 2026-11-01: "why is ci so slow?"): `cargo test` ran the 49 test binaries one after another, 18 of a pull request's 30 minutes, mostly waiting on time (schedules.rs alone 224 s) on a 32-core box; with nextest the same 201 tests took 4.8 minutes on strix-alpha, all passing. The suites have no doc tests, which nextest does not run.
- Master publishes its pull request's build (owner, 2026-11-01: "why is it testing the code on the pr and then again?"; "do it now, don't wait"): a pull request's run, once every test passes, keeps its installers and the appliance's release programs on the build box (`~/magnus-ci/builds/<tree>`, by the merged files' tree; the newest two), and master's run after the merge, when its files are exactly that tree, publishes them unchanged to test and to strix-alpha's appliance, with their versions; it builds only when that build is no longer kept. The appliance's programs (`release/appliance-programs`) are built in release mode after the pull request's tests pass, not beside them, so the tests, which have time limits, share the machine with no more builds than before (the update test's two). (Built beside them at first, run 41265192814 failed; that was blamed on the third build and it was moved, but the next run failed the same way without it: the cause was the test Postgres, below.) Since one build goes to development and then test, its version names its run, not a channel: `<next>-test.<run>.pr.<n>` for a pull request's, `<next>-test.<run>` for one master built itself, the same for the appliance. `test` is kept, not a new word, because versions compare their words alphabetically: `test.<run>.pr.<n>` sorts after every earlier `test.<run>` and `review.<run>.pr.<n>`, so apps and the appliance move forward to it without a version bump. Reason: master rebuilt the installers (their version is compiled in) and the appliance's programs after every merge, about 7 minutes from merge to running, and what testers and the appliance got were bytes no test had run on; now the build the owner reviewed in development is the one that ships.
- A run that ends badly and its notice are one write (`emit_all`, each event with its own actor): a task the model fails (`failed` and its "Failed" notice), a chat turn that leaves something undone (`completed` and its "Not done" notice), and the kill switch's release (and each "Stopped by the kill switch" notice). Reason: each was written after the event it tells of, with the run's stop work between; a reader could see the run ended with no notice (invariant 3's test counted events in between and failed in CI run 41266235376), and a crash between the two would have left the failure silent for good, against K24. Tests: `a_run_that_ends_badly_and_its_notice_are_one_write`, `releasing_the_switch_and_its_notices_are_one_write` (both fail on the code before).
- Every schedule notice is one write with what it tells of: a schedule ended (past its end or runs, or by its own run's `end_schedule`), paused on a person or after failures, or unable to start; and a timer's "your wait has ended" with the run's resume. `post_notice`, which wrote a notice on its own, is gone; `notice_to` builds the event for `emit_all`, and the sweep logs a failed write (it tries again next sweep) instead of dropping it unseen. Reason: the same race as the line above, found while fixing it and left open in #44; a crash between the two writes left a schedule ended or paused with nobody told (K24), or a waiting run told twice. Tests: the three schedule tests in `tests/scratch/tests/schedules.rs` check each schedule notice shares its write with its event, and the kettle's run its wait's end with its resume (they fail on the code before).
- The test Postgres (`magnus-claude-pg`) runs without durability: `fsync`, `synchronous_commit` and `full_page_writes` off, set by `dev/services.sh` before every CI job (Postgres's documentation, "Non-Durable Settings", for data that need not survive a crash). Reason: every test drops its database at the end, and `DROP DATABASE` forces a checkpoint; in CI run 41266235376 these took up to 33 s, 13 to 27 s of it waiting on the disk the installer builds were writing to, and every test's commits waited behind them, so about 70 tests stalled together and one test appliance missed its 30 s start. It holds only test databases (live data moved to `magnus-appliance-pg`, which stays durable). It is made by `dev/postgres.sh`, which holds all its settings (1000 connections, mmap shared memory, no durability) and reads its address and password from the tests' default (`tests/invariants/src/pg.rs`); `dev/services.sh`, before every CI job, makes it again, empty, when it does not answer a query in two minutes, as after a power cut that damaged its data (tried: with its control file removed, the job went on after 124 s). Before, its settings were `ALTER SYSTEM` changes made by hand and nothing could make it again.
- In CI the update test's two builds run at the lowest CPU priority (`nice -n 19`), and the main test step keeps the browser flows' screenshots of a failed step (`MAGNUS_SHOTS`). Reason: in run 41267885346 three browser and schedule tests timed out at the end of the run while those builds compiled on every core, and left no screenshot to say why; the builds have no time limit and the tests do. IO priority is not set: the build box's disk has no IO scheduler (`none`), so it would do nothing.
- A screenshot is evidence, never a check: a browser check that cannot take one logs "screenshot not kept" and goes on, in every script (`flow`, `outcome`, `schedules`, `library`, `volume`, `killswitch`, `requests`, and the app driver), and each test keeps its screenshots in a folder of its own (`magnus_scratch::shots()`: `$MAGNUS_SHOTS/<test>/<n>`). Reason: a test bug the line above created by turning screenshots on in CI: in run 41279528035 (#45) `desktop_flow` passed every check of a step, then failed when the full-page screenshot after it took over 30 s on the loaded build box (the failure screenshot a moment later showed the page complete); and the two sign-in flows numbered their screenshots into one folder, writing over each other's.

## The looks as the references draw them (2026-11-01, `themes`)

- Every token the Magnus Workspace references draw takes the references' value (their `renderVals` and inline styles), replacing the contrast-chosen values of decision 251 that remained: muted text on light glass #55555A and on dark #A1A1A8, chevrons #8A8A8F / #7A7A80, light accent text the accent itself (Blue #3557D9, Teal #1F7A6D, Clay #B4532A), dark Blue accent text #9DB1FF, dark glass search field 10% white; plus the references' file-type colors (`pdf-bg`, `pdf-fg`). Reason: owner, 2026-11-01, with the four boards: "themes as built do not match the spec and references exactly".
- The contrast check (`desktop/tests/theme-check.mjs`) checks the pairs the design draws, not every text color on every surface: body, secondary and muted text on each surface; text on a selected row, the search field, the bubble, the approval card and the segmented control; and every text color on the reading surfaces. Accent-colored text sits on cards and is checked there, never on the glass itself. Reason: the references' values fail pairs the design never draws (accent text straight on glass) and pass every pair it does; every look still reaches 4.5:1.
- Two values not in the references, both outside them: the tint Magnus gives a material it chooses itself (`system`) is 86% (light rgba(248,248,246,0.86), dark rgba(24,24,28,0.86)), so the references' text reaches 4.5:1 over any desktop under Acrylic; and the search field's placeholder and icon on dark glass are #B4B4BB (`search-muted`), since the references' #A1A1A8 over their 10% field falls to 4.2:1 over the wallpaper's darkest color. The app-only soft accents of Teal and Clay are lightened (#EAF6F3, #FDF2EC) for their accent text to reach 4.5:1 on them.
- The layout takes the references' sizes: sidebar 260px, task panel 420px (was 42%), the conversation a 760px column with 32px sides, 44px primary buttons and verdict buttons, 40px search field, Needs you as a 44px card, 34px tabs filling the panel's width, the user's bubble at most 78% wide; headings are sentence case (12px, 600, muted), not uppercase; decision cards carry no colored stripe. Reason: as above.
- A task's result is drawn as the references draw it: each part of the deliverable a card in the app's own colors (the sandboxed frame is given the look's tokens and color scheme and draws on a transparent ground), a timeline with its day in accent text, a table with no header row when its columns are unnamed and its total row set off; then Summary, Checks, the verdict at the foot, and a one-line note of the model, its steps and the cost. Reason: as above; the frame drew white boxes with grey text in every look.
- The conversation marks the row of the task open in the panel when the panel opens or closes, without redrawing the thread. Reason: the mark was set only when the thread was drawn, so opening a task from its row highlighted it only under the pointer.
- The looks test (`tests/scratch/tests/looks.rs`, `desktop/tests/looks.mjs`) sets up the references' scene and checks each look's drawn colors and sizes (and Settings > Appearance's) against the references' values, kept in the test apart from `theme.js`; on master's interface it reports 40 differences.

## Composed results in chat (2026-11-01, `composed-in-chat`)

- What a task started from chat delivers shows in the chat: its result in the thread carries the task's deliverable (`view`, as the task's outcome has it), and the app draws it there in the sandboxed frame, under the result's line and across the conversation's column (a timeline's rows span their card, which a 70-character cap on lists had kept short), with each delivered file's preview still after it (where it is opened or downloaded, as on the outcome page beside its file rows); "See the outcome" still opens the full page beside the conversation. Reason: owner, 2026-11-01: "Composability does not show up in chat". A chat answer's own `show` was drawn, but a task's composed deliverable (a timeline, a table, a chart) was left out of the thread's result, so in the conversation it was a one-line summary. Test: `tests/scratch/tests/chat_results.rs` with `desktop/tests/chat-results.mjs` (fails without the change: the result has no deliverable; with the kernel half alone, the conversation draws none).
- The conversation is a column: its head, then the thread, which alone scrolls, then the composer; nothing lies over the thread. This replaces the sticky head and composer over one scrolling page and the scroll padding that kept them clear (88px and 170px, decided with the flex rows). Reason: the installed app's driver scrolls a target to the edge of the scroller without that padding, and with a deliverable in the thread it found "See the outcome" and the task's row under the composer ("element click intercepted", CI runs 41285037777 and 41290566650). Test: `chat-results.mjs` checks the thread lies under neither (on the old layout, 237px of it lay under the composer).
- A composed frame in a conversation is drawn again at the height it last had, set after the thread is drawn rather than written into its markup (written in, a loaded frame changed the markup and the thread drew itself again, replacing the row the installed app was clicking: "element not interactable", CI run 41286116816). Reason: every redraw of the thread makes its frames anew, at the browser's default 150px until they load, so the conversation shrank by the deliverable's height and its scroll moved; the installed app's flows clicked a row as it moved ("element click intercepted", CI run 41285037777). Test: `chat-results.mjs` forces a redraw and measures the new frame (150px instead of 387px without the change).
- A daily schedule's time is found on the wall clock once a day across clock changes: when clocks go back and its time comes twice, it is due at the first still ahead (a schedule made between the two, at the second), never at both; when clocks go forward past it, it is due as they resume, an hour on, and the next day at its time again. Reason: on 2026-11-01, as clocks went back, this pull request's CI failed twice in the repeated hour: two schedule tests set a daily time a minute or two ahead ("the schedule for 01:16 did not fire after the restart"). chrono's `Local` gives the two instants of a repeated time latest first, so `earliest()` took the second 01:16 and the schedule came due an hour late (in the second pass it would also have skipped a day); and a time in the hour clocks skip (02:30 in March) had no instant at all, and the schedule stopped for good. Carried in this pull request because its CI could not pass in that hour without it. Test: `a_daily_schedule_is_due_once_a_day_across_clock_changes` (state.rs, Los Angeles time, and a time zone that gives the two instants latest first as `Local` does; `chrono-tz` for tests only; fails without the change: due at 09:31 UTC instead of 08:31).

## A closed panel is gone (2026-11-01, `panel-hidden`)

- A closed task panel takes no room and covers nothing (`aside.detail[hidden] { display: none; }`). Reason: #46 gave the panel `display: flex`, which outranks the `hidden` attribute's own rule, so in a window 1100px wide or narrower, where the panel lies over the conversation, the closed panel still covered the conversation's right side and took its clicks. Test: the looks test opens a 1000px window with the panel closed and finds the conversation, not the panel, under its right side (fails without the change).

## Spec review fixes (2026-11-01)

- Someone stays owner however many role changes race in: the check and the change are made under the decision lock (`Kernel::decide`). Reason: the check ran outside any lock, so two owners taking the role from each other at the same moment both passed it and the appliance was left with no owner, and so no one who could set roles, policy or the kill switch (found reviewing the owner's specs against the code, 2026-11-01). Test: `two_owners_demoting_each_other_at_once_leave_one` (settings.rs; without the lock, both demotions succeed in the first round).

## The owner's specs against Magnus today (2026-11-01, `spec-decisions`)

- Where the owner's Connectors, Admin Console and Authentication & Isolation specs (2026-10-31) conflict with recorded decisions or the invariants, nothing is built until the owner rules; `docs/proposals/spec-decisions.md` puts 22 questions to the owner with a proposed answer each (four of them change protected invariant tests and need the owner's approval for that too), and lists what the specs leave open. Reason: the specs reverse decisions the owner made a day earlier (the app's typed password, the backup password, identity by username, the owner's roles), and building either side first would leave the other half in force.

## The owner's rulings on the specs (2026-11-01, `owner-rulings`)

- No passkeys (owner, 2026-11-01: "No passkey"): people go on signing in with their username and password typed in the app (DEC 274 and 276 stand), the browser-with-passkeys sign-in of the authentication design is not built, and the admin signs in and steps up with their password (and code). The backup password stays only as the way back in when the provider is down, on this machine, never for the admin console.
- Approved as proposed in `docs/proposals/spec-decisions.md`: identity by `sub` (3), Keycloak's `admin` and `member` mapped onto Magnus's roles (4), a person can only tighten an approval (5), updates install by themselves with step-up only for a manual install or rollback (6), a signed catalog manifest's classes count as the owner's classification (7), owner controls move to the console one at a time with their invariant tests (8), the console's kill switch seals and revokes as today (10), new work is refused while the kill switch is on (11), a person turned off in Keycloak has their schedules paused (12), config tokens only on the plain loopback listener (13).
- The admin's access to people's data is a policy (owner, 2026-11-01: "In any org personal data is org data and no right to privacy. This should be policy configurable to allow private data."): by default the admin can read every person's tasks, chats, files and memory, connector results included; a policy setting makes people's data private from the admin; each read is recorded.
- Per-person walls in the database (9), approved (owner, 2026-11-01: "approve per person data walls"): Postgres row-level security on the per-person content store, so the connection serving a person sees only that person's content, with the start-up reading of the log through its own role; planned in its own document first, built after 11-14. The smaller conflicts (15-22) are not yet ruled.

## Identity by `sub` (2026-11-01, `identity-sub`)

- A person is the identity provider's account that first signed in as them (its `sub`), not a username: their first sign-in through the provider binds them to it (`SubjectBound`), and an account with another `sub` signing in under their username is refused ("not the one carol signed in with before"). The owner can clear a person's binding (`DELETE /v1/people/{id}/account`), and the next account to sign in as them becomes theirs, for an account made again or a person moved to a new one; Settings' people list says whether each person is bound. Reason: owner's ruling 3 (2026-11-01); a Keycloak account deleted and made again, or renamed, under the same username became the old person, with their tasks, memory, schedules and approvals. People already on the appliance are bound at their next sign-in. The status check still asks by username; it is reworked with ruling 12. Test: `an_account_made_again_under_the_same_username_is_not_the_old_person` (people.rs, on the real Keycloak, in CI).

## New work under the kill switch (2026-11-01, `kill-switch-refuses`)

- While the kill switch is on, new work is refused, not queued: a task, a chat message, a task made from a chat message, or a message sent again is answered 409 "The kill switch is on: nothing new starts until the owner releases it", and nothing is created or changed (a message sent again is checked before its old turn is hidden). Every new run passes one check (`Kernel::create_run`), and the API turns its refusal into that answer; schedules already did not fire under the switch. Runs the switch stopped are unchanged: on release their people get "Stopped by the kill switch" with Continue (DEC 336). Reason: owner's ruling 11 (2026-11-01); a task started under the switch was accepted, suspended at once, and on release its person was told it had been stopped by the switch. Test: `new_work_is_refused_while_the_kill_switch_is_on` (kill_switch.rs; on the code before, the task is accepted with 201).

## Config tokens on this machine only (2026-11-01, `config-tokens-loopback`)

- The config's long-lived tokens (the owner's scripts, the evaluation runner, the test harness) are accepted only on the plain listener from a loopback address; over HTTPS, where the LAN and a tunnel reach the appliance, they are refused with 401 ("works only on the appliance itself"), and people sign in instead. Reason: owner's ruling 13 (2026-11-01); the tokens never expire, and they worked on every listener, so a copy taken from a script worked from the network. Test: `config_tokens_work_only_on_this_machines_plain_listener` (config_tokens.rs; on the code before, the token works over HTTPS).

## A person turned off in the identity provider (2026-11-01, `turned-off-schedules`)

- When the identity provider turns a person off or no longer knows them, their sessions end, each recorded in the log (`SignedOut`), and their schedules pause ("<provider> has turned your account off", with a notice), until they are turned back on and resume them. The check asks about everyone signed in or with a schedule, as the appliance starts and every five minutes (was ten, signed-in people only). Reason: owner's ruling 12 (2026-11-01); a person turned off kept their schedules running in their name, the check never asked about people not signed in, and the sessions it ended left no trace in the log (DEC 341). Their connector connections pause with them when connections become each person's own (connectors spec). Test: `a_person_turned_off_in_keycloak_loses_their_sessions_and_their_schedules_pause` (people.rs, on the real Keycloak, in CI).

## The admin's access to people's data (2026-11-01, `data-access-policy`)

- A person's tasks, conversations, files and memory are the organisation's by default: the owner reads them through `GET /v1/people/{id}/data/{tasks|chats|files|memory}`, each read recorded (`PersonDataRead`) and told to the person in their inbox ("alice read your tasks"). The owner can make people's data private (`PUT /v1/data-access {"private": true}`), and then reads only their own. Each person's own workspace stays theirs: the app's views show a person their own things, and the owner's reading is this separate, recorded path. Reason: owner's ruling 14 (2026-11-01: "In any org personal data is org data and no right to privacy. This should be policy configurable to allow private data."); it amends DEC 336, by which the admin saw no other person's tasks. The admin console will show it (admin.md). Test: `the_admin_reads_a_persons_data_unless_the_policy_makes_it_private` (data_access.rs).

## Recall only, behind per-person walls: the plan (2026-11-01, `data-walls-plan`)

- Magnus holds no one's content in memory; it recalls it (owner, 2026-11-01: "Magnus should not hold anyone s data in memory. It should be recall only."). This supersedes the part of DEC 20 that rebuilds content into memory at start: records (who, when, what kind, status, labels) stay a projection in memory, which decisions need under one lock; content (what people and sources wrote) is read from `public.content` per request, as the person it is for, through row-level security (ruling 9: `public.content` gains its person; a `{role}_person` role reads only the rows of `magnus.person`), and dropped when the request ends. Memory and library search move into Postgres, as the knowledge index already is. Reason: the owner's instruction; with everything in memory the walls between people were filters in code, and a wall in the database could not guard Magnus's own answers. DEC 20 was chosen for invariant 3, one consistent view for decisions, and simplicity; privacy was not weighed. Plan, steps and two questions for the owner (whether records stay in memory; the reply being written): `docs/proposals/recall-only.md`.

## Recall only, step 1: whose each row of content is (2026-11-01, `content-person`)

- Each row of the content store records the person it belongs to (`public.content.person`), written with it: the person the event names, else the person of its run, item or schedule (`State::content_owner`; a run started in the same write is its `TaskCreated`'s person). The organisation's own content (knowledge entries, the playbook) belongs to no one person and has none. `migrate` fills rows written before, and a restore fills the rows it puts back, from their events by the same rule (`log::CONTENT_OWNERS`). A new role, `{role}_person` (no login; the kernel's role is a member), reads `public.content` only where `person` is the `magnus.person` of its transaction, under row-level security, and cannot read the event log at all; the kernel's own role keeps reading every row, as replay needs. The policy is not forced on the table's owner (the administrator `migrate` runs as), which fills the column. A release installed without `migrate` since writes rows without the column, and says so. Reason: owner's ruling 9 and "recall only" (2026-11-01), step 1 of `docs/proposals/recall-only.md`; nothing reads through the role yet (step 2). Test: `each_persons_content_is_walled_from_everyone_else_in_the_database` (content_walls.rs; fails with the owner left out, and with the restore's fill left out).

## Recall only, step 2: the recall path (2026-11-01, `recall-path`)

- Content is recalled through `Recall` (`recall.rs`): a read for one person, by event seq, in a transaction as `{role}_person` with `magnus.person` set to them, so the database returns only rows that are theirs; content not theirs, or deleted, is simply absent. The owner's read of a person's memory (`GET /v1/people/{id}/data/memory`) is the first reader: the kernel keeps each memory's event seq as a record and recalls the fact and topic for that person. The other data reads (tasks, chats, files) move with their areas in steps 3 to 5, and the person's own views with them. Reason: "recall only" (owner, 2026-11-01), step 2 of `docs/proposals/recall-only.md`: a wrong person in code now gets nothing from the database instead of someone else's content. Test: `the_owner_reads_a_persons_memory_recalled_through_their_wall` (content_walls.rs; fails with the read taken from the kernel's memory).

## Recall only, step 4: memory is recalled (2026-11-01, `memory-recalled`)

- The kernel keeps a remembered fact's record (who, which run, its label and sources, when, its event) and not what it says: the fact and its topic are recalled for the person through their wall whenever a request needs them (`Kernel::remembered`): their memory view, the owner's read of it, `memory_search`, the memory pass's list of what is already remembered, a trashed memory's title, and the removal that finds where the fact was written. Memory search ranks the recalled facts in the kernel, as before, for the length of the request; a full-text index in Postgres is not needed at a person's memory's size. Reason: "recall only" (owner, 2026-11-01), step 4 of `docs/proposals/recall-only.md`. Done before step 3 because it builds directly on step 2's recall path. Test: `a_persons_memory_is_recalled_from_the_content_store_for_them` (content_walls.rs): a fact edited in the content store shows edited in her view, her search and the trash, and a row the database says is not hers is neither shown nor found. Step 2's test changed with it: carol's own view is no longer the kernel's (it said "unchanged until step 4").

## Each person's own connections (2026-11-01, `own-connections`)

- The organisation provides a connector and each person signs in to it with their own account (owner, 2026-11-01: "connectors are per person login. The connector itself is provided by the org."; connectors spec, Isolation: "A connection belongs to one user. No admin or other user can act through it."). The owner still adds, classifies and removes apps. A sign-in is stored under its person (`connector_<app>__<person>` in the vault), made and finished only by them (`POST /v1/connectors/{name}/sign-in`, `.../sign-in/complete`; a sign-in started by one person cannot be finished by another), and ended by them (`DELETE /v1/connectors/{name}/connection`, revoked at the app). A run uses only its person's sign-in: an app they have not signed in to is not offered to their runs, and a call to it is refused, naming the Library. Everyone can list the organisation's apps with whether they are signed in; only the owner gets the addresses, tools and classifications (through the owner's own sign-in). Removing an app, and the kill switch, revoke every person's sign-in. An app's key from the config file stays the organisation's. The one sign-in everyone's runs used before is no longer used by anyone: each person, the owner too, signs in again; it is revoked with its app or by the kill switch. Reason: the owner's instruction and the spec; until now one sign-in, the owner's, served every person's runs, so carol's task acted on alice's calendar. The app's Connectors page moves to the Library next (the spec's card and groups). Test: `the_owner_adds_an_app_each_person_signs_in_with_their_own_account_and_the_kill_switch_revokes_every_sign_in` (connectors.rs; it replaces the test in which carol's task used alice's sign-in, which was the bug).

## A sign-in waits for the provider to know the client Magnus just registered (2026-11-05, #170)

- After registering itself (RFC 7591), Magnus hands out the sign-in address only once the provider's sign-in page answers it with anything but 401 `invalid_client`. It asks every quarter second for ten seconds at most in all, however slowly the provider answers (a request started near the end used to get ten seconds of its own: review of #209), then hands the address out as it is. Redirects are not followed. Reason: on master's rerun of run 42105845733, gitlab.com answered the address built from a client it had just registered with 401 `invalid_client`, "The requested OAuth 2.0 Client does not exist". A person would have landed on that error, and `gitlab.rs` failed on it. In five timed trials gitlab.com knew a new client within half a second. Ten seconds is how long the person may wait for the sign-in page, not a figure GitLab publishes. Tests: `connectors.rs` `a_client_the_provider_does_not_know_yet_is_waited_for_before_the_person_signs_in`, against the stand-in OAuth app answering a new client's first sign-in request that way, and `a_sign_in_waits_for_a_new_client_ten_seconds_at_most` (unknown for seven seconds, then silent: 17 seconds before the fix).
- Not here: gitlab.com allows about ten registrations an hour from one address (the 429 half of #170). Magnus registering once per app and reusing the client is #208.
- #170's other half, a change to only PROGRESS.md or DECISIONS.md restarting the heavy suite, is already handled: `release/ci-select.sh` selects no tests for documents.

## Connectors in the Library, as the spec's cards (2026-11-01, `connectors-library`)

- Every person's Library lists the organisation's apps in the spec's groups (Connected, On this computer, Needs attention, Available; a group with nothing in it is not shown, and nothing is On this computer until local connectors exist), each as one card: its name, one plain sentence on what Magnus can do with it, and one button (Connect, Sign in again; Connected shows as a chip). The Library's search finds connectors as well as files. Connect opens the system browser at the app's own sign-in page; the card says to finish there and come back, keeps a button to open the page again, and turns Connected when the answer reaches this computer. A connector opens to as whom it is connected (from the sign-in's ID token where the app gives one; else "Your own account"), since when, when Magnus last used it, what it allows in plain words, the last things Magnus did through it for this person (from the event log, each a link to its task), and Disconnect, which ends the person's own sign-in at the app. No card shows a protocol word (OAuth, scope, token, MCP, port, an address). Owner reported (2026-11-01): "connectors does not match the spec in the app"; the spec's The connector card and Library. Reason for each choice: the sentence and the plain-words list come from the classes the organisation gave the app's tools, since an app added by its address has no signed manifest's listing (a catalog manifest's `summary` replaces it when the catalog exists); "Needs attention" is a sign-in the vault holds that no longer yields a token.
- The owner's side moves out of the person's way: Settings, Connectors is the organisation's apps (owner only): each with what Magnus can do with it, its tools and what each may do, its changed-tool decisions, and Remove for everyone; and "Add an app for everyone" with its address as the one field. The app names itself (its protected-resource metadata's `resource_name`, RFC 9728; else the part of its host that says something, as Google's servers all call themselves "StatelessServer" at initialize), and the form sends what it returns as private and possibly others' words and what reaches it as anyone, the most guarded, which the API still requires to be named (an app added without a label is refused, as before). Until the admin console exists, Settings is where the owner does this. Each person connects from their own Library, the owner too.
- Tests, against the real catalog (no fakes, below): `a_person_s_card_for_gmail_says_what_magnus_can_do_in_plain_words` and `the_library_shows_google_s_connectors_as_the_spec_s_cards_in_the_browser` (google.rs, with `desktop/tests/connectors.mjs`: the groups, the cards without protocol words, search, Connect outside the desktop app, the connector's page, the owner's registration and one field); `an_app_is_named_from_its_address` (unit). The installed-app test `app-apps.mjs` follows the new flow: the owner adds by address in Settings, connects from the Library card, then classifies in Settings. The connected card's parts (as whom, since when, recent actions, Disconnect) are tested with real calls in the next pull request.
- Not yet: the same card in chat, a task and approvals, and Magnus's own catalog tools to find connectors and place a card (next); the signed catalog, the relay, brokered and local connectors (they need Alpha Vector's catalog key and the relay deployed).

## Real connectors, from the catalog (2026-11-01, `catalog`)

- No fakes (owner, 2026-11-01: "No fakes. Test everything against real. Part of the build is bringing real connectors in catalog online and making them useable."): every connector, app and service test runs against the real service. The eight invariant tests keep their scripted, misbehaving model (owner's choice, "Keep scripted model"): they prove the boundary holds whatever the model does, which a real model cannot be made to do on cue. The fake OAuth app and the fake MCP apps leave the connector tests as real connectors come online (the next pull request replaces the rest with real calls). Supersedes, for connectors and apps, the early decision that every external system in the tests is a fake.
- Connectors are built on Alpha Vector's developer accounts with each provider, and each person signs in with their own account (owner, 2026-11-01: "Connectors should be built for developer org accounts and users have their own oauth sign in."). First in the catalog, by the owner's choice: the providers that let Magnus register itself, then Google Workspace, Microsoft 365 and Slack. Checked 2026-11-01: Notion and Linear register Magnus themselves; Sentry likely; Atlassian does but each customer's admin allows Magnus's domain; Asana, Slack, Google and Microsoft need Alpha Vector's own client; Vercel admits only clients it approved. The owner has a Google developer project for the Workspace MCP servers (a Desktop app OAuth client, with Gmail, Calendar, Drive and Chat enabled).
- The catalog (`catalog/*.toml`, `crates/magnus/src/catalog.rs`): one manifest per connector, compiled into the appliance so the release's signature covers it, until Alpha Vector's catalog key and signing service exist. A manifest names its server, how people sign in (`dcr`, or `preregistered` through a `provider`), exactly the access asked for, and the class of every tool, which counts as the owner's classification (ruling 5). First: Google's Gmail, Calendar, Drive and Chat (`gmailmcp`, `calendarmcp`, `drivemcp`, `chatmcp.googleapis.com/mcp/v1`), each naming exactly the tools its live server lists (read 2026-11-01; Gmail writes drafts, labels and the trash and cannot send; Drive cannot share), with the scopes those tools need from the ones the server's metadata offers. What reaches each, and what comes back, is set from what its tools can do: Gmail and Drive stay in the person's own account (private); Calendar and Chat reach attendees and members, who may be anyone (public); all four return the person's private data written by others.
- The organisation's registration with a provider (Alpha Vector's client there) is set by the owner (`PUT /v1/registrations/{provider}`, Settings, Connectors), kept in the vault (`registration_<provider>`), and recorded in the log only as set or cleared (`RegistrationSet`), never its client ID or secret, which no answer shows again. A preregistered connector is in no one's Library until its registration is set. Sign-in uses the registration's client (its secret at the token exchange), the manifest's scopes, PKCE, and, at Google, offline access, so a sign-in lasts. Until the relay exists, the appliance holds the registration; the relay takes it over (connectors spec).
- Tests, against Google: `google_s_connectors_come_online_with_the_organisation_s_registration_and_sign_in_at_google` (finds each live server's sign-in from the server, starts a person's sign-in at Google with the organisation's client, and checks Google answers with its sign-in page, not an error), `each_google_manifest_names_exactly_the_tools_its_live_server_lists` (tool drift), and the Library's two above. They take Alpha Vector's Google client ID from `catalog/registrations.toml`, and need no secret: none of them reaches the token exchange, the one step that uses it. `every_manifest_is_well_formed` (unit).
- Alpha Vector's client IDs with each provider are in `catalog/registrations.toml` (Google, Microsoft, GitHub, Vercel, Slack, Sentry, as the owner gave them, 2026-11-01). A client ID is public: every sign-in address a browser sees carries it. Client secrets are never in the repository. Reason: the owner will not add repository secrets ("I am not going to add this myself"), and a test that needs only the ID needs none.

## Recall only, step 5: library files are recalled (2026-11-01, `files-recalled`)

- The kernel keeps a library file's record (owner, name, type, size, label, where it came from, and the event that holds its content) and not its bytes or its search text: an upload's bytes (kept until now for the runs given them) and every text file's search text leave memory. A download, `project_read`, a search of the library or of everything (whose words are matched against the recalled text, for the length of the request), the files a task is given (its sandbox inputs and its model context's images and documents) are recalled for the file's person through their wall (`Kernel::file_bytes`, `file_texts`, `given_files`). The run's context is built after that recall, so building it can now fail, and a failure stops the run with its error as any other. A file's name stays in the record until step 3 settles the titles shown in lists. Reason: "recall only" (owner, 2026-11-01), step 5 of `docs/proposals/recall-only.md`. Test: `a_persons_files_are_recalled_from_the_content_store_for_them` (content_walls.rs): a file edited in the content store downloads, is found and is given to a task edited; a row the database says is not hers is neither downloaded nor found.

## Recall only, step 6: knowledge entries are read from the index (2026-11-01, `knowledge-recalled`)

- The kernel keeps a knowledge entry's record (its readers, integrity, author, run, endorsement, when, and the event that holds its content) and not what it says: its text, source with the citations' quotes, evidence and value leave memory. The kernel reads them from the content store only to index the entry (when it is added, endorsed, restored, or the index is behind at start), and every reader reads them from the index, as the reader role whose row security lets through only the entries the asker may read: the knowledge list and search, `knowledge_search`, the endorsement card, the value read from an entry's sources, and the answer to adding one. An entry whose content was deleted is taken out of the index, not indexed from memory. Its title stays in the record until step 3 settles the titles shown in lists. Reason: "recall only" (owner, 2026-11-01), step 6 of `docs/proposals/recall-only.md`. A knowledge entry is the organisation's, written to no one person (`content_owner`), so the person's wall cannot return it; the index's reader role is its wall, as the plan says ("the index already holds it"). Test: `a_knowledge_entry_s_text_is_read_from_the_content_store_not_kept` (knowledge.rs): an entry edited in the content store is found, shown and put on its endorsement card edited once indexed again; with its content deleted, no one finds it. It fails with the text taken from the kernel's memory.

## Recall only, step 3a: what a person asked is recalled (2026-11-01, `task-words-recalled`)

- Step 3 (chats and tasks) is split into reviewable pull requests: 3a what a person asked (a task's text, title and scope, and a schedule's), 3b chat conversations (the conversation a turn or task carries, branches, attachments), 3c a run's replies and tool results (the agent loop recalls its own run), 3d inbox cards, outputs, outcome views and spoken text. Reason: one step 3 would be several thousand lines, past what CLAUDE.md lets one pull request be.
- The kernel keeps a task's and a schedule's record (who, when, its run, chat origin, schedule, files, project; the event that holds its words) and not its words: the text the person wrote, the title the model gave it and the scope the person confirmed are recalled for its person through their wall (`Kernel::words`, `person_words`, `words_of`, `asked`) whenever a request needs them. That covers the task and schedule lists, a task's page, search, the library's and a project's views, where a file came from, the person's chat threads and messages, the trash's titles, the owner's read of a person's tasks, chats and files, notices' titles, the run's first message, the verifier, the memory pass, a task started from chat, a schedule's firing, and the developer views (as each run's person). A view reads only the fields it shows (`Recall::fields`: text, title, scope), never a task's attachments, once per request. A firing whose words cannot be recalled is a firing that failed, with its notice; a notice whose title cannot be recalled still goes, without it. Reason: "recall only" (owner, 2026-11-01), step 3 of `docs/proposals/recall-only.md`. Test: `a_persons_requests_are_recalled_from_the_content_store_for_them` (content_walls.rs): a task's, a schedule's and a chat message's words edited in the content store show edited in her task list, task page, schedules, chat thread and thread list, and not at all once the database says they are not hers; it fails with the words taken from the kernel's memory.

## Connectors are a catalog of apps, set up already (2026-11-01, `connectors-catalog`)

- Specs are for implementation (owner, 2026-11-01: "all specs are for implementation not proposal"): the Connectors, Admin Console and other specs are built as written, amended only by the owner's later rulings (`docs/proposals/spec-decisions.md`, among them no passkeys).
- Every catalog connector is in everyone's Library from the start, already set up; connecting is a click and a login on the provider's page (owner, 2026-11-01: "the connectors are suppose to offer a catalog of apps not forms"; connectors spec rev 46, "What the user sees"). The forms that asked the owner for a provider's client ID and secret, and the "Add an app for everyone" address field, are deleted from the app, not hidden or moved: adding a server by its address belongs to the admin console only. `GET` and `DELETE /v1/registrations` go. Alpha Vector's client IDs come with the catalog (`catalog/registrations.toml`, each with whether it takes a secret); a secret, where the provider takes one, is provisioned to the appliance by Alpha Vector's deployment (`PUT /v1/registrations/{provider}` with only `client_secret`, never typed by a person, never shown, logged only as provisioned). Until it is, the card says "Not ready on this appliance yet" instead of offering a sign-in that could not finish. Both are interim until the relay at `connect.pyrallon.ai` holds the secrets and hands out the client IDs (next; on Cloudflare Workers, the owner's choice), when `catalog/registrations.toml` and the provisioning call go.
- Tests: `google_s_connectors_are_in_every_library_already_set_up_and_sign_in_at_google` (google.rs); `desktop/tests/connectors.mjs` fails if the Library, the connect path or the owner's Connectors settings render a text field to type in (the spec's acceptance test, for the parts that exist; the full one, on a fresh install with Gmail, Slack, GitHub and Vercel, needs the relay and those manifests); `every_manifest_is_well_formed` checks each preregistered connector's client is in the catalog. The installed-app connector test adds its app through the API, as the admin console will.

## The admin console, part 1: its own process, reachable only from the organisation's network (2026-11-01, `admin-listener`)

- The admin console is built from the owner's "Magnus Admin Console Spec" as written (owner, 2026-11-01: "yes, build the admin console next its a full spec not a proposal"; "all specs are for implementation not proposal"), amended by the owner's rulings: no passkeys (ruling 1, "no passkeys", said again 2026-11-01), so admins sign in and step up with their password and their code; the user API keeps the controls the invariant tests drive until each moves with its test and the owner's approval (ruling 8); the console's kill switch is the app's (ruling 10); reading a person's data is a policy, each read recorded (ruling 14). `docs/proposals/admin.md` is marked superseded. Built in parts, each its own pull request: this listener and shell; sign-in (a confidential client in Keycloak, a server-side session, CSRF, step-up); the audit log and the root database role; the kernel's control socket, the kill switch, Overview and Health; Users; Security (sign-in rules, agent policy, credentials); Operations (modules, providers, updates, network, with ACME); Knowledge (ontology, context, memory, activity).
- `magnus-admin` is its own binary (`crates/magnus/src/admin`, `src/bin/magnus-admin.rs`), run as `magnus-admin --config magnus.toml` beside `magnus serve` and shipped with every appliance release (`release/appliance-programs`). Its `[admin]` section names its hostname, the appliance's addresses on the organisation's network to listen on, the LAN and VPN subnets it takes connections from, and the organisation's certificate. It refuses to start on every address (`0.0.0.0`, `::`) or loopback, or with an allowed subnet that takes in loopback; it drops a connection from outside the allowed subnets, or from loopback, before any TLS; it speaks TLS 1.3 only; a request naming any other host is not found (404); every answer carries a strict Content-Security-Policy (`default-src 'none'`, scripts, styles and images only from the console itself, never framed) and the other hardening headers. Reason: the spec's Access section, each layer holding if another is misconfigured. ACME (DNS-01) comes with Operations › Network; until then the organisation names its certificate.
- The shell: a top bar with the organisation's name and the appliance's status, the five sections in a sidebar (a bottom tab bar at phone width), the desktop app's components and themes (`app.css`, `theme.js`, served from the binary), and the sign-in until there is a session. Every API route but `/api/v1/health` answers 401 `sign_in_required` until sign-in exists. Nothing of the console is on the user API's listener.
- Tests: `the_console_answers_only_its_own_network_by_its_own_name_over_tls_1_3`, `an_address_outside_the_allowed_subnets_is_refused`, `the_console_will_not_listen_on_every_address_or_loopback_nor_take_loopback` (admin_reach.rs, the spec's Reach invariants, against the real process on this machine's network address); `a_subnet_takes_in_exactly_its_addresses`, `loopback_and_every_address_are_never_the_consoles`, `a_hostname_is_a_hostname` (unit).

## The admin console, part 2: signing in (2026-11-01, `admin-sign-in`)

- Admins sign in to the console on the organisation's identity provider's own page, with their password and their code when they use one, never a passkey (owner's rulings 1 and 2; "no passkeys", 2026-11-01). The console is the realm's confidential client `magnus-admin`: the authorization code flow with PKCE (S256), its one redirect `https://<hostname>/auth/callback`, and it proves itself with its own key (`private_key_jwt`, ES256), made on its first start and kept in the appliance's state (`admin/client-key.p8`, owner-only); `magnus-admin jwk` prints the public half, and `config/keycloak-client.py --admin-console` registers the client with it. A sign-in finishes only in the browser that started it (a short-lived `__Host-` cookie holds its state) and only once.
- The ID token is checked as the spec asks: its signature against the realm's published keys, ES256 or RS256 only (never `none`), its issuer, audience and authorized party, expiry and nonce; identity is `sub`. Only the realm role `admin` (read where the appliance reads realm roles) enters; anyone else is told only admins can enter. Reason: the spec's Authentication section; the appliance's own sign-in does not yet check signatures, and the console, as root, does.
- The session is the console's, server-side; the browser holds only `__Host-magnus_admin` (Secure, HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict, Path=/). It ends after 30 minutes unused or 12 hours (`idle_minutes`, `session_hours`). The provider is asked again at least every 4 minutes (`refresh_seconds`) with the session's refresh token; an admin turned off, no longer `admin`, or another account there ends the session at once, so access ends within 5 minutes as the spec asks. Every change carries the session's CSRF token (`x-csrf-token`). A member's app session or token opens no console route. Signing out ends the console's session and the provider's.
- Step-up: a fresh password (and code) on the provider's page (`/auth/step-up`, `prompt=login`, `max_age=0`), checked by its `auth_time`, counting for 5 minutes (`step_up_seconds`); signing in counts as one when the password was typed for it (`step-up-typed`). An action that needs it answers `401 step_up_required` without one, and the console's page goes to the provider and comes back. The sections' step-up actions call the same check (`Admin::step_up`); `POST /api/v1/session/step-up` answers whether one is current. The windows are tunables with the spec's values as defaults, so the tests can see them lapse.
- Tests (against the real test Keycloak, in Chromium, `desktop/tests/admin-sign-in.mjs`): `only_an_admin_signs_in_on_the_providers_page_and_the_session_holds_to_its_rules` (a member is refused; an app token opens nothing; CSRF; step-up and its lapse; sign-out; an admin turned off at the provider is out at the next check), `a_sign_in_finishes_only_in_the_browser_that_started_it_and_once` (admin_sign_in.rs); `the_consoles_own_signature_verifies_and_nothing_else_does`, `an_id_token_is_for_this_client_this_realm_this_sign_in_and_current` (unit). The reach tests move their console helpers to `tests/scratch/src/admin.rs`.

## The admin console, part 3: the audit log and the root role (2026-11-01, `admin-audit`)

- Every console action is written to the event log before it answers (admin console spec, Authority and audit): who (the admin's `sub` and name), what, to what, from which address, and for a change its before and after, as an `AdminAction` event. The kernel writes it, at the console's request over the kernel's control socket (`crates/magnus/src/control.rs`: a Unix socket in the appliance's state, mode 0600, never on the network), because the kernel is the log's one writer and checks it has no gaps (DEC 20); a second process inserting into the log would break that. An action whose entry cannot be written does not happen: a sign-in is refused, a sign-out answers 503. Recorded so far: sign-ins, sign-ins refused (not an admin), step-ups, sign-outs, and sessions the provider ended; each section's actions add theirs. The state keeps the audit, rebuilt from the log at start.
- The Audit screen (Security › Audit) is the log as a table, filtered by actor, action, target and time, with CSV export (`GET /api/v1/audit`, `/api/v1/audit.csv`; a cell a spreadsheet would run as a formula is kept as text).
- The console's own database role (`admin.database_url`, `magnus migrate` creates it): it reads past the walls between people (BYPASSRLS), every person's content and the knowledge index, and the log, to which it may add but which it can never update, delete from or truncate; nor can it change or delete content. No other process holds it. Reason: the spec's "Only the magnus-admin process holds the Postgres role that bypasses row-level security", and its invariant that it cannot update or delete an audit entry. The Data tab (Users) reads through it, each read audited.
- Tests: `the_consoles_root_role_reads_past_every_wall_and_can_never_change_the_log`, `every_console_action_is_written_to_the_log_by_the_kernel_and_kept` (admin_audit.rs, against Postgres and the kernel's socket); the sign-in test checks each of its actions is in the audit with its source address, filtered, and in the CSV.

## The admin console, part 4: the kill switch, Overview and Health (2026-11-01, `admin-ops`)

- The console's kill switch is the app's own (owner's ruling 10; admin console spec, Kill switch): its logic moves, unchanged, from the user API's handler into the kernel (`Kernel::kill_switch`), which both the handler and the kernel's control socket call (`POST /v1/kill-switch` with who moves it). Engaged, it seals the vault, revokes every sign-in at its provider, suspends every running run, refuses new work and holds schedules, as before; the switch's state, kept in the log, outlives a restart. The state now keeps who last moved it and when.
- In the console: a red "Stop all agents" in the top bar, pressed and held for a second (pointer, or Space or Enter held), engages it with no step-up; while it is on, a red banner on every screen says who engaged it and when, with Release, which needs a fresh password (`401 step_up_required` without one) and is recorded before it acts. Engaging is recorded after the switch, so the switch works even when nothing else can be written. Operations shows the switch (on or off, who and when, tasks halted, schedules held, sign-ins revoked) and Health (the kernel, the event log, the identity provider and the console, each answering or not; the machine's load, memory and disk; tasks running; recent errors). Overview shows the status strip, the cards (tasks running and waiting, people signed in, knowledge and memory changes by agents in the last day) and alerts (the kernel, the log or the identity provider not answering, the disk over 85%), each naming where it is fixed. Certificate expiry and the tunnel come with Operations › Network; live streaming over server-sent events comes with the sections that need it (the shell polls every 5 seconds meanwhile).
- `GET /api/v1/health` is Health, an admin's alone; the shell's status dot before sign-in reads `/status`, which says only that the console answers (not even its version).
- Tests: `the_kill_switch_through_the_kernel_is_the_apps_own_and_outlives_a_restart` (admin_audit.rs, through the kernel's socket); `the_console_stops_all_agents_at_once_and_releases_only_with_a_fresh_password` (admin_sign_in.rs, against the real Keycloak); the reach test checks Health, Overview and the switch answer no one signed out. The kill switch's own tests pass unchanged.

## The admin console, part 5a: Users, their roles and their sessions (2026-11-02, `admin-users`)

- The console manages the realm's people through its own service account, `magnus-admin-svc` (admin console spec, Server and API): a confidential client proving itself with the console's own key (`private_key_jwt`, ES256, the same key as the sign-in client: one process holds both, so a second key would protect nothing), holding only `manage-users`, `view-users`, `query-users` and `view-realm` in the console's realm. It is called only after the admin's session has passed the guard, and the step-up where one is needed. `config/keycloak-client.py --admin-console` registers it with the console's client.
- Users lists everyone in the realm with their role (admin is the realm role `admin` held directly), status (disabled when turned off at the provider; invited while the account still waits for its first password, Keycloak's `UPDATE_PASSWORD` required action; else active), last sign-in on the appliance and sessions open there. The kernel names people by their own id and binds each to the provider's `sub` (owner's ruling 3); its control socket answers for people by `sub` (`GET /v1/people`, `GET`/`DELETE /v1/people/{sub}/sessions[/{id}]`, `POST /v1/people/{sub}/turn-off`).
- Invite: an email, a name and a role. The provider makes the account, waiting for its password, and emails the link to choose it, valid three days (owner's ruling 1 and "no passkeys": a password, and a code if they use one, instead of the spec's passkey enrollment). If the email cannot be sent the account stays and the console says so; Resend tries again. Cancel removes an invitation nobody has taken up; an account in use goes only by Delete (part 5b). Inviting an admin needs a fresh password, as a role change does: it grants the same thing.
- A role change needs a fresh password. The last admin turned on can be neither demoted, turned off, nor have their invitation cancelled (`409 last_admin`). Admins by group membership are not counted, so the check can only err towards refusing.
- Turning someone off turns them off at the provider, ends their sessions there and every session on the appliance, and pauses their schedules, exactly as when the provider turns someone off (`Kernel::turn_off`, which the five-minute check now calls too). Turned back on, their schedules stay paused until they resume them. End all sessions ends them at the provider and on the appliance; a single session ends on the appliance.
- Every change is written to the audit log before it is made, and a change that then fails is recorded as failed. Ids that reach the kernel's paths are letters, digits, `-` and `_` only.
- Not in this part (5b): the Schedules and triggers tab with Suspend and Resume, the Data tab (root reads of a person's private data, each recorded), and Delete with the person's key destroyed.
- Tests, against the real Keycloak with a real SMTP server receiving the provider's real email. The build box lets no container reach the host (CI: nothing reached a server on its LAN address or the Docker gateway), so the server (`mail-sink`, `tests/scratch/src/bin/mail-sink.rs`, the standard library only) runs in a container of its own sharing the test Keycloak's network, on the Keycloak image already on the box, and writes each message to a directory the test reads (`tests/scratch/src/mail.rs`): `an_admin_invites_someone_who_chooses_their_password_from_the_providers_email` and `the_last_admin_stays_and_a_person_turned_off_loses_every_session_and_schedule` (admin_users.rs). Each also checks its screen in light, dark and glass, and at phone width with no sideways scroll (`MAGNUS_VISIT` in `desktop/tests/admin-sign-in.mjs`). The reach test checks every Users route refuses anyone not signed in.

## The Library is the connector catalog, as the owner's mockup draws it (2026-11-02, `library-catalog`)

- The Library is the connector catalog, a page of its own (`#/library`), laid out as the owner's Magnus Connector Catalog mockup draws it in every look (connectors spec rev 48, Library): a heading with Close; Yours and Discover; one search box taking a service's name or what the person wants done (it searches names, sentences, publishers and abilities); category chips (All, Email & chat, Files, Code, Work, Design & CAD); and sections of cards. Discover: On this computer, Needs attention, Popular. Yours: Connected, Needs attention, Ready to enable. A section with nothing in it is not shown; nothing matching says so. Reason: the owner, 2026-11-02: "I don't see the catalog change"; #67 had removed the forms but kept the old list.
- A card: the connector's tile, its name, the review mark ("Reviewed by Alpha Vector", catalog connectors only), one plain sentence, who makes it ("by Google"; "Added by your organisation" for one an admin added by address), and one action at its corner: a plus to connect, a check when connected (opening its page), a spinner while the person finishes on the provider's page (with "Open it again"), sign in again when it needs attention, Enable for a program on this computer. A connector's page (`#/library/<id>`, and in the side panel from a card in chat or a task) has the mockup's two states: not connected, what Magnus can do with it, what it asks for (each scope as a plain line), the "You sign in on Google's own page…" note and Connect; connected, the account, what it can and cannot do, when connected and last used, recent actions and Disconnect.
- Catalog manifests carry what the Library shows (spec, Catalog and connector manifests): `publisher`, `category`, `abilities`, `asks` (one plain line per scope, in order), `can`, `cannot`; a unit test checks each is there, that `asks` matches `scopes`, and that none says a protocol word. `asks` says what the provider's grant allows, honestly ("Write drafts and send email"); `can` and `cannot` say what Magnus's tools for it do ("cannot send email"). `letters` and `tint` draw each tile until the provider's own mark is in its listing (the spec's icons are the providers' marks, used as their brand guidelines allow; none is in the repository yet).
- Files, which shared the old Library, have their own entry, Files, beside Tasks and Schedules, opening in the side panel as before ("Saved to Files", "Save to Files"). Reason: the spec's Library is the catalog alone, and files still need their place.
- Only the four Google connectors are in the catalog; Slack, GitHub, Vercel and the rest join as their manifests and registrations are added, and programs on this computer as the local bridge is built. Their sections appear then, with nothing else to change.
- Tests: `desktop/tests/connectors.mjs` (run by `the_library_shows_google_s_connectors_as_the_spec_s_cards_in_the_browser`): the catalog under Popular with each card's mark, publisher and plus; Yours empty; search by name and by what is wanted; the chips; light, dark and glass at the window's width and at phone width, with no sideways scroll; connecting asks nothing to be typed; a connector's page says what Magnus can do and what it asks for, with no Disconnect before connecting. The files tests now go through Files.

## Behavior: tasks and schedules under control in conversation, and composed replies (2026-11-02, `behavior-controls`)

- Owner's rulings on the updated behavior spec (2026-11-02): the behavior spec comes before the rest of the admin console; and people may grant standing authority over their own work within limits (only actions they could approve themselves, inside the organisation's policy), with invariant 6 reworded and its test updated in the step that builds grants. Putting the admin console on strix-alpha (its service, configuration, provider clients and certificate) is part of the admin spec's work, decided from the spec, not asked of the owner. `docs/proposals/behavior.md` orders the updated spec into steps.
- Magnus controls tasks and schedules in a conversation (behavior spec, Tasks and schedules in conversation): `list_tasks`, `list_schedules`, `cancel_task`, `pause_schedule`, `resume_schedule`, `edit_schedule`, `cancel_schedule`, chat tools like `create_task` (allowed in a chat turn, never in a task). They and the app's buttons call the same kernel operations (`controls.rs`), so the two cannot disagree. A person controls their own; the owner and admins control anyone's (the spec: "users control their own, admins control others'"); anyone else is told "no such task" or "no such schedule", so its existence is not told.
- Cancelling a task stops its run, records the cancellation, and answers with what it had already done, the changes that cannot be undone (irreversible or financial actions that went through) first, for Magnus to tell the person. Cancelling a schedule also stops a run of it still going; pausing stops future runs only ("Paused by you", or by name when the owner or an admin did it).
- Editing a schedule changes when it runs (every N minutes, or a daily time) and when it ends (until, max runs), as a new event `ScheduleChanged`; what each run does stays (its words are recalled from where they were set: to change them, cancel it and set up another). The app gets the same: `POST /v1/schedules/{id}/pause`, `PATCH /v1/schedules/{id}`.
- A reply is composed, not routed (spec, Reply composition): chat's instructions no longer sort a message into answer, task, schedule or question; one reply can answer, start a task, propose a schedule, act on tasks and schedules, or ask, together, and Magnus works out which task "cancel that" means, asking only when it is genuinely unclear.
- Tests: `magnus_cancels_a_task_in_conversation_and_says_what_it_had_done`, `magnus_pauses_resumes_edits_and_cancels_a_schedule_in_conversation` and `cancelling_a_schedule_stops_its_run_in_flight` (chat_controls.rs). `a_schedule_ends_after_its_runs_at_its_time_or_by_its_own_run` (schedules.rs) deleted the kettle's schedule while its run waited and then watched that run resume; under the spec a cancel stops that run, so the test now pauses the schedule instead, which keeps what it checks (no more firings; the wait's end told in the same write that resumes the run): Magnus lists, cancels, pauses, resumes, edits and cancels through the model's tool calls; another person is refused and changes nothing; the owner controls someone else's; an ended task is not cancelled twice; an impossible time is refused; the app's buttons are the same operations.

## Behavior: tasks and schedules as live cards in their conversation, and schedules' own threads (2026-11-02, `behavior-cards`)

- A task's card in its conversation keeps its live status and has Cancel while it runs or waits; a confirmed schedule's card shows its next run and latest result (from the schedule itself, never a copy), Paused or Scheduled, and Pause or Resume, Cancel and Open; a cancelled schedule's card says it no longer runs (behavior spec, Tasks and schedules in conversation: "Cards render from the object's current state. Buttons are direct UI actions that don't go through the model"). The buttons call the operations #74 gave the app and Magnus alike.
- A task's result that arrives after the person has moved on (a message of theirs since the task started) quotes what they asked ("You asked: …") above it.
- Each schedule run posts to the schedule's own thread (`schedule-<id>`): its run starts with that thread as its conversation, so the thread lists each run and its result like any other, is in the person's thread list under what the schedule does, and takes a reply; the conversation the schedule was set up in keeps only its card, which shows the latest run and links to "Its runs". Reason: the spec, "so recurring output doesn't bury the conversation".
