- Tests: `each_schedule_run_posts_to_the_schedules_own_thread` (schedule_threads.rs: the run's result is in the schedule's thread, which is listed, takes a reply and is nobody else's; the conversation it came from gets none of it); `tasks_and_schedules_are_live_cards_in_their_conversation` (live_cards.rs, with `desktop/tests/live-cards.mjs` in a real browser): the moved-on result quotes the request; the schedule's card shows its next run and pauses and resumes from the card; the memo's Cancel cancels it and is gone once it has; the schedule's Cancel cancels it and the card says so; the appliance agrees afterwards.
## Org context, step 1: the bundle (2026-11-02, `org-context`)

- The owner's "Magnus org context build spec" replaces the memory spec (2026-11-02). Rulings the same day: erasure stays the content store (no per-user keys for the log; a removed person's content rows are deleted); relationship memory enters a person's runs by code, the behavior spec being the later revision, the one exception to "three things enter a run by code"; org context comes before behavior steps 3-7. `docs/proposals/org-context.md` orders the spec into eleven steps.
- The bundle is OKF v0.2 (as published, https://github.com/GoogleCloudPlatform/knowledge-catalog/blob/main/okf/SPEC.md) with the spec's Magnus profile. A document is an event, `BundleWritten` (path, version, kind, scope, owner, the OKF actor, the run, its taint, its source resources), whose frontmatter and body are content: in the content store, nobody's row for an org document and its person's for theirs, so the walls and deletion apply as to everything else. The kernel's state keeps where each document is, whose, its versions and sources; it reads documents through its own role and decides who sees them. Reason: the spec's tables are what Magnus's projection (DEC 20) and content store already are; a second copy would be a second truth.
- Every write is checked (K8) and refused with the reason when it breaks a rule: the path's place sets the kind (`/sources/`, `/types/`, `/entities/<type>/`, `/conventions/<area>/`, `/people/`, `/users/<user>/`, `index.md`, `log.md`); each kind's required fields and OKF `type`; scope follows the path (org, or user with its owner); OKF actors (`human:<id>`, `process:<id>`, `<producer>/<version>`); ISO 8601 times with their offset; statuses; verifications {by, at, kind}; sources and links are bundle paths or source URIs (relative links are not the profile's); an entity's identity keys and relations are those its type declares; indexes are headings and bulleted links with no frontmatter (the root may carry `okf_version: "0.2"`), logs dated sections newest first; `/index.md` at most 16KB (K9). Every write is a new version, kept. A type becomes stable only with an admin's approval (the console's, step 10): Magnus writes types as draft.
- Visibility (K2): a person's own documents are theirs alone, and no one writes into another's. An org document drawn only from the bundle or from people's answers is everyone's; one drawn from items in the org's systems is seen, until the kernel asks each source (K3, step 4), only by the person whose run wrote it, under whose grant the items were read. A read removes links to what the reader cannot see, and an index line whose links are all hidden goes; a search returns only what they can see. Reads carry the taint of the run that wrote the document (M1).
- Tools: `bundle_read`, `bundle_search` (every word, over path, title, description and body) and `bundle_write` (tasks only; a chat turn reads). YAML is written with `serde_yaml_ng`, a maintained fork of the deprecated `serde_yaml`.
- Tests: the validator's unit tests in `bundle.rs` (every place and its kind; T9's malformed documents: no type, a bad time, an identity key or relation the type does not declare, a relative link, the wrong scope; indexes, logs and T10's cap; actors); `magnus_writes_the_bundle_through_the_kernel_which_checks_every_write_and_guards_who_sees_what` (org_context_bundle.rs, through real tasks).
- The console's step-up tests use a 30-second window (were 8 and 4) and wait past it to see it lapse. Reason: CI on #76 failed both (`stepped_up_until` null right after a step-up; release refused right after a fresh sign-in): a window counts from the password typed on Keycloak's page, and a real browser's sign-in on the loaded build box takes longer than 4-8 seconds to come back. Nothing in the console changed.

## The admin console on strix-alpha (2026-11-02, `admin-on-appliance`)

- The owner (2026-11-02): "I don't have admin dashboard so I can't even see what is built or should be available", then "yes, do the admin console and connectors first". The console's program shipped with every release, but nothing configured or started it on the appliance. The org context queue waits behind this and the connectors.
- `release/admin-on-appliance.sh`, run by CI after each master build is on the build box's appliance, safe to run again. It decides once, from the spec and the machine, what the spec leaves to the organisation (the owner, 2026-11-02, chose the local name):
  - hostname: `<machine>.local` (mDNS), port 7443;
  - listen: the machine's address on the network its default route uses (strix-alpha: 192.168.0.50) and its Tailscale address, never every address or loopback;
  - allow: those networks only (the LAN's /24 and Tailscale's 100.64.0.0/10);
  - certificate: from an organisation CA made on the appliance (its key owner-only beside the config), naming the hostname and those addresses, which the owner trusts once on their computers;
  - the console's database role: a password made there, kept in the owner-only config.
- Each run, the script also:
  - migrates, which creates or updates that role;
  - registers the console's client and service account in the realm with the console's own key, as root (the CI user has sudo there and the realm administrator's file is root-only);
  - gives the appliance's owners the realm role `admin` (`keycloak-client.py --assign-admin`, new);
  - installs the console as a systemd user service beside the appliance, on the installed programs, restarted so it runs the release;
  - turns mDNS on where it is installed.
- It prints the console's address, a hosts-file line for computers where `.local` does not resolve, and the CA's certificate (public) with its fingerprint. It prints no secret.
- The same CI step carries Google's client secret, when the repository's `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` is set, into the appliance's vault through its owner-only `PUT /v1/registrations/google`. Nothing else holds it until the relay does.
- Test: `the_setup_writes_an_admin_console_that_answers_its_hostname_on_the_network_only` (admin_on_appliance.rs). From a config with no `[admin]`, the console starts on what the script wrote, answers its hostname over TLS the new CA verifies, refuses another hostname, keeps its keys owner-only, and a second run writes nothing more. The realm and systemd parts run on the build box only.

## A schedule is approved once, then runs on its own (2026-11-02, `schedule-approved-once`)

- Owner, 2026-11-02: "When schedules are created they create a new task. Each task must be approved which is not autonomous. The schedule should be approved once and run until completion or it's stopped." Each run of a schedule is a new task, and every approval was per task, so each run asked again: before any action the owner's policy asks about, and for review at the end when a criterion had no executable check.
- Confirming a schedule now covers the policy's asks in all its runs, for actions its person could approve themselves (the owner; a holder of `approver:<risk>`; a reversible change, anyone), except moving money. The first standing authority under the owner's ruling (people grant within limits, the same day): the confirmation is the grant, scoped to that schedule's runs. A person who could not approve an action is still asked, as for any task of theirs.
- The kernel's own asks stay per action (owner's ruling, the same day: "Keep asking each time"): a run that has read untrusted content and private data asks before each thing it sends out (the Rule of Two, invariant 4), and private data bound for a wider audience asks (the audience rule).
- Every run a schedule starts has the verifier on (owner's ruling, the same day): a second model judges the criteria no code checks, and only what it cannot judge goes to the person for review. Invariant 7 holds unchanged.
- Test: `a_confirmed_schedule_runs_without_asking_again_within_its_persons_authority` (schedule_approved_once.rs): Bob's schedule sends an irreversible email and completes with nothing asked of him; Carol's, which she could not approve, asks; a one-off task of Bob's still asks. Invariants 4, 6 and 7 pass unchanged.
- `a_scheduled_run_cannot_set_up_a_schedule_and_its_schedule_waits_for_it` (schedules.rs) made the owner's scheduled run wait on an approval to send; a confirmed schedule's send no longer asks her, so the run now waits on a question to her, which keeps what the test checks: a schedule pauses while its run waits on its person, and goes on once answered.

## The admin console answers every name it is reached by (2026-11-02, `admin-names`)

- The owner (2026-11-02): "There is never a time that strix-alpha.local will be reachable"; Zero reaches strix-alpha over the LAN, Tailscale and pyrallon.ai ("all three"). `.local` needs mDNS, which strix-alpha does not run, and the console answered only that one name, so opening it by its address gave "not found".
- The console now answers its hostname and `admin.aliases`. On strix-alpha:
  - the hostname is the machine's Tailscale name when the `tailscale` command reports one (it resolves wherever the owner's computers are), else its LAN address;
  - the aliases are the LAN address (192.168.0.50) and the Tailscale address (100.92.130.94).
- The certificate names every one of them, and the realm accepts a sign-in coming back to any of them (`keycloak-client.py --admin-hostname`, now repeatable).
- The setup script now keeps its own `[admin]` section each run (`release/admin-section.py`): names, listen addresses, networks and certificate follow the machine, and the database password is kept. A section someone else wrote is left alone.
- Not through pyrallon.ai: the console spec keeps it to the organisation's network and never behind the tunnel, which is why it refuses loopback. Tailscale reaches it from anywhere the owner is.
- Test: `the_setup_writes_an_admin_console_that_answers_its_hostname_on_the_network_only` (admin_on_appliance.rs), extended:
  - a section with one name, as the first run on strix-alpha wrote it, is brought up to every name and keeps its password;
  - the console answers its Tailscale name and its address, both over TLS the CA verifies.


## The admin console's shell: the patterns every screen uses (2026-11-02, `admin-shell`)

- Owner, 2026-11-02: "do not build min slices and give up before the job is done … You build a spec completely not part of it and call that success." The console is built to its spec in full. `docs/admin-console.md` checks every line of the spec, each with the pull request and the test that proves it; the console is reported done only when every line is checked.
- The spec's shell patterns, in `desktop/admin/admin.js` (`UI`), so each section after this uses them and none is built without them:
  - lists open a detail panel on the right, full screen at phone width; Escape or × closes it and goes back to the list;
  - a reversible change shows a toast with Undo for ten seconds;
  - a destructive one opens a confirm sheet, which can ask for the target's name typed;
  - a step-up opens the provider's sign-in in its own small window, and the action is sent again once the session holds the fresh password. The window is followed through the session, not its handle, because the console's `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin` cuts the link when it goes to the provider. Where windows are blocked, the whole page goes and comes back, as before;
  - empty, loading and error states drawn the same everywhere, each error with Try again.
- Live data: `GET /api/v1/events`, server-sent events. The console looks every two seconds and sends `status` (the kernel and log up, the cards, the kill switch) when it changes. A stream lasts no longer than the provider check (`refresh_seconds`) and ends with its session, so an admin turned off at the provider loses it as they lose every route; the browser reconnects through the guard. The top bar and Overview are drawn from it; the five-second kill switch poll is gone.
- The admin's menu (spec, Shell: "the signed-in admin's menu (theme, sign out)"): light, dark or match computer, and solid or glass, the desktop app's own `Theme`.
- Overview: each certificate the appliance serves (the console's, and the appliance's own when it has one) with when it ends, and an alert from 14 days before, linking to Operations › Network. The knowledge card opens Knowledge › Activity.
- The console now serves the app's fonts (Inter, IBM Plex Mono) at `/assets/fonts/`: its stylesheet asked for them and got 404, so it was not the app's look.
- Colours that were wrong in dark: the kill switch's button and banner, and danger buttons, were the dark theme's light red with white text (2.1:1). They are now a fixed strong red with white text (6:1) in every look. Status pills are the app's chips.
- Test: `the_consoles_screens_share_a_live_stream_a_step_up_sheet_panels_sheets_and_undo` (admin_shell.rs), in Chromium against Keycloak: the menu sets the theme; the kill switch's banner arrives over the stream with no reload; Release asks in the sheet, the provider's window takes the password, and the switch is released; a row opens its panel and Escape closes it; a cancelled sheet ends nothing; Turn off's Undo turns the person back on; each is in the audit log. The stream is refused without a session, opens with `status`, and ends at sign-out. Overview names the console's certificate. `the_end_of_a_certificates_validity_is_read_from_its_pem` (admin/cert.rs).

## The admin console's Security › Connectors and credentials (2026-11-02, `admin-connectors`)

- The organisation's apps in the console (admin console spec, Security › Connectors and credentials): each with its status (ready, needs its credential, tools not reviewed, off), how people sign in (each person's own account, the organisation's key, none), its scopes, its credential (only whether it is set, and when), how many people are signed in, and when anyone last used it. Spec decision 18 proposed leaving these out; the owner ruled the specs are built as written, so they are in.
- Its actions: add an app by its address; turn one off or on for the organisation; set who may use it (groups; empty is everyone); set, rotate or revoke the organisation's credential; remove an app that was added. A catalog app is turned off, not removed; a config-file app is changed in the file.
- The organisation's credential for an app is its key, or, for an app people sign in to through a provider whose client takes a secret (Google), that provider's client secret. Setting it on one Google app sets it for every Google app, and revoking it ends every person's sign-in to each of them, at the provider. An app people sign in to with their own accounts and no client secret holds nothing for the organisation; revoking there ends every person's sign-in.
- Google's client secret can now be set here by an admin, as well as by the CI step from the repository's secret. Either way it is written to the vault, the log records only that it was set, and nothing shows it again.
- An app turned off is filtered out of `Kernel::connectors()`, so it leaves the Library and the boundary's facts: no one is offered it and no task can reach it. People's sign-ins stay until revoked. Events: `ConnectorSettings` (on or off, who may use it) and `CredentialSet` (an app's key set or revoked; never the value).
- Add, remove and the registration secret were the app's owner routes; they are now the kernel's own operations (`add_connector`, `remove_connector`, `set_registration`, ...), which the app's routes and the console's (through the control socket) both call.
- Every change is audited before it is made, with before and after, and a credential appears in neither: only `{ kind, set }`.
- The console passes on the kernel's refusals with their status (409, 400, 404), where before any kernel refusal showed as 502.
- Test: `the_console_sets_rotates_and_revokes_the_organisations_credentials_and_never_shows_one` (admin_connectors.rs), against Keycloak:
- An app's tool is never classed `sandbox`: the owner's classification refuses it, and one an older log classed so counts as unclassified, so it is not offered (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: `sandbox` means the appliance's own microVM and does not change state, so an app's tool classed so skipped the task contract, the Rule of Two and the audience rule, and the default policy ran it without asking, while it acted at the app. Test: `an_apps_tool_is_never_classed_as_the_sandbox` (connectors.rs).
  - Google's client secret set from the console serves Gmail and Drive, and the app's Library sees Google's client as complete;
  - an app added by its address with a key; who may use it set;
  - turned off, Gmail leaves the Library, and comes back when turned on;
  - the key rotated with a new time;
  - every admin route that reads (each person's page, the audit and its CSV, the live stream included) holds none of the three credential values (the spec's invariant "No admin endpoint ever returns a credential value");
  - the key and Google's secret revoked; the added app removed, a catalog app refused;
  - each change in the audit log, the rotation recorded as `{ kind: key, set: true }`;
  - the screen in every look and at phone width.

## The admin console's Users › Schedules, Data and Delete (2026-11-02, `admin-users-detail`)

- **Schedules** (admin console spec: "Schedules and triggers: each one, with Suspend and Resume"): a person's schedules with what each does, its rule, next run, runs and failures, and whether it is suspended and why. An admin suspends one ("Suspended by <admin>, an admin", which the person sees in their own list) or resumes it; a schedule whose run waits on its person goes on when they answer. Magnus starts work on request or on a schedule and has no other triggers, so there are only schedules to show.
- **Data** (owner's ruling 14): a person's tasks, conversations, files and memory, each list or one item (a task with its steps, a conversation's messages, a file's details and text, a memory), recalled as them, through their wall. Nothing is read until the admin chooses what to open. Each read is in the console's audit log before it is made (the admin, the person, the resource, the item, the time), in the kernel's log (`PersonDataRead`, now with the item), and in the person's inbox ("<admin> read your files", "opened one of your files"). While the organisation's policy keeps people's data private, the console reads none of it. Reading moved into the kernel (`read_person_data`), which the app's owner route and the console both call.
- **Delete**: needs a fresh password and the person's email typed, and is refused for oneself and for the last admin. The kernel, as the admin:
  - stops their runs and ends their schedules;
  - ends their sessions;
  - revokes their own sign-ins to apps, at each app;
  - records their content as removed (`Removed`, so a deletion cut short finishes at the next start), deletes it from the content store, and takes it out of the backups (as removing from the trash does);
  - drops their records from the appliance's state (`PersonErased`: tasks, runs, inbox, schedules, memory, files, projects, trash, preferences, binding).
  Then their account is deleted at the provider. The log keeps that they existed and that root deleted them.
- The spec says deletion "destroys that user's encryption key". Magnus has no key per person: what people write is in the content store, walled per person in the database (owner's ruling 9), and is searched there. So deletion removes the content itself, from the store and from every backup, which leaves it as unrecoverable as destroying a key would. Per-person keys would mean Postgres could no longer search what people wrote; that would be the owner's call.
- Test: `root_suspends_a_persons_schedule_reads_their_data_on_the_record_and_deletes_them_for_good` (admin_people.rs), against Keycloak:
  - Bob's schedule is suspended (his own list says an admin did) and resumed;
  - his files are listed and one file's words read;
  - each read is in the audit log with admin, person, resource and time (the spec's invariant), and Bob is told;
  - with people's data private, the console reads none;
  - Delete is refused without a fresh password and with the wrong email typed;
  - once deleted, his account, session, schedule and every row of his content are gone, and the deletion is in the audit log;
  - the screens in every look and at phone width.

## The admin console's Security › Sign-in and Agent policy (2026-11-02, `admin-security`)

- **Sign-in** (admin console spec; owner's ruling 1, no passkeys):
  - How members sign in: their password, and a code from an authenticator app that is optional (whoever set one up is asked) or required (everyone sets one up and is asked). Required sets the code step of the realm's browser and direct-grant flows from CONDITIONAL to REQUIRED, so a password alone signs no one in; optional sets it back.
  - How admins sign in: shown as fixed, the password and their code as members.
  - Session lifetimes: members' (the realm's idle and longest) and the console's own (idle 5-240 minutes, longest 1-24 hours, kept in the log as `ConsoleSettings` and taken by the console at start).
  - Lockout: on or off, failures before it, the first and longest waits, and whether it is for good.
  - Every change needs a fresh password and is audited with before and after. The console's service account gains `manage-realm` in its realm (still that realm's management only, as the spec allows).
- **Agent policy**:
  - Each Cedar policy set (the organisation's, a group's, a person's) is shown with its live text (the organisation's default when none was written) and an admin's draft. A draft (`PolicyDrafted`) changes nothing in force.
  - Magnus now has a Cedar schema (`policy_admin::SCHEMA`: people in groups, tools in apps with risk and audience, the two actions, the run's taint as context). Every draft and every apply is checked against it, and each problem is given with its line and column (Cedar's diagnostics, via `miette`). The editor highlights the text and marks the lines with problems as it is typed.
  - The test panel tries a principal, action, resource (a tool, with its risk as classified or given) and context against what is live, or with the draft in place. It shows the decision (acts without asking, asks first, refused), the rule that decided it, and the layers it went through.
  - A diff against the live text, and Apply, which needs a fresh password, accepts only a policy that fits the schema, and drops the draft.
  - Every version is listed; rolling back puts an earlier one in force again as a new version, also with a fresh password. Applies and rollbacks are audited with the text before and after.
- In the console, sets are named `org`, `group:<name>` and `user:<person>`, so each fits one path segment.
- Layout fixes found on the way:
  - the app's stylesheet fixes `body` at the window's height, which cut the console's main area short on long screens;
  - form rules made checkboxes full width;
  - admins named in the log by their provider id are now shown by name.
- Test: `root_sets_the_sign_in_rules_and_edits_tests_applies_and_rolls_back_the_agent_policy` (admin_security.rs), against Keycloak:
  - the rules are read; with the step-up lapsed, the sign-in rules, Apply and rollback all answer `step_up_required` (the spec's invariant, for these);
  - with a fresh password the rules land in the realm; a code required makes Carol's password alone fail, and optional again lets it through; bad rules are refused;
  - a problem is placed on its line;
  - a draft is not in force, and the test panel gives "allow" live and "ask" with the draft, naming the rule;
  - a policy that does not fit the schema is refused, and the draft applied is the app's own policy;
  - a rollback brings version 1 back as version 3;
  - each change is audited;
  - both screens in every look and at phone width.
- Tests: `the_schema_parses_and_the_default_policy_fits_it` and `a_problem_names_its_line_and_column` (policy_admin.rs).

## The admin console's Operations (2026-11-02, `admin-operations`)

- **Kill switch**: besides the tasks halted, schedules held and sign-ins revoked, it says how many microVMs it froze (a run it stopped whose VM is stopped with its disk kept) and how many run now. Release, from here as from the banner, needs a fresh password.
- **Modules**: Magnus's run modules (the verifier, the lost-run reset, escalation, the memory pass), each with what it provides, its version (Magnus's), whether it is on, and how often it acted. An admin turns one on or off for the organisation (`ModuleSet`), on top of the config, for runs that start from then (K9: running tasks keep theirs).
  - The spec's "install from a package file" and "remove" have nothing to act on: Magnus's modules are part of its release (owner's ruling 22 keeps "module" for these), so a new one arrives with an update. The console says so; both answer 409.
- **Model providers**: the appliance's adapters, each with whether it is enabled and answering, its program, its endpoint, the models it offers now, its last conformance run, and the last error a run met on it. Settings whose name holds key, secret, token, password or credential are never shown. The connection test asks it for its models and says how long that took.
  - An admin disables or enables one (`ProviderSet`; a disabled provider's models are offered to no one), or makes one of its models the default for new tasks.
  - The default model's provider cannot be disabled until another default is chosen.
- **Health**: the tunnel (cloudflared) beside the other services, when `admin.tunnel_ready` names its readiness address (else "not set up"), and the microVMs running. Both are live through the stream.
- **Updates**: the version running, the latest release with its notes, whether it is available, what is going on and what happened last, and the release kept to go back to.
  - Install now and go back each need a fresh password (owner's ruling 6: automatic updates stay; step-up is for a manual install or a rollback).
  - The desktop app's channels, each with its published version and who gets it. An admin sets the channel everyone but the owner and testers follows (`DesktopChannelSet`).
- **Network**: the tunnel's state and its public names (`admin.public_hostnames`; the console is never one), the console's own name, aliases and addresses, the admin's own address, and every certificate with when it ends.
  - The allowlist is changed from here with a fresh password, and refused when it would leave out the admin's own address, or take in loopback. It takes effect at the next connection, is kept in the log (`ConsoleSettings.allow`), and is taken by the console at its next start.
- **Overview** now shows whether the tunnel is connected, and alerts when it is not and when an enabled model provider does not answer.
- Test: `root_runs_the_appliance_modules_providers_updates_and_network_from_operations` (admin_operations.rs), against Keycloak:
  - the kill switch counts microVMs;
  - the verifier is turned off and back to the config's, and a module package is refused;
  - providers are listed and tested, and the default's cannot be disabled;
  - the desktop channel is set;
  - the network shows the console's subnets and no tunnel, and Health names the tunnel and the microVMs;
  - with the step-up lapsed, install, rollback and the allowlist all answer `step_up_required`;
  - with a fresh password, install answers and rollback has nothing kept;
  - an allowlist that shuts out the admin's own address is refused, one with loopback is invalid, and a wider one is saved with the console still answering;
  - each change is audited;
  - the screens in every look and at phone width.

## The admin console's Knowledge (2026-11-02, `admin-knowledge`)

- The organisation's shared ontology, context and memory are the org context bundle that agents curate (the owner's org context build spec replaced the memory spec; #76). The console's Knowledge section is that bundle as an admin oversees, corrects and rolls it back:
  - **Ontology** is the entity types (`/types/`). Each shows its definition, its attributes (identity keys), its relations with their cardinality, a drawing of it and its directly related types (both ways), its entries, what refers to it, and its history.
  - **Context** is what every agent receives: the map (`/index.md` and each area's index), who does what (`/people/`), the terminology and standing facts (`/conventions/`), and the sources. Each item shows who last changed it and when, and is edited in place. "As agents see it" shows the map as a chosen person's agent receives it, with links to what they cannot see turned into text (K2).
  - **Memory** is the entries (`/entities/`): summary, linked entities, sources, created, last used (the last run that read it), filtered by source, writer, linked entity and date. An entry's provenance is its writer (an agent and its version, or an admin), run, sources and the taint it was read under, with every version.
  - **Activity** is every change agents make, newest first, from the log: who, what, created, updated or removed, its run and sources, filtered by agent, area, change and time, and live from the stream. A change opens with its before and after.
- What an admin does:
  - Ontology: create a type; edit it as OKF text; add or remove a relation; approve it as stable (only an admin may, as #76 decided), deprecate it, or bring it back as a draft; merge it into another type (each entry moved and re-typed, every reference repointed, the type removed, each step a version); delete it, refused while it has entries or is referred to.
  - Memory: edit an entry, delete it, or roll it back; delete or roll back many at once, with a fresh password.
  - Activity: undo one change, or a selection as a batch, with a fresh password. Each document goes back to how it was before the earliest selected change, as a new version, or is removed if it did not exist then.
  - Every version can be shown against now and rolled back to.
- An admin's write goes through the same checks as an agent's (`bundle::validate`), as `human:<admin's sub>`. It keeps the document's sources and taint, so editing launders nothing. A removal (`BundleRemoved`) keeps every version in the log to bring back.
- Overview's "agent knowledge changes in the last 24 hours" now counts agents' writes to the bundle, and opens Activity.
- Tests (admin_knowledge.rs):
  - `the_kernel_keeps_every_knowledge_change_and_root_rolls_any_back`, through the control socket, run here:
    - the agent's provenance;
    - an admin approves a type as stable; a malformed edit is refused; the type is rolled back;
    - the agent's change is in Activity with its before and after, and undone;
    - a type in use is not deleted but merged, and its versions stay readable;
    - the map as Bob's agent sees it.
  - `root_oversees_corrects_and_rolls_back_the_organisations_knowledge_from_the_console`, against Keycloak:
    - the same through the console (the spec's two Knowledge invariants), with relations added and rolled back, an entry edited and rolled back, and an agent's change undone;
    - bulk and batch actions refused without a fresh password, and a bulk delete answering per entry;
    - every change audited;
    - the four screens in every look and at phone width.
  - `an_id_is_one_segment_and_never_leaves_the_bundle` and `okf_text_splits_into_frontmatter_and_body` (admin/knowledge.rs).
- At phone width the two-column screens (Ontology, Agent policy) are one column of `minmax(0, 1fr)`, as on wide screens, not `1fr`: a plain `1fr` grew to its widest child, so a type's history, with a run id or an author's id as one unbroken word, made the screen scroll sideways by 116px (#85's first CI run). Its tables now scroll inside their own box, as the console's other wide tables do, and long ids in tables wrap.

## The admin console's trusted certificate: issued through the tailnet (2026-11-02, `admin-certificate`)

- The spec (Access): "a certificate browsers already trust: ACME DNS-01 under a domain the organisation controls, or the organisation's own CA". Until now the console had only a CA made on the appliance, which the owner had to trust by hand on each computer.
- Chosen: `admin.trusted_via = "tailscale"`. The owner reaches strix-alpha over Tailscale ("not found over tailscale"), and its name there (strix-alpha.tailfc1d45.ts.net) is the console's hostname. `tailscale cert` has Let's Encrypt issue a certificate for that name by ACME DNS-01, under the tailnet's domain, with tailscaled answering the challenge.
  - No secret is needed. ACME DNS-01 under pyrallon.ai would need a Cloudflare API token in the repository; that stays possible later as another `trusted_via`.
  - The organisation's own CA stays as it was: `tls_cert` without `trusted_via`. Renew reads it again once the organisation has renewed it.
- How it is served (`admin/net.rs` `Certs`, `admin/trusted.rs`):
  - by name: browsers naming the hostname get the trusted certificate once it is issued; the LAN and tailnet addresses, and every name until then, get the CA's;
  - swapped while the console runs, from the next handshake, with no restart;
  - asked for on start when it is not yet issued, every twelve hours when fewer than 30 days are left, and on Operations › Network › Renew (`POST /api/v1/network/certificate/renew`, audited as `certificate_renewed`, or `certificate_renewed_failed` with what tailscale said).
- A failure never stops the console. It keeps what it serves; Overview's alerts and Operations › Network give tailscale's reason. The likely one on a new tailnet: HTTPS is off. The owner turns it on in Tailscale's admin console under DNS.
- The setup script writes `trusted_via` when the machine is on a tailnet. It lets its own user ask tailscaled for the certificate (`sudo tailscale set --operator`), because the console runs as that user.
- No step-up for Renew: it changes no setting and serves only a certificate for the console's own name.
- Tests:
  - `the_console_serves_the_tailnets_certificate_for_its_hostname_and_its_own_until_then` (admin_certificate.rs, against a stand-in for `tailscale cert` whose CA stands for Let's Encrypt): a browser trusting only that CA opens the console by its hostname, and is shown the issued certificate; a restart serves it at once without asking again; with HTTPS off, the console still starts, on the CA's;
  - `an_admin_renews_the_consoles_certificate_and_sees_why_it_could_not_be_issued` (Keycloak): a failed Renew keeps what is served, gives tailscale's reason in the answer, on Network and as an alert; a successful one is served from the next connection; both are audited; the screen in every look;
  - `the_setup_writes_an_admin_console_that_answers_its_hostname_on_the_network_only`, extended: the setup's config gets the tailnet's certificate for the Tailscale name, and the address keeps the CA's;
  - `the_trusted_certificate_answers_for_the_hostname_alone_once_issued` and `a_key_that_is_not_the_certificates_is_refused` (admin/net.rs).
## The admin console reaches the provider's admin API where the server keeps it (2026-11-02, `console-admin-origin`)

- The owner (2026-11-02): "users are not available in the admin app". On strix-alpha, Keycloak answers its admin API only on an origin of its own (`hostname-admin`, `https://127.0.0.1:19446`, with a certificate from the box's own CA), and its public address refuses `/admin/`. The appliance already reaches it there (`sign_in.oidc.status_admin_url` and `status_admin_ca`, above). The console asked the public address, so Users listed no one, and Sign-in rules, invites, roles and turning people off failed the same way. The tests run Keycloak on one address, so they could not see it.
- `admin.idp_admin_url` and `admin.idp_admin_ca` say where the admin API answers and which CA to trust there. Unset, the console takes the appliance's own `status_admin_url` and `status_admin_ca` from the same magnus.toml, since it is the same server. So strix-alpha needs no change to its config. The service account's token is still taken at the public token endpoint, as the appliance's status client does.
- A CA that cannot be read does not stop the console. Each call to the admin API says why, and the reason is logged at start.
- Test: `the_console_manages_people_through_the_providers_own_admin_origin` (admin_admin_origin.rs), against Keycloak behind two doors, as on strix-alpha. The public door refuses `/admin/`, and the admin door serves only it. With only the appliance's `status_admin_url` set, the console lists the realm's people, turns one off and on, and draws the screen. Without the fix, the list answers 502. Unit: `the_admin_api_is_the_realms_own` (admin/directory.rs), with the admin origin and an unreadable CA.
## The desktop app carries the sign-in server's name back when connecting an app (2026-11-02, `connector-sign-in-iss`)

- The owner (2026-11-02): "No way to sign in to individual connectors on the app." Google's authorization server says it names itself in its answer (`authorization_response_iss_parameter_supported`, RFC 9207). So the kernel refuses an answer that names no one: that answer could be another server's code, sent to this one's token endpoint (a mix-up, as recorded for connector sign-in). The app's shell caught the redirect on its loopback address but passed on only `code` and `state` (`loopback_wait`). Every Google connection was refused after the person had signed in at Google. The app's own sign-in to Magnus already passed `iss`.
- `loopback_wait` now returns `iss` too, and the Library sends it.
- The installed-app test (`the_owner_connects_an_app_from_settings_and_reapproves_a_changed_tool`, `desktop/tests/app-apps.mjs`) now signs in to an app that names itself as Google does (`FakeOAuthApp::naming_itself`). With the old shell, the card never turns Connected. The kernel tests keep the fake that names no one, which RFC 9207 still allows.
- Ships with the next development build of the desktop app, which the owner's apps follow.
## An app blocked for a group leaves that group's Library (2026-11-02, `library-groups`)

- The connectors spec (Org policy, isolation and audit): "For groups: allowed or blocked per group, and blocked connectors disappear from those users' Library." Until now an app had only the groups it is for (`access_groups`). The boundary enforced them (`has_rights`), but the Library listed every app to everyone, so a person saw, opened and could start signing in to an app they could never use.
- An app also has the groups it is blocked for (`blocked_groups`, set from the admin console beside who may use it, and kept in the log in `ConnectorSettings`). A block wins over an allow.
- One rule decides both who reaches an app and who sees it (`boundary::may_use`): in one of its groups (or it names none), and in none it is blocked for.
- A person's Library, the app's page and starting a sign-in all apply it: the app is not listed, its page is not found, and a sign-in is refused with "your organisation has not made this app available to you". The owner still manages every app in Settings, each marked as theirs or not (`for_you`), and their own Library hides the ones not theirs.
- Group-scoped knowledge from an app's documents still follows only `access_groups`: a block list cannot be written as the knowledge ACL's groups.
- Tests: `an_app_blocked_for_a_group_leaves_that_groups_library_and_cannot_be_signed_in_to` (library_groups.rs: Gmail allowed for staff and blocked for finance; Carol has it, Bob and Dana neither see it, open it, nor start signing in; the owner still manages it; clearing the block keeps who may use it); `an_app_is_for_its_groups_and_never_for_the_groups_it_is_blocked_for` (boundary.rs).

## A connection's life: Disconnect revokes at the app first, and both ends are in the log (2026-11-02, `connector-lifecycle`)

- The connectors spec, Lifecycle: "Disconnect first calls the provider's revocation endpoint"; for a provider with none, "the tokens are deleted and the card names the provider page where the user removes Magnus"; "Revoked at the provider: the next call fails and the connection moves to Needs attention".
- Before this change, Disconnect deleted the tokens and then revoked them. The outcome was only a trace line: not in the log, not told to the person, and no test checked it.
- Disconnect now:
  - reads the sign-in, revokes its refresh and access tokens at the app (RFC 7009), then deletes it here whatever the app answered;
  - answers whether the app ended it (`at_provider`), and why not;
  - for a catalog app, names the app's own page where the person removes Magnus (`remove_access` in the manifest; Google's is myaccount.google.com/connections).
  - The app's toast says which happened.
- Both ends of a connection are now in the log:
  - `AppConnected`: app, person, and the account the app named;
  - `AppDisconnected`: app, person, `at_provider` and why.
  - No token is recorded. The connection itself stays in the vault.
- Tests (connector_lifecycle.rs):
  - `disconnect_revokes_at_the_app_first_and_both_ends_of_a_connection_are_in_the_log`: both tokens revoked at the app; the card is available again; a second Disconnect is not found; both events are logged with whose they are and no token.
  - `an_app_that_cannot_revoke_is_disconnected_here_and_the_person_is_told`.
  - `a_sign_in_ended_at_the_app_asks_the_person_to_sign_in_again`: the person removes Magnus in the app's own settings; the card turns Needs attention, and signing in again brings it back.
  - `connectors.rs` expects Disconnect's new answer.
## Magnus knows the person's apps while it works, and offers an app's card with why (2026-11-02, `agent-apps`)

- The connectors spec, How the agent uses connectors:
  - "the context always holds the user's connector index";
  - "catalog tools to search the library by service name or by what the user wants done";
  - "Read a connector's details";
  - "Place a connector's card in chat, saying what it is for"; only the user completes a sign-in.
  - Inside work: "When a task or chat needs a connection, Magnus says so in that thread with the card".
  - Until now, a run saw only the tools of apps already connected, and knew nothing of the rest.
- Every run and chat now holds the person's app index, placed before their words:
  - each app the organisation lets them use (the same rule as the Library, `boundary::may_use`, and only apps turned on);
  - its one sentence, and whether it is connected;
  - it is rebuilt at every model call, so a connection made mid-conversation shows at once.
- Three read tools, built in for chats and tasks (`app_tools.rs`):
  - `find_apps`: the person's apps whose name, publisher, sentence or abilities hold the words asked for, best first. With nothing found it says so: "do not suggest one that is not there".
  - `app_details`: what Magnus can do with the app, the access it asks for, and what it can and cannot do.
  - `offer_app`: puts the app's card in the conversation with Magnus's reason. It is refused for an app outside the person's Library, and says so for one already connected. Nothing is connected until the person signs in.
- The card in a conversation (`connector_card`):
  - it is the same card as in the Library: tile, name, publisher, the reason, the app's sentence, and one Connect button through the app's existing connect path;
  - it shows the app as the person stands with it now: Connected once they have connected it, "not ready" while the appliance is not set up for it, gone from their Library if blocked since.
  - Magnus reads it back in the conversation as "Offered the app …".
- In a task, the same card leads the task's page, under the decisions it waits on, until the person connects the app. The task carries the apps it offered (`app_offers`, each as the person stands with it now). In approvals the card is still to come.
- Tests (agent_apps.rs):
  - `magnus_finds_an_unconnected_app_by_what_is_wanted_and_offers_its_card_with_why`: the index is in the context; find_apps finds both calendars by "find a time on my calendar"; the card is in the thread with its reason, ready to connect, and nothing is connected; `desktop/tests/chat-app-card.mjs` draws it with Connect and no protocol words, in every look and at phone width.
  - `a_task_that_needs_an_app_shows_its_card_with_why`: a task offers the calendar, and the task carries its card with the reason.
  - `magnus_cannot_offer_an_app_outside_the_persons_library`: Gmail blocked for Bob's group is not in his index, not found and not offered; a request nothing fits ("post in our Slack") is answered plainly.
- A sign-in the app refused (connectors spec, Lifecycle: "Re-sign-in: a refresh rejected as invalid marks the connection Needs attention and raises the card"):
  - A refresh the app refuses (a 4xx, not a failure to reach it) is kept on the stored sign-in as lapsed, and is not tried again until the person signs in again.
  - The index says the app needs signing in again, and its tools stop being offered to the run.
  - The boundary's refusal tells Magnus to offer the card, and `offer_app` gives one with "Sign in to … again".
  - Signing in again brings it back.
  - Test: `a_sign_in_the_app_refused_is_offered_again_as_a_card_and_its_tools_wait` (agent_apps.rs).
- More access (connectors spec, Remote MCP connectors: "a call needing an ungranted scope … becomes a Connect card asking for the added scope, and the call proceeds once approved"):
  - An app that refuses a call with MCP authorization's step-up challenge (HTTP 403, `insufficient_scope`, the scopes it names) is answered by the kernel itself:
    - the scopes are kept on the person's sign-in (`wants`);
    - the card "Approve more access" is placed in the conversation or task;
    - Magnus is told nothing happened at the app, and to call again once approved.
  - Her index says the app needs more access, and the Library's card turns to approving it.
  - Approving is signing in again: the sign-in asks for the added scopes too. The new sign-in holds them, and the call then goes through.
  - Test: `a_call_that_needs_more_access_asks_for_it_with_a_card_and_goes_through_once_approved` (agent_apps.rs). The fake app holds per-token scopes and challenges a tool that needs one more.

## The app's card in approvals (2026-11-02, `approval-app-card`)

- The connectors spec, The connector card: "The same card appears in the Library, in chat, in a task, and in approvals, so connecting never sends the user to a separate settings system." The card was in the Library, in chat and in a task; an approval for an app's action named only the tool.
- Each approval in Needs you whose tool is an app's now carries the app's card (`app`, the same `offered_card` as in chat and tasks), as the person whose task it is stands with the app now. It is worked out when the list is read, not stored with the item, so a sign-in the app refuses while the decision waits shows at once.
- Only that person signs in to their app. Someone else who decides it (an approver with the role) sees the card with where the person stands, and no button: a sign-in is always the person's own.
- The kernel's own tools have no card.
- Test: `an_approval_for_an_apps_action_carries_the_apps_card_as_the_person_stands_with_it` (approval_app_card.rs): the card while Carol is connected; after the app refuses her sign-in, it asks her to sign in again; in the app (`approval-app-card.mjs`), Carol gets the button and Alice, who decides, gets the state and the decision, in every look and at phone width.

## Tools searched across connectors, loaded on demand (2026-11-02, `tool-search`)

- The connectors spec, How the agent uses connectors: "Index, then load", and "tools searched across connectors and full schemas loaded on demand". Every run was offered the full definition of every tool of every app the person had connected. A dozen apps can bring hundreds of tools: every model call pays for all of them, and the model chooses worse among so many.
- Up to `LOAD_ALL` (40) tools across the person's connected apps, a run is offered all of them, as before: most people connect a few apps, and searching would only cost them a step.
- Past it, a run is offered none at first, and the builtin `find_tools` instead. It searches the run's app tools by words in their names and descriptions, best first, up to eight, and answers with each one's name and what it does. From the run's next step those tools are offered with their full definitions, for the rest of the run.
- What a run found is read back from its own `find_tools` results in the log, so no new event is needed, and a run picked up after a restart keeps what it found.
- The verifier still sees every tool the run may use (`every_tool_for`), since a criterion's check may name one the agent never searched for.
- Every call still passes the boundary as before: a tool not yet found is only not offered, never permitted or refused differently.
- Tests (tool_search.rs):
  - `past_the_limit_a_run_is_offered_the_tools_it_finds_and_they_go_through`: 45 tools; the first step has `find_tools` and no app tool; "calendar events" finds `list_events`; from the next step it alone of the app's tools is offered, with its definition, and its call goes through.
  - `within_the_limit_every_tool_is_offered_and_there_is_nothing_to_find`.

## An app's content in shared org memory passes policy, proven (2026-11-02, `app-content-policy`)

- The connectors spec, How the agent uses connectors: "Writing a connection's content into shared org memory passes policy like any other action." Nothing needed building. `knowledge_add` is a tool like any other, so the boundary puts it to the Cedar policy with the run's taint. What it files is readable only by whom its sources allow. No test showed the first part for an app's content.
- Test: `filing_an_apps_private_content_in_the_knowledge_base_is_decided_by_policy` (app_content_policy.rs). An org rule forbids `knowledge_add` when the run holds private content. After Alice's calendar is read through her app, filing is refused by that rule and nothing is filed. Filing from a public web page goes through.

## A retired tool leaves the agent at once (2026-11-02, `retired-tools`)

- The connectors spec, Remote MCP connectors: "A retired tool leaves the agent at once." A run was offered every pinned tool of a connected app, including one the app no longer listed. The agent then called a tool the app had retired, and the call failed at the app.
- When the app's live list of tools is read (kept two seconds), a tool not on it is not offered. When the list cannot be read, the pinned tools are still offered as before: an app that cannot be reached fails its calls, and nothing is learned about which tools it retired.
- The pin stays. A tool that comes back unchanged is offered again with no new review, and one that comes back changed waits for review as any changed definition does (B10).
- Test: `a_tool_the_app_no_longer_lists_leaves_the_agent_at_once` (retired_tools.rs): create_event offered, retired and not offered, listed again and offered, with no new review.

## A sign-in is a step-up only when the password was typed for it (2026-11-02, `step-up-typed`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). Every new console session counted as stepped up, but a plain sign-in does not ask the provider for a password: while the admin's session at the provider lives (and the console's own refresh keeps it alive), the console's Sign in comes straight back. That session could release the kill switch, delete people or change roles with no password typed, against the spec's Step-up ("a passkey check in the last 5 minutes"; owner's ruling 2: the password, and the code).
- A new session is stepped up only when the ID token's `auth_time` is no earlier than the moment its sign-in started, the same check a step-up already makes; otherwise its first action that needs one asks for the password. "Signing in counts as one" (the step-up line under the console's authentication) now means a sign-in with the password typed.
- Test: `a_sign_in_the_provider_lets_through_without_a_password_is_no_step_up` (admin_sign_in.rs): in the same browser, still signed in at the provider, Sign in again asks for nothing, and the new session has no step-up and is answered `step_up_required`.


## Who sees an org document comes from what its run read (2026-11-02, `bundle-readers`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). Whether an org document is seen by everyone or only by its writer was decided from the `sources` its frontmatter cites, which the model writes. A run that read Alice's private file could publish what it said to the whole organisation by citing only `human:alice`, against K2 ("U can read every item listed for D") and the line above that a document drawn from items in the org's systems is seen only by the person whose run wrote it.
- The kernel now adds to a document's recorded sources one `private:<place>` for each place whose private content the writing run read (its `private_from`: an app, `library`, or `unnamed` for privacy carried in from where the run started). Such a source is neither a bundle path nor a person's answer, so the document is its writer's alone, whatever it cites. The frontmatter is unchanged: these are the kernel's record, shown in the console's document view.
- An admin's edit, a rollback and an undo keep those sources and the writer: a version written with no run keeps the writer whose run read them. Otherwise an admin's edit would have hidden such a document from everyone, its writer included, or, with the new sources dropped, shown it to everyone.
- Left for its own pull request: a document an admin removed and then restored is rebuilt from its frontmatter, so it loses these sources and its taint (the review's next finding, taint on rollback and undo; done in `restored-taint`).
- Test: `an_org_document_from_private_content_is_its_writers_alone_whatever_it_cites` (org_context_bundle.rs): Alice's task is given her own file, its run writes what the file says into `/people/finance.md` citing only `human:alice`; Bob can neither read nor find it, also after an admin edits it, and the document's writer is still Alice.

## A person's apps when they leave or are turned off (2026-11-02, `person-connections`)

- The connectors spec, Isolation: "When an admin removes a user, every connection is revoked at the provider and its tokens deleted. When the identity provider turns a user off, their connections refuse every call and schedules pause until they are back; tokens stay in the vault unused."
- Removing a person already revoked their sign-ins at each app (`erase_person`), but no test checked it. The admin console's delete test now has Bob connect an app first and checks that both his tokens are revoked at the app.
- Turning a person off ended their sessions and paused their schedules, but a run of theirs already going, such as one waiting on an approval, still called their apps. Now `turn_off`, whether from the provider's check or the admin console, records `PersonTurnedOff`. The boundary refuses every app call for that person ("their apps answer no calls for them until they are back; nothing was sent"), and the app is never reached.
- Their sign-ins stay in the vault unused: turning someone off is not removing them, and they may be back.
- They are back when they sign in again (`SignedIn` clears it), since the provider let them in. Being turned back on at the provider alone changes nothing here until then, because no session can act for them before it.
- Test: `a_person_turned_off_has_no_apps_until_they_are_back` (turned_off_apps.rs, Keycloak). Carol's task waits on an approval to read her calendar, and Keycloak turns her off. Approved after that, the call is refused and never reaches the app, and nothing is revoked. Turned back on and signed in again, her next task's call goes through with the same sign-in.

## A version put back comes back as it was written (2026-11-02, `restored-taint`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). A rollback and an undo wrote the old content as an admin's edit, which takes its taint and its `private:` sources from the version there now. Put back over a clean version, or after the document was removed, content drawn from someone's private file came back clean and seen by everyone, against M1 (content keeps its label) and K2.
- A rollback and an undo now put the version back from the log as it was written: its own label, the `private:` sources its run read, and the person whose run read them, not the current version's. That person is the bundle's writer at that version, worked out from all of the path's writes and removals up to it (read by path, not from the newest events only). A label the log cannot read counts as untrusted and private.
- Each agent's version now names its writer (`writer` on `BundleWritten`), and so does a version put back; an admin's edit names none and keeps the document's. A run's person is otherwise lost from the state when its task goes to the trash. For versions written before this, the writer comes from the live run or, once its task is in the trash, from the run's `TaskCreated` in the log (codex review of #99).
- A version seen only by its writer whose writer cannot be found is not put back (409): it would otherwise keep the current writer, and show one person's content to another.
- Its own version's taint, not the joint of both: what is put back is only that version's content, and joining writers would show one person's private content to another.
- Left for its own pull request: merging one type into another moves each entity to a new path as an admin's fresh write, so a moved entity loses its taint and `private:` sources the same way.
- Test: `a_version_put_back_keeps_the_taint_and_the_reader_it_was_written_with` (org_context_bundle.rs): the document from Alice's own file is removed after her task went to the trash, written afresh by Bob's run and that undone; removed and rolled back; removed and its removal undone; and removed, written afresh by an admin and that undone. Each time it is Alice's, untrusted and private, with `private:library` among its sources, and Bob cannot read it.

## An app turned off still has its sign-ins ended (2026-11-02, `off-apps`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). Turning an app off keeps every person's sign-in to it in the vault, so turned on again it works. But the kill switch took the apps whose sign-ins it revokes from the apps that are on, and a person's Disconnect looked the app up the same way: a turned-off app's sign-ins stayed valid at the app through the kill switch (B11: "each OAuth sign-in taken out of it and revoked at its provider"), and a person could not end their own (connectors spec, Lifecycle) until the owner turned it back on.
- Both now take every app, on or off. An app removed altogether has had every sign-in revoked already (`remove_connector`), so nothing is left behind there.
- The inbox's app cards still list only apps that are on: they are what a person can use.
- Test: `an_app_turned_off_still_has_its_sign_ins_ended_by_disconnect_and_the_kill_switch` (connector_lifecycle.rs): Alice and Carol sign in to an app, the owner turns it off over the kernel's control socket; Alice's Disconnect revokes hers at the app, and the kill switch revokes Carol's there. On master Disconnect says "no such app" (404); with that fixed alone, Carol's sign-in is never revoked.

## The owner's lists in conversation follow the policy on people's data (2026-11-02, `private-views`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). In conversation, `list_tasks` and `list_schedules` give the owner and admins everyone's tasks and schedules with what each run said it did (its summary). That is a read of people's data, but it skipped the policy that can make it private and was not recorded, against owner's ruling 14 ("A policy setting can make people's data private from the admin. Each read is still recorded."), which the Data tab already follows.
- Now, with people's data private, other people's rows keep their record (id, person, status, when, schedule) and lose their words: the owner and admins still control anyone's (behavior spec, Tasks and schedules in conversation), but read nothing of theirs. Otherwise each person whose rows were listed has the read in the log (`PersonDataRead`, as "tasks") and is told in their inbox, as for the Data tab. The record-and-tell step is one function for both (`record_data_read`).
- Not done here, as the owner's call: the developer views (V1-V7) show every run's trace to the `developer` role (K10, K45), and the policy names the admin. Whether making people's data private also covers the developer role: answered below ("Developer views and the policy on people's data").
- Test: `the_owners_list_of_everyones_tasks_in_conversation_follows_the_policy_on_peoples_data` (data_access.rs): Bob's task ends with what it found; Alice, the owner, asks in conversation what everyone is working on. Her list shows Bob's task and its words, the read is in the log and Bob is told; once people's data is private, Bob's task is listed without its words, and no read is recorded. On master the read is not in the log.

## A person deleted keeps no rights and takes only their own sign-ins (2026-11-02, `erase-all`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). Deleting a person (`erase_person`) left the roles the owner gave them in Settings, and the rights their provider last gave them, in the appliance's state. Whoever was next known by their name, a new account at the provider, started with them: an admin deleted came back an admin. It also revoked every sign-in in the vault whose name ended in `__<person>`, so deleting `bob` revoked `ops__bob`'s too.
- Now the deletion clears both rights maps (`roles_set`, `provider_rights`), and revokes exactly the person's own sign-ins: a sign-in is `connector_<app>__<person>`, and an app's name holds no `__` (its tools are `<app>__<tool>`) though it may end in `_`, so a sign-in is the person's when what is left of it, once `connector_` and `__<person>` are taken off, is such a name (codex review of #102: not the first `__`, which an app named `calendar_` would move).
- Not resolved here: the vault's name is ambiguous when one app's name is another's with a `_` added and one person's id is another's with a `_` in front (`calendar_` and Bob, `calendar` and `_bob`): both sign-ins would have the same name. That is how sign-ins are named, not this deletion.
- Left for its own pull request: a deleted person's branched conversations (their entries as shown then) and their tasks' attachment lists stay in the state until the appliance restarts.
- Test: `a_person_deleted_keeps_no_roles_and_takes_only_their_own_sign_ins` (person_erasure.rs, Keycloak): the owner makes Bob an admin; Bob and `ops__bob` each sign in to an app, and Bob to one named `notes_` too; root deletes Bob over the control socket. Bob's roles are the config's again, not admin, his two sign-ins were revoked (at `notes_` too), and `ops__bob`'s is still connected. On master it revoked two; with that fixed alone, Bob is still an admin.

## Developer views and the policy on people's data (2026-11-02)

- Owner, 2026-11-02, on whether making people's data private covers the developer views: "Developer and admin are currently the same. That will change eventually. Currently I am the developer. Admin grew out of that with the appliance because the appliance will have admins that the org hired and won't be me." So the developer views stay as they are while the owner is both. Once an organisation's own admins run an appliance, the developer role is no longer theirs, and what it may see of people's data is decided then.

## The provider's check asks after the account a person is bound to (2026-11-02, `active-by-account`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). Every five minutes, and as the appliance starts, the kernel asks the identity provider whether each person signed in or with a schedule is still there and turned on (owner's ruling 12). It asked by username. A person is the provider's account that first signed in as them (`subjects`, owner's rulings 2026-11-01), so with their account deleted and another made under the same username, the check found the new account and kept the old account's sessions and schedules going.
- It now asks after the bound account by its id (`sub`, Keycloak's `/users/{id}`): deleted, it is no longer known, and the person is turned off as for an account turned off. A person not yet bound is still asked after by username.
- Test: `a_persons_account_deleted_ends_their_session_though_another_takes_their_username` (people.rs, Keycloak): Carol signs in; her account is deleted and another made under her username; at the next check her session ends. On master it never does.

## A run ends once: the first end stands (2026-11-02, `cancel-races`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). Cancelling a task checks that its run has not ended, then records the cancel; a run that ended on its own in between (completed or failed) was turned into a cancelled one, and the person was told it was cancelled. The other way, a run's own end recorded after a cancel turned the cancelled run into a completed or failed one.
- Now completed, failed and cancelled each apply only to a run that has not ended: the first stands, in the state and so on replay (nothing reopens an ended run; suspend and resume already checked this). A cancel that lost the race tells the person how the task ended ("the task has already ended (completed)") and stops nothing.
- Left, with no test that can fail on it at will: a schedule removed or paused while a firing it was due for is starting (its words are being recalled) still starts that run. Closing it needs the firing to check again after `create_run` (and cancel the run if the schedule is gone or paused) and `cancel_schedule` to list the schedule's runs after recording its removal, not before; it goes with a way to hold a firing between the two in a test.
- Test: `a_run_ends_once_the_first_end_stands` (state.rs): a cancel after a run completed leaves it completed with its summary and end time; a failure after a cancel leaves it cancelled. On master both are overwritten.

## A sign-in waits for the person who started it (2026-11-02, `pending-sign-in`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). Finishing a sign-in to an app took it from those waiting before checking whose it was, so someone else's attempt with its state (refused) ended it, and the person who started it had to start again.
- Now a sign-in is taken only by the person who started it, for the app it was started for; anyone else's attempt is refused and leaves it waiting. An expired one is still refused, and swept when the next sign-in starts.
- Test: `another_persons_attempt_leaves_a_sign_in_waiting_for_its_owner` (connectors.rs): Carol's attempt at Alice's sign-in is refused, and Alice then finishes it. On master Alice's finish is refused.

## Policy drafts are console actions in the audit log (2026-11-02, `policy-drafts-audited`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02): "every console action in the append-only log with who, what, when, source and before/after". Keeping or dropping a policy draft went to the kernel's event log under the admin's name but not to the audit log.
- Now `policy_drafted` and `policy_draft_dropped` are written to the audit log before the change, with the set's draft before and after; a draft is not in force, so neither asks for a fresh password.
- Test: `root_sets_the_sign_in_rules_and_edits_tests_applies_and_rolls_back_the_agent_policy` (admin_security.rs, Keycloak) now drops the sales draft and finds both in the audit with their text. On master neither is there.

## The kernel numbers each document version, and a path counts on through removal (2026-11-02, `bundle-versions`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02; "every write is a new version"). The version was worked out from the state before the kernel's write lock was taken, so writes at once to one path took the same number; and a removal dropped the path's count, so it began again at 1 and (path, version) named two versions in the log, the older one out of reach.
- Now `emit` numbers each `BundleWritten` under the write lock from the last number the path was written with, which the state keeps through a removal. Callers take the number from the event recorded.
- `a_version_put_back_keeps_the_taint_and_the_reader_it_was_written_with` rolled back to "version 2" because Bob's new document began again at 1; with the count kept it is version 3, and the test now says so.
- Test: `every_version_of_a_document_has_its_own_number_through_removal_and_writes_at_once` (org_context_bundle.rs): written, removed and written again, a document is at version 2 and version 1 is still the first; eight writes at once take eight numbers. On master the second write is version 1 again, and the eight writes at once all took one number.

## A link to a hidden document is removed with its text, in every form (2026-11-02, `hidden-links`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). The spec: documents come back with "links the user cannot see removed", and in the index "a line with some visible links keeps only those". A hidden link became its text (`[Carol's pay review](/people/finance.md)` became "Carol's pay review"), and links were found only in the inline form, so `[text][label]` with `[label]: /path` passed both the check on writing and the filter on reading.
- Now a link to a document the person cannot see is removed with its text, inline (with or without a title) or by reference (`[text][label]`, `[label][]`, `[label]`), and a definition of one is dropped; a bullet whose links are all hidden is still dropped. Reference definitions are checked as links on writing. Links are found in the whole body: brackets nest, so a label's own brackets do not end it, a destination may begin on the next line, and a line is a definition only when it starts with `[label]:` (codex review of #108).
- Frontmatter is left as written: the spec says documents come back "with their OKF frontmatter intact". Links to items a person cannot read inside an entity's frontmatter are step 4's (T3).
- Test: `a_hidden_documents_link_leaves_with_its_text_whatever_its_form` (org_context_bundle.rs): Bob reads a page linking a document no one can see inline with a title and by reference, and sees the page's other link but neither the hidden link's text nor its path; his index keeps the visible link of a mixed bullet and drops the hidden-only one. On master he sees "Carol's pay review". And `a_hidden_link_leaves_whatever_its_label_or_line_holds` (bundle.rs): a label with brackets, a line with a later `[x]:`, a destination on the next line and a reference link each leave whole; a relative destination on the next line is still refused.

## An entity moved by a merge of types keeps what it was written from (2026-11-02, `merge-taint`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02; DEC "keeps the document's sources and taint, so editing launders nothing"). Merging a type wrote each entity at its new path through the admin's write, which takes the taint and private sources of the document at that path; there is none, so the entity came back clean, its `private:` sources and writer gone.
- Now each entity moves with the label, the `private:` sources and the writer of the version it moves from; its new path's version is checked as any admin write. Documents repointed to the new type keep theirs as before (same path).
- Test: `an_entity_moved_by_merging_types_keeps_its_taint_and_its_reader` (org_context_bundle.rs): Alice's run reads her file and writes an entity; merged into another type, it keeps its label, `private:library` and Alice as writer. On master it comes back clean.

## A person erased takes their branches and attachments from the state (2026-11-02, `erase-branches`)

- Left for its own pull request by `erase-all`: deleting a person removed their tasks, runs and files from the state but not the threads they branched (each holds the conversation up to the branch, as shown then) nor their tasks' attachment lists (file names and ids), which stayed until the appliance restarted.
- Now `PersonErased` drops each branch of theirs and the attachments of each task of theirs; other people's stay.
- Test: `a_person_erased_leaves_no_branch_or_attachment_of_theirs` (state.rs): Bob and Alice each branch a thread and attach a file to a task; Bob erased, his are gone and Alice's stay. On master Bob's both remain.

## The admin console's tracker checks only what is built and tested (2026-11-02, `tracker-truth`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). Unchecked in `docs/admin-console.md`, each with what is built: the realm role checked on every request (it is checked at sign-in and each refresh, at most every 4 minutes); only `magnus-admin` holding `magnus_root`, and Postgres as `magnus_root` (`magnus migrate` creates the role, but the console never connects as it, and its password is in the config `magnus serve` loads); a VPN connection accepted (no test connects from one); hold-to-stop at phone width within 5 seconds (untested); every screen in every look (seven screens are never visited); install and remove of modules.
- Correction to the Modules entry above ("owner's ruling 22 keeps 'module' for these"): question 22 is open, not ruled. Install and remove still answer 409, as the owner's call rather than a ruling, in the tracker and in `admin/ops.rs`.
- PROGRESS.md said org context steps 2 to 7b were committed on their branches; no branch has them, and the plan has no 7b. It now says steps 2 to 11, with only step 1 built.
- Documents and a comment only: no behaviour changes, so no test.

## A stable type is changed only by an admin (2026-11-03, `stable-types`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). The org context build spec, Tools, `bundle.write`: "A type document moving to stable requires admin approval, because types become Cedar entities". The kernel refused Magnus making a draft type stable, but once an admin had, Magnus's next write could keep it stable with new identity keys or relations, or put it back to draft: either changes a Cedar entity no admin approved.
- Now Magnus's write to a stable type is refused with the reason, whatever status it asks for. Changing, deprecating or reopening a stable type is the console's (admin console spec, Knowledge › Ontology).
- Test: `a_type_an_admin_made_stable_is_changed_by_no_run_of_magnus` (org_context_bundle.rs): an admin makes the customer type stable; Magnus's rewrite with a third identity key and its rewrite as draft are both refused, and the type stays version 1, stable, with its two keys. On master the rewrite is version 2.

## How a document was checked is the kernel's record (2026-11-03, `kernel-verifications`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-02). The org context build spec, Bundle format: each entry in `verified` is {by, at, kind, event, note}, `event` the event-log id; Outcome capture: acceptances, rejections and owner reviews are recorded by the kernel, corrections and downstream results by Magnus through `bundle.record_outcome`. But `bundle_write` took whatever `verified` Magnus wrote, so a run could record an owner's review no one gave, or drop one given; and a convention needed a non-empty `verified`, so a new one could be written only with an invented entry.
- Now the record is kept by the kernel from version to version: Magnus's write carries it back unchanged or leaves it out (the kernel puts it back), and a write adding, changing or dropping an entry is refused with the reason. A new convention starts with an empty record. Recording outcomes (`bundle.record_outcome`, acceptances and rejections) is step 8 of the plan, still to build.
- An admin's write in the console is unchanged: it is a person's, recorded as theirs, and stands in for the review queue until step 8.
- Test: `a_conventions_verifications_are_the_kernels_record_and_no_write_of_magnus_makes_one` (org_context_bundle.rs): Magnus writes a new convention with no entry; its write adding Carol's review is refused; with Carol's review on the record, its rewrite with an empty list is refused and one leaving it out keeps the review. On master the first write is refused ("needs verified").

## An app is added only if people sign in to it themselves (2026-11-03, `add-by-sign-in`)

- The connectors spec, Remote MCP connectors: "A server that asks for a key, token or client details instead of a sign-in is refused, and the admin is told why"; Client registration: asking for client details "is excluded because it breaks plug and play. A server that supports none of the three cannot be added". The console asked for "The organisation's key, if it takes one", the kernel stored it and called the app with it, and it took a `client_id` for an app whose sign-in registers no client.
- Now `add_connector` (the console's add and the owner's in the app) refuses a key, token or client ID; a server that answers 401 without saying where to sign in (it wants a key); and a server whose sign-in lets no client register itself, since Alpha Vector's own clients are for the catalog's providers. Each refusal says why. The console's form no longer has the key field. Client ID Metadata Documents come with their own pull request, so a server offering only that is refused until then.
- A server that asks for no sign-in at all is still added and called without a credential: the spec keeps such a service out of the catalog but says nothing of one an admin adds. Open with the owner.
- Apps added with a key before this keep working, and their key can still be rotated or revoked; none can be added.
- `admin_connectors.rs` added an app with the organisation's key and rotated it: the behaviour the spec refuses. It now asserts each refusal, adds an app people sign in to, and rotates Google's secret instead.
- Test: `the_console_sets_rotates_and_revokes_the_organisations_credentials_and_never_shows_one` (admin_connectors.rs, real Keycloak): a key, a client ID, a server answering 401 with no sign-in and one with no client registration are refused with their reasons, and none is listed; the app added by address signs people in themselves. On master the key is taken (201).
- An app's address with a user, password, query or fragment is refused before the app is asked anything (review, 2026-11-04).
