Reason: a key or token could ride in the address, where the key field's refusal never saw it: the address was fetched, recorded in `ConnectorAdded`, listed by the API, and written into connection errors. Apps added before this keep their address. Test: `an_app_address_holding_a_secret_is_refused` (connectors.rs).

## Each vault entry opens only under its own name (2026-11-03, `vault-binding`)

- The connectors spec, Custody: tokens are "encrypted at rest in the appliance's vault, under a key that never leaves the appliance, bound to one user and one connector". Every entry was encrypted under the vault's one key with nothing tying it to its name, so Bob's sign-in to an app, copied in the vault's file over Alice's, opened as Alice's and her runs would act as Bob.
- Now each entry is encrypted with its name as associated data (XChaCha20-Poly1305), and marked as such; under any other name it does not open. Entries written before, or restored from a backup made before, are bound to their names when the kernel takes the vault, never when it is only opened: a new release's check before an update opens the running release's vault and must leave it as it is (review of #115).
- Going back a release (the owner's rollback, or the automatic one when a new release does not start) seals every entry again as releases before binding read them, from the newer release before the earlier one starts; the vault stays sealed until then, so nothing is stored bound in between, and a release that knows binding binds them again when it starts. If going back fails, the running release binds them again.
- "Under a key that never leaves the appliance" is still open: a backup carries the vault's key, so tokens restore with it. Keeping the key on the appliance means a restored appliance asks everyone to sign in again; that choice is the owner's.
- A backup's restore test opens each entry as the vault would (`vault::check`), bound or from before.
- Tests (vault.rs): `a_sign_in_moved_under_another_name_does_not_open` (on master Bob's token opens as Alice's) `an_entry_from_before_binding_is_bound_only_when_the_kernel_takes_the_vault` (before, opening the vault rewrote it) and `going_back_a_release_leaves_the_vault_as_the_earlier_release_reads_it`.

## An app that refuses Magnus its tools is said to (2026-11-03, `app-refused`)

- On strix-alpha, Gmail, Calendar, Drive and Chat were all signed in, yet Google answered every request for their tools with HTTP 403 (its tool list still in the body; the same request with no sign-in gets 200). Magnus logged a warning, offered none of their tools, and still told the agent each was "Connected." Asked to send an email, it found no Gmail tool, tried gmail.com in the browser, and reported "a tool-access problem in that run" (owner, 2026-11-03: "gmail connector failed a tool call").
- Now the kernel keeps, per connection, the HTTP status the app answered with when it last refused Magnus its tools, in Magnus's own words (`apps::refusal`). Nothing the app wrote, neither its body nor its `WWW-Authenticate` challenge, reaches the agent: what comes from an app is untrusted, and this path does not pass the boundary's taint (review of #116); the log keeps both. A failed connection is no refusal and is not said to be. The person's app index says "Connected, but the app refused Magnus when asked for its tools (HTTP 403 Forbidden), so none of them can be used now", telling the agent to say so plainly and not to try another way such as the browser; `app_details` says the same. While an app refuses, none of its tools is offered, not even those pinned from an earlier listing, so the index and the tools agree (review of #116). It clears the next time the app lists its tools.
- A run's tools are gathered before its context, so the first model call already knows of a refusal (asking the app for its tools is how Magnus learns it).
- The log's refusal keeps the app's `WWW-Authenticate` challenge, which Magnus dropped before, so the reason for a 403 can be read.
- Why Google refuses is still open: its setup guides say each Workspace MCP server needs its own MCP service enabled in the Google Cloud project (`gmailmcp`, `calendarmcp`, `drivemcp`, `chatmcp.googleapis.com`) and the project enrolled in the Workspace Developer Preview; that is the owner's to check. Gmail moves to a brokered connector on Gmail's own API (owner, 2026-11-03), which needs neither.
- Tests: `an_app_that_refuses_magnus_its_tools_is_said_to_refuse_from_the_first_step` (agent_apps.rs; the test app, once listed and its tool pinned, answers tools/list with 403, its list and a challenge carrying an instruction, as Google answered with its list; on master the index says "Connected." and nothing more, and before the review's fix the challenge reached the agent) and `a_refusal_says_only_its_status_never_what_the_app_wrote` (apps.rs).

## How StrixAlpha is reached through pyrallon.ai (2026-11-03, `remote-access`)

- The owner asked how to put pyrallon.ai and a Cloudflare tunnel in front of StrixAlpha. `docs/remote-access-and-relay.md` is the plan. No tunnel, DNS record, Access application, private route or Worker was created by that plan. The owner has since brought the tunnel up; the Access application on the hostname is replaced by device certificates, below.
- `magnus.pyrallon.ai` is the appliance's HTTPS port 7700. The plain port 7701, the admin console on 7443, Keycloak's admin port 19446, Postgres and SSH are not public hostnames. The console stays on Tailscale, as the admin spec requires. SSH, if wanted from outside, is a private route to 192.168.0.50/32, not an ingress.
- The OAuth relay is a Worker at `https://pyrallon.ai/v1/connections/callback`, not a process on StrixAlpha. It is not implemented: the connectors spec marks the handoff to the appliance as proposed, not ruled, and the appliance registry is still unspecified. A Worker that forwarded authorization codes without that would be an open redirect. Provider consoles stay on the loopback redirect until both sides speak the relay.

## A paired computer carries its own certificate (2026-11-03, `remote-access`)

- Samuel: people reach the appliance from anywhere at `https://magnus.pyrallon.ai`, with no Cloudflare, WARP, or other account of their own. Cloudflare carries the bytes. Magnus does identity. Each computer is paired once, and a lost one can be revoked. This replaces the plan, the same day, that left the desktop app on the LAN and put Cloudflare Access in front of the hostname.
- The certificate is signed by Cloudflare's managed client-certificate CA (`POST /zones/{zone_id}/client_certificates`). A certificate the appliance signed would not pass the edge, and a CA of our own is Enterprise only. The key is generated in the desktop app and never leaves it; the appliance stores the fingerprint and Cloudflare's certificate id in `state/accounts/devices.json`, not in the log. The token in the vault (`cloudflare_mtls`) is zone-scoped, SSL and Certificates Write. Pairing is refused through the tunnel, so the answer is not a page Cloudflare's edge decrypts: the first computer pairs on the LAN or Tailscale.
- The edge blocks a missing certificate and a revoked one. `cert_verified` stays true after revocation, so the WAF rule has to name `cert_revoked` too. A Transform Rule copies the fingerprint into `x-magnus-client-cert`, overwriting the client's value. The origin believes that header only from loopback with `Cf-Connecting-Ip`. The LAN is unchanged and keeps pinning.
- The desktop trusts the public roots for `magnus.pyrallon.ai` and presents the device certificate. Every other address still pins. The session is the one the app already keeps per address: sign in once on that name, then the keyring answers. No VM-connectors pull request was open to coordinate with; the only open one was this branch.

## A squash-merged pull request stops no later build (2026-11-03, `publish-after-squash`)

- When the build in a channel is a pull request that merged, the next build must hold the master commit it landed as (CI asks GitHub for the merge commit and passes it as `MAGNUS_LANDED`, `number=commit`), not its head. Reason: #119 was squash-merged, so master held its work in a new commit but never its head 6d4343c, and every later pull request's publish failed (#120 first). A merge commit holds the head, so this asks nothing more of a pull request merged the usual way. Test: `release_channels.rs`, `a_pull_request_squash_merged_stops_no_later_build`.

## A brokered connector's server image is named by its digest and built reproducibly (2026-11-03, `brokered-image`)

- The connectors spec, Brokered API connectors: "Brokered connectors are MCP servers Alpha Vector builds over a provider's public API, run on the appliance, and fed by the same credential plane as everything else." "Packaging. Each brokered connector is one server image per provider, identified in its manifest by digest." "Credentials. ... The server never sees a refresh token, holds no state between calls, and writes nothing to disk." "Tools. ... with input and output schemas validated on both sides." Owner, 2026-11-03: Google's hosted Gmail server cannot send email, so Gmail becomes the first brokered connector; owner, 2026-11-03, on brokered against hosted: "the case for brokered is exactly why the VM's exist". This pull request is the image and the manifest; the runtime that boots it is the next (`brokered-runtime`), and Gmail the one after.
- The manifest: `kind = "brokered"`, its `endpoint` the image's digest (`sha256:<hex>`), `hosts` the provider API hosts and `issuer` the provider's authorization server. Reason: the spec's field table says "endpoint ... Brokered: the server image digest", so a manifest keeps one shape; the brief for this work named a separate `image` field. `hosts` and `issuer` are not in the spec's table; the first is what "the provider API hosts named in its manifest" needs, the second where people sign in, since no MCP server of the provider's says so (`catalog::kind_problem`). Until the runtime lands every catalog manifest is still `remote` (`every_manifest_is_well_formed`), so none is called as a remote app at its digest.
- The image is an initramfs (a newc cpio) whose `/init` is the server, a static musl program: the microVM it boots has no disk at all, so "writes nothing to disk" holds by construction. `brokered/build.sh` builds it reproducibly (the pinned toolchain, now with the musl target in `rust-toolchain.toml` so every build box has it, the lockfile, build paths remapped, symbols stripped, fixed times and owners): built in two target directories it has one digest.
- The server kit (`brokered/kit`, Rust, for a static program with no runtime in the image) answers MCP on vsock with MCP's stdio framing (newline-delimited JSON-RPC), one session per connection; takes the access token from each `tools/call` (`params._meta["io.magnus/access-token"]`) and keeps nothing between calls; checks each call's input against its tool's input schema and its answer against its output schema (`schema::check`: type, properties, required, additionalProperties false, items, enum; the appliance uses the same checker on its side); and reaches the provider only by HTTPS CONNECT to the host's egress proxy on vsock, TLS end to end in the server (next bullet). `brokered/test-server` is the image the runtime's tests boot. The tracker's line names TypeScript on the official MCP SDK; a TypeScript SDK is not built.
- The kit's way out is TLS, and an answer cut short is an error (review of #120, 2026-11-03: the kit handed a server the proxy's plain tunnel, so `exchange` sent the request and its bearer token in the clear past the proxy; and it read an answer by discarding read errors, so a truncated body came back as a response). `https(target)` is the only way out a server is given (`tunnel` is no longer public; `exchange` takes only `Https`): through the proxy, then a TLS handshake (rustls 0.23 with ring, the public roots of webpki-roots, as the Gmail server already did), and nothing is sent until the provider has shown a certificate for its name from a trusted root. Roots beside the public ones come from the appliance only, on the kernel command line (`magnus_roots`, which the kernel hands to `/init` as its environment), for an organisation whose egress inspects TLS or a test's stand-in. An answer is whole only by its framing: a read error is passed up; a TLS peer's end without its closing alert (rustls's unexpected end) is accepted only when the body is complete; a body shorter than its `Content-Length` is an error; a chunked body must end with its zero-length chunk; one delimited only by the end must end cleanly; 1xx, 204 and 304 have no body (RFC 9112, 6.3).
- Tests: `brokered_image.rs` `a_server_image_is_built_reproducibly_and_named_by_its_digest` (two builds, one digest, the file's SHA-256; `/init` alone, static, root's, time 0; without the remapped paths the two builds differ); the kit's `a_session_runs_a_call_only_with_its_token_and_schemas` and `an_exchange_sends_the_token_and_reads_a_chunked_answer`, now run in CI (`desktop.yml`); `a_value_is_checked_against_its_schema`; `a_brokered_manifest_names_its_image_and_hosts` and `an_image_is_named_by_its_full_digest`. The review's fixes: `nothing_is_sent_until_the_provider_proves_its_name` (real TLS on a local host: a host in the clear, one with an unknown CA's certificate and one with another name's are each refused before any request, and none sees the token; one with a trusted certificate for its name gets the request and answers; with a verifier that accepts any certificate, or with the request sent on the plain connection as before, it fails); `a_read_that_fails_is_an_error`, `a_body_shorter_than_its_length_is_an_error` and `a_chunked_body_without_its_last_chunk_is_an_error` (each fails with the reading as it was). The kit's tests are kept in `tests/brokered-kit/kit.rs`, its `tests` module by path, with the repository's other tests.

## Brokered connectors run on the appliance, one microVM per person per connector (2026-11-03, `brokered-runtime`)

- The connectors spec, Brokered API connectors: "Brokered connectors are MCP servers Alpha Vector builds over a provider's public API, run on the appliance, and fed by the same credential plane as everything else." "Isolation. One instance per user per connector, each in its own Firecracker microVM, started when the agent first calls it and stopped when idle. User data never shares a process." "Credentials. The appliance injects a short-lived access token into each request. The server never sees a refresh token, holds no state between calls, and writes nothing to disk." "Network. The microVM can reach only the provider API hosts named in its manifest. A connector cannot send a token anywhere else, even if its code is wrong: each microVM goes out only through an egress proxy on the appliance that admits those hosts and nothing else." "Tools. Tools cover the provider's operations at the level a person works (find, read, create, update, share, delete), with input and output schemas validated on both sides." "Testing. Every brokered connector runs contract tests in CI against provider test accounts before its manifest is published." Custody: "Tokens never enter model context and never reach agent sandboxes." Kill switch: engaging it "refuses every connector call". The manifest, the image and the server kit are `brokered-image`'s; this pull request boots them, and Gmail is next.
- The appliance finds an image in `[brokered] images` by its digest, checks the file's SHA-256 before every boot and boots a copy of the bytes it checked.
- Each instance is a Firecracker microVM with the sandbox's kernel and Firecracker, one vCPU, `[brokered] memory_mib` (128: the image is a few megabytes unpacked into memory), no drives and no network device (`vm_config`; `launch` refuses either). It is started at the person's first call to that connector and stopped once unused for `[brokered] idle_s` (300 seconds: a run's calls are minutes apart at most, and an instance boots in about a second), never while a call is in flight. Its tools are listed once per image, from an instance with no way out and no one's token: an image is known by its digest, so what it lists is too, and listing does not start anyone's instance.
- The kernel's `Mcp` client is unchanged: brokered calls go through `brokered.rs`'s own small client for the kit's vsock framing. The access token goes with each `tools/call`, in `params._meta["io.magnus/access-token"]`, from the boundary's credential stage (refreshed there as for any app); the refresh token never leaves the vault. What comes back is cleaned of the token as `Mcp::clean` cleans a remote app's answer, before the boundary cleans it again.
- Schemas: the appliance checks each call's input before sending it and the structured answer after, with the server kit's checker (`schema::check`), as the server checks both too.
- Egress: the guest's only way out is vsock to the host, which Firecracker sends to `v.sock_<port>` beside the instance; only the proxy's port is bound. The proxy reads one `CONNECT host:port` and admits it only when it is one of the manifest's hosts, exactly (a host alone is its port 443); anything else is answered 403 and logged. Upstream is reached from the appliance. TLS runs end to end in the guest, so the proxy never sees the provider's traffic.
- Sign-in is the provider's own OAuth 2.0 with PKCE at the manifest's `issuer`, with no resource indicator: RFC 8707's resource names an MCP server a token is for, and a provider's API (Google's) takes the plain request. Everything else about the sign-in, its refresh, Disconnect and revocation is the remote connectors' code.
- Disconnecting stops that person's instance of that connector; deleting a person stops all of theirs; the kill switch stops every one, before its revocations.
- Roots: the server's TLS (the kit's `https`) trusts the public roots and those `[brokered] roots` names (DER files: an organisation whose egress inspects TLS, or a test's stand-in), given to the microVM on the kernel command line as `magnus_roots`, which the kernel hands to `/init` as its environment. A file named there that cannot be read stops the config loading. It widens nothing a microVM may reach: the proxy still admits only its manifest's hosts.
- Review of #122 (2026-11-03), two fixes:
  - A brokered connector cannot say it deduplicates. A configured connector without `idempotency` is already `none` (`config::header` returns "none"), but one could say `header`, and the boundary would resend a call whose outcome is unknown (or an intent a crash left) as if its server dropped repeats, though a brokered call carries no idempotency key. Of refusing, treating `header` as `none`, or carrying the key, refusing is the smallest that is correct and says so: such a config does not load ("carry no idempotency key"), so an unknown outcome is always put to the person. Carrying the key would not help: the servers' providers (Gmail's API) take none.
  - A stop fences the calls that wait for it. A call took its token, then could wait on the instances' lock while a stop (the kill switch, Disconnect, deletion) took it first, stopped everything and returned; the call then booted a new microVM with the token it held. Now each `stop` counts its scope (connector, person, both or neither) under that lock, the boundary reads the count for the call's connector and person before it takes the token, and the call goes ahead only if, once it has the lock and again just before it sends, the count is unchanged, the kill switch is off and the person is still signed in.
- Not done here: the jailer for these microVMs (the sandbox's are unjailed too, PROGRESS.md); shipping images in the appliance release (`release/to-appliance.sh`) with the first catalog connector that needs one; the signed catalog service the spec has images published with.
- Tests: `brokered.rs` (real Firecracker, the test server image built from the checkout, a local HTTPS host standing in for the provider's API, with a certificate for `127.0.0.1` from the test's own CA in `[brokered] roots`, and FakeOAuthApp for its authorization server, which now takes the plain request for a provider's API):
  - `each_person_has_their_own_microvm_called_with_their_current_token_reaching_only_its_hosts`: Alice's two calls run in one microVM and Carol's in another; each reaches the provider over TLS (nothing before the handshake carries a token) with that person's token current then (every call refreshed, four different access tokens, none a refresh token); a server answering with its token shows the model none of it; through the proxy a host not in the manifest is refused (403, logged), a direct connection is unreachable, another host port refuses, and the other host is never reached; both stop once idle, and Alice's next call boots a new one.
  - `a_connector_sends_its_token_only_over_tls_it_has_checked` (the review of #120): a manifest host answering HTTP in the clear and one with a certificate from a CA the appliance does not trust are each refused before any request ("could not reach 127.0.0.1 securely"), and neither is sent a token; it fails on the kit's plain tunnel as it was.
  - `disconnecting_erasing_and_the_kill_switch_stop_the_persons_microvm` (real Keycloak for Carol's account): three running; Alice disconnects, two; Carol is deleted, one; the kill switch, none.
  - `a_call_waiting_while_its_microvm_is_stopped_boots_nothing` (brokered.rs, deterministic: the lock held, the stop queued on it first and the call after; without the check it goes on to boot) and `a_brokered_connector_cannot_claim_to_deduplicate` (config.rs; without the refusal the config loads).
  - Unit: `the_proxy_admits_only_the_manifests_hosts_exactly`, `an_instance_has_no_network_device_and_no_disk`; `a_brokered_manifest_names_its_image_and_hosts` now also maps the manifest to a brokered connector. On `brokered-image` the brokered connector is called as a remote one at `sha256:…` and every call fails.

## Slack's sign-in comes back through the appliance's public name (2026-11-04, `slack-https-redirect`)

- Slack app `A0CE08M8X25` has to be publicly distributed so a workspace other than Veyra (`T0BQJ3JUE01`) can install it. Slack requires every redirect of a distributed app to be https, so `http://127.0.0.1:17321/callback` cannot stay registered. The address Slack is given, and the address the token exchange repeats, is `https://magnus.pyrallon.ai/oauth/slack/callback`.
- The appliance answers that path with a 302 to `http://127.0.0.1:17321/callback`, copying only `code`, `state`, `iss` and `error`, and only when `state` is a Slack sign-in still waiting. The pending sign-in stays. The desktop, which is already listening there, posts the code to the appliance as it does for every other app, and the exchange uses the https address stored when the sign-in started. The refresh token stays in the vault. The microVM still receives only the access token, and still reaches only `slack.com`. Completing the exchange in the relay was the other way; it would put the code handler on the public path and skip the desktop's check that the answer matches the sign-in it started. The bounce keeps that check.
- The answer page is empty. The code is not written to the log. A state that is not waiting is a 404 with an empty body. `GET /oauth/slack/callback` is let through without a device certificate, the same as health, because the person's browser does not have the paired computer's key. A POST to that path is still refused.
- Cloudflare, which the owner applies (this change does not call Cloudflare). The WAF custom rule that blocks `magnus.pyrallon.ai` without a verified client certificate becomes: `(http.host eq "magnus.pyrallon.ai" and cf.tls_client_auth.cert_revoked) or (http.host eq "magnus.pyrallon.ai" and not cf.tls_client_auth.cert_verified and not http.request.uri.path eq "/oauth/slack/callback")`. If an Access application still covers the hostname, add a Bypass policy above the others, Include Everyone, selector URI Path equals `/oauth/slack/callback`. The loopback address is removed from the Slack app before distribution is turned on.
- PKCE stays off. Token rotation stays on.

## Slack is brokered over Slack's Web API (2026-11-04, `slack-brokered`)

- The brokered server calls Slack's Web API (`https://slack.com/api`) directly. It does not proxy `https://mcp.slack.com/mcp`. Reason: Gmail and Drive are Alpha Vector's own server over the provider's ordinary API, pinned by digest, with a host list and tools this repository classes. Slack's hosted MCP server is a second product. Enabling "Slack MCP Server" on the app added no scopes. That server's search tools need `search:read.public`, `search:read.private`, `search:read.im` and `search:read.mpim`, which this app does not have. The scopes it does have (`search:read`, the history and read scopes, `chat:write`, `users:read`) are what `search.messages`, `conversations.history`, `conversations.list`, `conversations.replies`, `chat.postMessage` and `users.info` take. File upload through the hosted server posts bytes to a URL Slack returns, which is not a fixed host. The microVM's allow-list is `slack.com` only. The switch can stay on; Magnus does not call that server, and it is not required.
- Posting is as the person, so the token is a user token (`xoxp`), not a bot token. Bot scopes are removed from the app: with both, `oauth.v2.access` returns the bot token at the top and the person's underneath, and a bot posts as the app. The sign-in uses the user-token endpoints Slack's metadata at `https://mcp.slack.com` names (`https://slack.com/oauth/v2_user/authorize`, `https://slack.com/api/oauth.v2.user.access`). `slack.com` itself publishes no authorization-server metadata (404 on 2026-11-04). Those endpoints return the user token as `access_token`. If an answer also carries a bot token, the user token is the one kept (`granted`). `users:read.email` is not asked for: no tool reads an email address.
- PKCE stays off. Reason: Slack's own note, enabling PKCE marks the app a public client and cannot be undone without Slack. A public client must not send the client secret, and its refresh tokens expire in 30 days. Magnus holds the secret on the appliance and sends it at the token exchange and on refresh, the same as Google's confidential path. The metadata advertises S256; the manifest says `pkce = false`, so the sign-in sends no code challenge and no verifier. Token rotation stays on: access tokens last 12 hours, and a rotated refresh token replaces the old one in one vault write, which the kernel already does. A refresh Slack refuses with HTTP 200 and `ok: false` (`invalid_refresh_token` and the same) marks the sign-in lapsed; Slack does not use an HTTP client error for that.
- The redirect is `http://127.0.0.1:17321/callback`, and no other. Reason: Slack matches a redirect exactly, including the port. The desktop app's free port is what Google's Desktop client accepts and what Slack will not. `http://localhost:8080/oauth/slack/callback` is the wrong host (`localhost` and `127.0.0.1` differ to Slack), the wrong port and the wrong path. Distribution stays off. Reason: a distributed app must use an HTTPS redirect. The appliance's public name is `https://magnus.pyrallon.ai`, and that name asks the browser for the device certificate (PR 119). The certificate's key never leaves the desktop app, so the system browser cannot complete that redirect. The desktop app listens on the loopback address, then posts the code to the appliance over the connection it already authenticated. An internal app can be installed to its own workspace (`T0BQJ3JUE01`) by that sign-in. Turning distribution on would reject this redirect.
- Classes: the reads are `read`. `post_message` is `irreversible`, because the people in the conversation are told and the message is not taken back. Audience `public`. Label `private+untrusted`.
- The client secret is `SLACK_CLIENT_SECRET` in GitHub Actions, put in the appliance vault by `release/admin-on-appliance.sh` the same way as `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` (`PUT /v1/registrations/slack`). It is not in the repository. On strix-alpha, that secret has to be set on the repository and the next master build has to provision it; nothing else on the machine, and no Cloudflare change.
- Not done here: Slack's hosted MCP server, canvases, files and lists; distribution and an HTTPS redirect, which wait on a path the browser can open without the device certificate; a Slack test account in CI (the contract test runs every tool once `MAGNUS_SLACK_TEST_REFRESH_TOKEN`, `MAGNUS_SLACK_CLIENT_SECRET` and `MAGNUS_SLACK_TEST_CHANNEL` are given).

## A Gmail write answered with something other than JSON is an unknown outcome (2026-11-04, `gmail-write-outcome`)

- Review of Gmail against the brokered rule. What holds: the catalog manifest is `kind = "brokered"`, its image is named by digest and built by `brokered/build.sh`, the microVM's allow-list is `gmail.googleapis.com` only, the appliance injects the person's access token on each call and never a refresh token, refresh stays in the kernel, the only scope is `gmail.modify`, and the server calls Gmail's REST API, not Google's hosted MCP server. Sending a draft and deleting one stay irreversible.
- What did not hold: `brokered/gmail` still parsed a success body itself and, when it was not JSON, said the write had not happened (`Failure::NotDone`). The review of #126 fixed that in `brokered/google` (an unreadable success after a write is an unknown outcome, so it is not retried as if nothing was done). Gmail was left on its own copy, so a draft Gmail had sent, answered with something unreadable, could be sent again. `read_body` now follows the same rule. Moving Gmail onto `brokered/google` stays its own pull request, as Calendar's decision said.
- Why the docs said Gmail's completion fails in the desktop app: Google's authorization server sets `authorization_response_iss_parameter_supported`, so the kernel refuses an answer that names no issuer. Until `connector-sign-in-iss` (2026-11-02) the desktop shell's `loopback_wait` returned only `code` and `state`, and every Google completion was refused after the person had signed in. The shell now returns `iss` and the Library sends it (`desktop/src-tauri/src/lib.rs`, `desktop/ui/app.js`). `docs/connectors.md` still described the old refusal in four places; those lines now say the app carries `iss` back. Still untested: exchanging a code with Google's token endpoint (it needs a Google test account CI can sign in with). The browser, which has no loopback listener, still says to connect from the desktop app.

## Gmail is brokered: Magnus sends only a draft the person approves (2026-11-03, `gmail-brokered`)

- Owner, 2026-11-03: Google's hosted Gmail MCP server cannot send email, so Gmail becomes a brokered connector over Gmail's REST API, and Magnus sends only drafts the person approves; on brokered against hosted: "the case for brokered is exactly why the VM's exist". The connectors spec, Brokered API connectors: "Tools cover the provider's operations at the level a person works (find, read, create, update, share, delete), with input and output schemas validated on both sides." "Testing. Every brokered connector runs contract tests in CI against provider test accounts before its manifest is published." AtonoRobotics/magnus-claude#117 (not landing in the kernel; its code is the source for the brokered servers) called the same REST API from the kernel; its Gmail code and error handling are ported into the server here.
- The server (`brokered/gmail`, on the server kit) calls `https://gmail.googleapis.com/gmail/v1/users/me/...` and nothing else. Its tools are the hosted server's, as the manifest classed them, plus `update_draft`, `delete_draft`, `send_draft`, `reply_draft` and `forward_draft`; a reply or a forward is written as a draft (a reply in its thread, with `In-Reply-To` and `References`; a forward with the original's text, not its attachments). Left out: the hosted server's `apply_sensitive_message_label` and `apply_sensitive_thread_label`, which move to the trash or to spam (as #117 implemented them), what `trash_*` and `mark_*_spam` already do. An address or subject with a line break is refused, so no header can be added through one.
- Classes: reads are `read`; drafts, labels, trash and spam are `reversible_write`; `send_draft` (sent mail cannot be called back) and `delete_draft` ("Immediately and permanently deletes the specified draft. Does not simply trash it.", Gmail API, drafts.delete) are `irreversible`, so the boundary asks before each. No tool deletes a message for good.
- One scope, `https://www.googleapis.com/auth/gmail.modify`: it is among the authorization scopes of every method the tools call (drafts create, get, list, update, delete, send; messages get, modify, trash, untrash; threads list, get, modify, trash, untrash; labels list, create), and permanent deletion of mail needs `https://mail.google.com/`, which is not asked for. #117 asked for `openid email profile https://mail.google.com/ gmail.modify`; `mail.google.com` would let Magnus delete mail for good, and the OpenID scopes named the account for #117's People call, which nothing here makes. `gmail.modify` is on the project's consent screen (#117) and is a restricted scope: in Testing, 100 users and a refresh token that lasts seven days, until verification (the tracker's Google line).
- Audience `public`, as #117 set it: a sent draft reaches its recipients. Having read a person's mail (private, and written by others), Magnus asks before writing anything to Gmail too (the Rule of Two, unchanged).
- Google's refusals, from #117's `rest::explain`: a 401 is a sign-in to do again; `accessNotConfigured` (or `SERVICE_DISABLED`, "has not been used in project") says an admin turns the Gmail API on in the project's library; `insufficientPermissions`, `ACCESS_TOKEN_SCOPE_INSUFFICIENT` or a `WWW-Authenticate` `insufficient_scope` challenge (Google's granular consent lets a person untick a scope) is the boundary's request for more access, with the app's card; rate limits say when to try again (`Retry-After`); a 404 says it was not found; a write's 5xx, or an answer lost after a write was sent, is an unknown outcome, which the boundary resolves as after a crash, never a failure retried as if nothing happened. The server says which with the result (`_meta["io.magnus/outcome"]`, the kit's `Failure`), and the appliance keeps only scopes the connector's manifest names. #117's Developer Preview message is not ported: the REST API has no preview, and the owner is not joining it (#117). A Testing-mode refresh token that dies after seven days (`invalid_grant`, #117) is already a lapsed sign-in: the kernel's refresh marks one refused with any client error, and the boundary tells the agent to offer the person a new sign-in.
- Sign-in: the catalog's preregistered Google Desktop client (`catalog/registrations.toml`) at the manifest's issuer, `https://accounts.google.com`, with PKCE and no resource indicator. The desktop app's redirect, `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/callback` with a free port, is what a Desktop client accepts on any port (#117, its `docs/oauth-redirects.md`); nothing changes here.
- Shipping: each appliance release now carries the images of `release/brokered-images`, built from its commit, in `brokered-images` beside its programs, where the appliance looks when `[brokered] images` is not set; the release refuses a catalog manifest naming an image its commit does not build (`release/to-appliance.sh`). The manifest's digest is the image built from this commit, checked by `gmail.rs` and `gmail_contract.rs`: a change to the server changes the manifest with it.
- One setting, for a stand-in and for a real network: `[brokered] resolve` (where the proxy reaches a manifest host, instead of DNS). It widens nothing a microVM may ask for: the proxy still admits only its manifest's hosts, and it is the owner's own config. The server reaches Gmail by the kit's way out (`https`, TLS checked for `gmail.googleapis.com` before anything is sent, with the roots of `[brokered] roots` beside the public ones); its own TLS client, before the kit had one, is gone.
- Review of #123 (2026-11-03), two fixes in the server:
  - An edited reply stays a reply. `update_draft` rebuilt the message with no thread and no `In-Reply-To` or `References`, so changing the text of a draft `reply_draft` made cut it off from its thread. It now reads the draft first (`drafts.get`, `format=metadata`) and keeps its `threadId` and those two headers; the edit gives the recipients, subject and text.
  - A draft is read with every recipient. A message's readable form had To and Cc but not Bcc, though both composing tools take Bcc and `send_draft` sends the stored draft as it is: what Magnus shows before the person approves a send must name everyone it goes to, and a read-then-rewrite edit must keep them. `bcc` is now in it (empty for a message received, which carries none).
  - The image changed, so the manifest names the one built from this commit.
- Not done here: Drive, Calendar, Docs, Sheets and Chat (each its own pull request, the same way); the owner's Google test account in CI (the contract test runs every tool once `MAGNUS_GMAIL_TEST_REFRESH_TOKEN`, `MAGNUS_GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` and `MAGNUS_GMAIL_TEST_ADDRESS` are given; without them it checks only that Gmail is reached and an unknown token is said as a sign-in to do again); attachments (reading their bytes, sending them).
- Tests: `gmail.rs` (real Firecracker, the image built from this checkout and named by the manifest, a local HTTPS host standing in for `gmail.googleapis.com` with a certificate for that name from the test's own CA, and FakeOAuthApp for Google's sign-in):
  - `gmail_sends_a_draft_only_once_the_person_approves_it_reaching_only_gmail`: with nothing read before, the send asks Alice, nothing reaches Gmail until she approves, then exactly the send; every request went to Gmail's name by TLS and `Host`, with her token, and the proxy refused nothing. With `send_draft` classed `reversible_write` it fails.
  - `having_read_mail_gmail_labels_and_drafts_without_asking_and_asks_only_before_the_send` (named `having_read_mail_gmail_asks_before_the_draft_and_again_before_the_send` when this was written).
  - `gmail_s_refusals_reach_the_boundary_as_what_they_mean`: an `insufficient_scope` challenge becomes the request for access, a 503 on the send an unknown outcome; without the appliance's reading of the server's outcome it fails.
  - `gmail_contract.rs` `gmail_s_tools_do_what_they_say_against_gmail` (Gmail itself), and the server's own `gmails_refusals_are_said_as_what_to_do`, `every_tool_is_classed_in_the_manifest`, `an_email_is_written_as_gmail_takes_it_and_no_header_can_be_added`, `a_draft_is_read_with_every_recipient`, `an_edited_reply_stays_a_reply` (each fails without its fix), `a_message_is_read_as_a_person_reads_it`, `every_tool_names_its_schemas`; the kit's `a_session_runs_a_call_only_with_its_token_and_schemas` now with the outcomes; `a_release_brings_its_server_images`. The server's tests run in CI (`desktop.yml`, `-p magnus-gmail`).
  - `google.rs`: Gmail's sign-in at Google now asks for `gmail.modify`; its tools are checked against its image, not a live server; its card says it sends a draft when allowed (and `connectors.mjs`).

## Google Calendar is brokered (2026-11-03, `google-calendar-brokered`)

- Google's hosted Calendar MCP server refuses Magnus with 403, as its Gmail server did (the Developer Preview the owner is not joining, #117), so Calendar does not work in the owner's app. The owner wants all of #117's Google connectors; Calendar is the first after Gmail, brokered the same way, with #117's `google.rs` the source for its tools and its refusals.
- The server (`brokered/calendar`, on the server kit) calls `https://www.googleapis.com/calendar/v3/...` and nothing else. Its tools are the manifest's nine, at the level a person works: `list_calendars`, `list_events`, `search_events`, `get_event`, `suggest_time` (free times of a length between two times, from `freeBusy` for the person and those named, on the quarter hour), `create_event`, `update_event` (only the fields given), `delete_event`, `respond_to_event` (the person's own answer, every other guest kept, as Calendar replaces the list whole). Each change tells the people invited unless the call says `notify: false` (`sendUpdates`).
- Classes: reads are `read`; the four changes are `irreversible`, as the manifest already classed them: an invitation, a change or a cancellation is sent to its guests, who may be anyone, and cannot be called back. So the boundary asks before each. No tool shares a calendar or changes who can see it (`acl`), which the card says.
- One scope, `https://www.googleapis.com/auth/calendar`: the one Calendar scope on the project's consent screen (#117: openid, email, profile, `https://mail.google.com/`, gmail.modify, drive, calendar, documents, spreadsheets), and among the authorization scopes of every method the tools call (calendarList.list, events list, get, insert, patch, delete, freebusy.query). The narrower scopes the hosted server advertised (`calendar.calendarlist.readonly`, `calendar.events`, `calendar.events.freebusy`) also cover the tools, but are not on the consent screen. `calendar` could also share a calendar; no tool does. The OpenID scopes #117 added named the account for its People call, which nothing here makes.
- What Google's Calendar, Drive, Docs, Sheets and Chat servers share is one crate, `brokered/google`: a request to a Google REST API as the person over the kit's way out (TLS checked for the API's name), and #117's refusals as Gmail's server says them, with the API's name (`explain`): a 401 is a sign-in to do again, `accessNotConfigured` an API an admin turns on, a scope refused the boundary's request for more access, a rate limit when to try again, a 404 not found, a write's 5xx or an answer lost after a write was sent an unknown outcome. A POST that only asks (`freeBusy`) changes nothing, so without an answer nothing was done. Gmail keeps its own copy for now; moving it onto the crate is its own pull request.
- The workspace takes every crate under `brokered/` (`brokered/*`), so each connector's crate joins it without another line.
- Shipping: `magnus-calendar` is in `release/brokered-images`, so each release carries its image, and the manifest names the image built from this commit (checked by `calendar.rs` and `calendar_contract.rs`). Its tests and the crate's run in CI (`desktop.yml`).
- Review of #126 (2026-11-03), three fixes:
  - An answer of success that is not JSON, after a write, is an unknown outcome. `brokered/google` said it as not done, so an event Calendar had added, answered with something unreadable, could be added again on a retry, inviting its guests twice. A read's stays not done (`Api::json`).
  - A group Google could not list is named as not checked. `freeBusy` reports a group it could not expand (`groupTooBig`, `notFound`, `internalError`) under `groups`, apart from `calendars`; `suggest_time` read only `calendars`, so a group's members' times were taken as free. They are in `not_checked` now.
  - Every calendar comes back. `list_calendars` returned the first page and dropped the next page's token; it now reads page after page, 250 a page, up to 20 pages (5,000 calendars), and says whether there were more (`more`).
  - The image changed, so the manifest names the one built from this commit. Tests: `an_answer_that_is_not_json_after_a_write_is_an_unknown_outcome`, `a_group_google_could_not_list_is_named_as_not_checked`, `calendar_finds_a_time_when_everyone_is_free` (now with a group Google could not list), `calendar_lists_every_calendar_across_pages`; each fails without its fix.
- Not done here: Drive, Docs, Sheets and Chat (each its own pull request, the same way); the owner's Google test account in CI (the contract test runs every tool once `MAGNUS_GOOGLE_TEST_REFRESH_TOKEN`, a refresh token for the consent screen's scopes, and `MAGNUS_GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` are given; without them it checks only that Calendar is reached and an unknown token is said as a sign-in to do again); recurring events changed as a series (each tool works on one occurrence, `singleEvents`); attachments and conference links.
- Tests: `calendar.rs` (real Firecracker, the image built from this checkout and named by the manifest, a local HTTPS host standing in for `www.googleapis.com` with a certificate for that name from the test's own CA, `stand_in.rs`, and FakeOAuthApp for Google's sign-in):
  - `calendar_adds_an_event_only_once_the_person_approves_it_reaching_only_google`: with nothing read before, adding an event asks Alice, nothing reaches Calendar until she approves, then exactly the add, telling its guests, with only the fields given; every request went to Google's name by TLS and `Host`, with her token, and the proxy refused nothing. With `create_event` classed `reversible_write` it fails.
  - `calendar_finds_a_time_when_everyone_is_free`: one `freeBusy` for her and Bob, and the free hours between their busy ones, asking nothing. With busy times ignored it fails.
  - `calendar_s_refusals_reach_the_boundary_as_what_they_mean`: an `insufficient_scope` challenge becomes the request for access, a 503 on the add an unknown outcome. Without either reading in `explain` it fails.
  - `calendar_contract.rs` `calendar_s_tools_do_what_they_say_against_calendar` (Calendar itself; with the account, every tool on one event it adds a week on, with the account its only guest and no one told, then deletes); the server's own `every_tool_is_classed_in_the_manifest`, `an_answer_keeps_everyone_else_invited` (fails without its fix), `an_event_is_written_as_calendar_takes_it`, `an_event_is_read_as_a_person_reads_it`, `times_are_read_and_written_as_rfc_3339`, `free_times_fall_between_busy_ones`, `every_tool_names_its_schemas`; the crate's `google_s_refusals_are_said_as_what_to_do` (fails without its fix) and `a_query_carries_only_what_has_a_value`.
  - `google.rs`: Calendar's sign-in at Google asks for `calendar`; its tools are checked against its image, not a live server.

## Google Chat is brokered (2026-11-03, `google-chat-brokered`)

- Google's hosted Chat MCP server refuses Magnus with 403, as its Gmail server did (#117), so Chat does not work in the owner's app. The owner wants all of #117's Google connectors; Chat is brokered as Gmail is, with #117's `google.rs` the source for its tools (`list_messages`, `search_messages`, `search_conversations`, `send_message`) and its refusals.
- The server (`brokered/chat`, on the server kit) calls `https://chat.googleapis.com/v1/spaces...` and nothing else. Its tools, at the level a person works in Chat: find a conversation (`search_conversations`, by words in its name) and who is in it (`list_members`), read its messages (`list_messages`, newest first, between two times if given; `get_message`), search them (`search_messages`: Chat's API filters messages by time, not words, so it reads up to 500, newest first, and says whether there were more), and send (`send_message`, to a space, in a thread if given, or to a person by email in the conversation already there with them), edit (`update_message`) and delete (`delete_message`) the person's own. Only a name as Chat gives it (`spaces/…`, `spaces/…/messages/…`) goes in a path.
- Classes: reads are `read`; sending, editing and deleting are `irreversible`: everyone in the conversation sees each (an edit is marked as one), and what they saw cannot be called back. So the boundary asks before each. Starting a conversation and adding or removing people are not tools, which the card says. Audience `public`, as #117 set it: a message reaches a conversation's members, who may be outside the organisation.
- Scopes, the narrowest that cover the tools (Chat API reference, each method's authorization scopes): `chat.spaces.readonly` (spaces list, findDirectMessage), `chat.memberships.readonly` (members list) and `chat.messages` (messages list, get, create, patch, delete). #117 and the hosted server asked for `chat.messages.readonly` and `chat.messages.create`, which cannot edit or delete. The original implementation recorded missing consent-screen scopes and Chat app configuration (#117). Existing grants must include the access requested for each operation; writes require Chat app configuration (Google Cloud console, Google Chat API, Configuration). The current read-only live probe is recorded below: Google explicitly says Chat is turned off for the account.
- Integration on current master keeps its existing `brokered/google` and kit, including the unknown-outcome handling added for Calendar; no unrelated Gmail image or upload support changes. Chat's image is rebuilt against those shared crates.
- Shipping: `magnus-chat` is in `release/brokered-images`; the manifest names the image built from this commit (checked by `google_chat.rs` and `google_chat_contract.rs`; `chat.rs` is Magnus's own chat). Its tests run in CI (`desktop.yml`).
- Not done here: the owner's steps above; the owner's Google test account in CI (the contract test runs every tool once `MAGNUS_GOOGLE_TEST_REFRESH_TOKEN`, `MAGNUS_GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` and `MAGNUS_GOOGLE_TEST_CHAT_SPACE`, a space of the account's with no one else in it, are given; without them it checks only that Chat is reached and an unknown token is said as a sign-in to do again); starting conversations, reactions and attachments.
- Tests: `google_chat.rs` (real Firecracker, the image built from this checkout and named by the manifest, a local HTTPS host standing in for `chat.googleapis.com` with a certificate for that name from the test's own CA, `stand_in.rs`, and FakeOAuthApp for Google's sign-in):
  - `chat_sends_a_message_only_once_the_person_approves_it_reaching_only_google`: with nothing read before, the send asks Alice; nothing reaches Chat until she approves, then exactly the message, in its thread; every request went to Chat's name by TLS and `Host`, with her token, and the proxy refused nothing. With `send_message` classed `reversible_write` it fails.
  - `chat_finds_words_across_pages_and_asks_before_telling_someone`: the search reads both pages and keeps only what has the words; the send to Dee asks, and goes to her direct conversation. Reading one page only, it fails.
  - `chat_s_refusals_reach_the_boundary_as_what_they_mean`: an `insufficient_scope` challenge becomes the request for access, a 503 on the delete an unknown outcome.
  - `google_chat_contract.rs` `chat_s_tools_do_what_they_say_against_chat` (Chat itself; with the account, every tool on one message in a space no one else is in, then deleted); the server's own `every_tool_is_classed_in_the_manifest`, `only_chat_s_own_names_go_in_a_path` (fails without its fix), `a_message_is_read_as_a_person_reads_it`, `every_tool_names_its_schemas`; `brokered/google`'s three.
  - `google.rs`: Chat's sign-in at Google asks for `chat.messages`; its tools are checked against its image, not a live server.

## The app asks before an action in its own dialog (2026-11-03, `confirm-in-app`)

- Every question before an action (Disconnect, the kill switch, cancelling a task or a schedule, deleting a message, moving to and emptying the trash, removing an app for everyone, asking the owner to delete one's data) is asked in the app's own `<dialog>` (`ask`), never with `window.confirm`. Reason: owner, 2026-11-03, in the Windows app: "disconnect does not work in the library", and "nothing happens at all" when pressed. The appliance's log had no request from the app. A webview that ignores in-page dialogs answers `confirm` no without showing it (MDN, Window.confirm), so every button behind one did nothing, the kill switch's among them. The dialog's buttons are Back (and Escape) for no, and the action's own name for yes.
- Tests: the browser tests (`connectors.mjs`, `killswitch.mjs`, `schedules.mjs`, `live-cards.mjs`) run with `window.confirm` answering no unseen, as that webview did, and answer the app's dialog. With `confirm` as before, the kill switch is never engaged, the schedule never stopped and the task never cancelled, so they fail. The installed-app tests (`app-trash.mjs`, `app-chat-actions.mjs`, `app-settings.mjs`) answer the app's dialog (`appdriver.mjs`, `answer`) instead of WebDriver's alert, which the app no longer raises. These tests changed because they answered the mechanism that was the bug. Not tested here: the Windows app itself (the owner tests it), and Disconnect pressed in a browser test, since no browser test has a connected app.

## Sentry in the catalog (2026-11-03, `catalog-sentry`)

- Owner, 2026-11-03: "All of the connectors in pr117 need to be added to magnus"; the connectors spec uses a provider's own hosted server where it is adequate. Sentry's (`https://mcp.sentry.dev/mcp`) is: it searches and reads issues, events, traces, logs, replays and profiles, updates issues, and runs Seer and Sentry's other operations.
- Magnus registers itself there (`registration = "dcr"`), checked live 2026-11-03: the server's 401 names its protected-resource metadata, whose authorization server `https://mcp.sentry.dev` offers registration (`/oauth/register`), PKCE S256 and clients with no secret (and CIMD); a registration for `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/callback` with no secret was accepted (201), and the sign-in address built for it was answered with Sentry's consent page ("Magnus | Authorization Request"). Magnus registers the exact address it listens on at each sign-in, so a fixed port is never needed. Alpha Vector's Sentry client in `catalog/registrations.toml` is for sentry.io's own sign-in, which this server does not take; it stays unused.
- Scopes `org:read event:write`: what its tools need (getsentry/sentry-mcp `toolDefinitions.json`, main at 2a81238: the reads need `org:read`, `update_issue` needs `event:write`), of the five its metadata offers; `project:write`, `team:write` and `alerts:write` serve only its project-management operations, reached only through `execute_sentry_tool`. Sentry grants by the skills the person ticks on its own page (Inspect and Seer by default; Triage; Manage Projects & Teams), whatever scope is asked for, so the card says Magnus reaches no more than the person allows there.
- Tools: the 14 it lists at the top level after a sign-in (sentry-mcp `TOP_LEVEL_TOOL_NAMES`, read 2026-11-03), each only when a skill the person granted covers it. Its list needs a sign-in, so it was read from its source, not the live server, and no test checks it for drift until a test account signs in. Classes, which count as the owner's (ruling 5) and are the owner's to review:
  - read (11): `find_organizations`, `find_projects`, `search_issues`, `search_errors`, `search_logs`, `search_traces`, `search_metrics`, `search_profiles`, `search_replays`, `get_sentry_resource`, `search_sentry_tools`.
  - irreversible (3): `update_issue` (assigning notifies the assignee, and resolving or ignoring changes what the organisation sees and is alerted on: it reaches others, as Calendar's event changes do), `analyze_issue_with_seer` (starts Sentry's AI analysis, counted against the organisation's Seer use), `execute_sentry_tool` (runs any of the server's other operations by name, among them deleting alert rules and monitors and creating projects, teams and keys, so its class is the worst it can run; each time, the person sees which operation and with what).
- Audience public: issue changes reach the organisation's members, and `execute_sentry_tool` can make alert rules that notify any address. Label private+untrusted: what comes back is the organisation's error data, written by its apps and their users.
- Test: `sentry_is_in_every_library_and_magnus_registers_itself_at_sentry_s_sign_in` (sentry.rs, against Sentry): Sentry is in carol's Library ready to connect; her sign-in registers Magnus at Sentry for her computer's address, asks for exactly the manifest's access with PKCE for that server, and Sentry answers with its page, not an error. It fails without the manifest (Sentry is not in her Library). Each run registers one client at Sentry, as each person's sign-in does. Its steps are `magnus_scratch::catalog`, for every catalog connector that registers Magnus itself. `every_manifest_is_well_formed` covers the manifest.
- `desktop/tests/connectors.mjs` expected "4 connectors" and an empty Code chip: facts of the catalog before this, not of the Library. It now takes the count and the code services from `catalog/*.toml`, and checks an empty chip with Design & CAD, which no manifest uses.

## Hugging Face in the catalog (2026-11-03, `catalog-huggingface`)

- Owner, 2026-11-03: "All of the connectors in pr117 need to be added to magnus"; the connectors spec uses a provider's own hosted server where it is adequate. Hugging Face's (`https://huggingface.co/mcp`) is for finding and reading: models, datasets, Spaces, papers and documentation, the person's private repositories included. Writing beyond the repositories its own tool creates is not offered by it (a gap to revisit if people need to manage existing repositories).
- Magnus registers itself there (`registration = "dcr"`), checked live 2026-11-03: its protected-resource metadata names `https://huggingface.co`, which offers registration (`/oauth/register`), PKCE S256 and CIMD; its metadata lists only secret-based client authentication, yet a registration for `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/callback` with no secret was accepted (201, `token_endpoint_auth_method: none`), and the sign-in address built for it was answered with a redirect to Hugging Face's login (an unknown client gets 400). Hugging Face documents that a loopback address takes any port. It names no revocation endpoint, so Disconnect deletes the sign-in here and names its Connected applications page.
- Scopes `openid profile read-mcp read-repos`, reading only, of the seven its metadata offers: `openid` and `profile` name the account on the card; `read-mcp` is what its server reads the person's tool settings with (inferred from its name: Hugging Face's scope list does not describe it); `read-repos` reads private repositories. `contribute-repos`, `jobs` and `inference-api`, which its write, job and Space tools need, are not asked for: Magnus reads, and a tool needing more is refused by Hugging Face until the person approves more access.
- Tools: what each person sees depends on their Hugging Face settings. The four it lists to anyone were read from the live server (`hf_whoami`, `hub_repo_search`, `hub_repo_details`, `hf_fs`, each marked read-only); the seven a person may turn on are from its source (huggingface/hf-mcp-server at 1d48f44). A Space's own tools (`gr1_...`), named after the Spaces each person picks, are named by no manifest and stay hidden: Magnus cannot class tools whose names and effects it cannot know. Classes, the owner's to review (ruling 5):
  - read (4): `hf_whoami`, `hub_repo_search`, `hub_repo_details`, `hf_fs`.
  - irreversible (4): `hf_fs_write` (writes and deletes files in repositories, public ones included), `dynamic_space` (sends what it is given to anyone's Space, code Magnus cannot see), `hf_sandbox_exec` (runs a shell command), `hf_sandbox_fs` (writes files in a sandbox).
  - financial (3): `create_repo` (can start a Space on paid hardware), `hf_jobs` (runs jobs on hardware billed by the minute), `hf_sandbox` (starts a sandbox as a job on chosen hardware). They spend the person's money, the class the policy holds most tightly.
- Audience public: the tools a person may turn on publish repositories and send data to anyone's Space. Label private+untrusted: the Hub is written by anyone, and private repositories are the person's.
- Tests (huggingface.rs, against Hugging Face): `hugging_face_is_in_every_library_and_magnus_registers_itself_at_its_sign_in` (through `magnus_scratch::catalog`, as Sentry's: in carol's Library ready, her sign-in registers Magnus for her computer's address with exactly the manifest's access, and Hugging Face answers with its login, not an error; it fails without the manifest, which is not in her Library) and `every_tool_hugging_face_lists_without_a_sign_in_is_classed` (tool drift for the tools it lists to anyone; it fails without the manifest). `every_manifest_is_well_formed` covers the manifest.

## GitLab in the catalog (2026-11-03, `catalog-gitlab`)

- Owner, 2026-11-03: "All of the connectors in pr117 need to be added to magnus"; the connectors spec uses a provider's own hosted server where it is adequate. GitLab's (`https://gitlab.com/api/v4/mcp`, generally available since GitLab 19.5, on every tier) is: projects, issues and work items, merge requests with their diffs, notes and reviews, repository files and commits, pipelines and jobs. GitLab.com only: a self-managed GitLab is at its own address, which a compiled-in manifest cannot know; an admin adds one by its address.
- Magnus registers itself there (`registration = "dcr"`), checked live 2026-11-03: the server's 401 names its protected-resource metadata, whose authorization server `https://gitlab.com` offers registration (`/oauth/register`) and PKCE S256 (no CIMD); its metadata lists only secret-based client authentication, yet a registration for `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/callback` with no secret was accepted (201, `token_endpoint_auth_method: none`, scope `mcp`), and the sign-in address built for it was answered with a redirect to GitLab's sign-in (an unknown client gets 401). GitLab matches a redirect address exactly; Magnus registers the exact address it listens on at each sign-in, so that holds. Limits: GitLab takes 10 registrations an hour from one address, and Magnus registers at each sign-in, so more than ten people connecting within an hour from one office would be refused until the hour passes (to revisit with the relay, which can keep one registration); the test registers once per run. A top-level group or instance can turn MCP access off.
- Scope `mcp`: the one its metadata offers, and the one its tools take.
- Tools: the 44 it lists with its default toolsets (meta, core, merge_requests, work_items, repository, ci; duo_agent_platform for people who have GitLab Duo Agent Platform), from GitLab's tool reference (`docs.gitlab.com/user/model_context_protocol/mcp_server_tools`, read 2026-11-03): its list needs a sign-in, so it was not read from the live server, and no test checks it for drift until a test account signs in. The opt-in toolsets (wikis, code_security, orbit, asked for by a header Magnus does not send) and the older names it no longer lists are named by no manifest and stay hidden. Classes, the owner's to review (ruling 5); anything others see or are told of is irreversible, as Calendar's events and Chat's messages are:
  - read (31): `get_mcp_server_version`, `get_project`, `get_user`, `list_groups`, `list_projects`, `list_project_members`, `list_todos`, `search`, `search_labels`, `semantic_search`, `get_pages_content`, `get_merge_request`, `list_merge_requests`, `get_work_item`, `get_work_item_types`, `get_saved_view_work_items`, `list_work_items`, `list_branches`, `get_repository_file`, `list_repository_tree`, `get_commit`, `list_commits`, `list_releases`, `list_tags`, `get_pipeline`, `get_job`, `get_artifact_file`, `list_pipelines`, `list_duo_agents_and_flows`, `list_duo_sessions`, `get_duo_session`.
  - irreversible (11): `save_project` (creates a project, public if asked), `save_note` (comments, replies and resolves threads), `save_merge_request` (opens or edits a merge request), `save_merge_request_review` (comments, approves, asks Duo to review), `accept_merge_request` (merges), `save_work_item` (opens or edits issues, tasks and epics), `link_work_items`, `add_branch` (can start pipelines), `fork_repository` (creates a project), `add_commit` (commits, deletes files, starts pipelines), `save_pipeline` (runs, retries or cancels a pipeline, which may deploy).
  - financial (1): `start_duo_session` (starts GitLab's own agent, which pushes commits and opens merge requests, paid for in GitLab credits).
  - prohibited (1): `send_duo_session_input`: it is the person's approval of GitLab Duo's plan or tool call, and the approval card could show only the session's number, not what Duo asks; Magnus does not stand in for the person there, who answers in GitLab.
- `prohibited` joins the classes a manifest may give (`catalog.rs` `every_manifest_is_well_formed`, `catalog/README.md`): the kernel's hard deny that no policy or approval lifts (B9), and a prohibited tool is never offered to a run. Until now no catalog manifest needed it.
- Audience public: issues, comments, merge requests and commits reach each project's members and, in a public project, anyone. Label private+untrusted.
- Test: `gitlab_is_in_every_library_and_magnus_registers_itself_at_its_sign_in` (gitlab.rs, against GitLab.com, through `magnus_scratch::catalog`, as Sentry's): in carol's Library ready, her sign-in registers Magnus for her computer's address with exactly `mcp` and PKCE, and GitLab answers with its sign-in, not an error. Red with the appliance built without the manifest ("gitlab is not in carol's Library"), green with it. `every_manifest_is_well_formed` covers the manifest.

## Supabase in the catalog, read only (2026-11-03, `catalog-supabase`)

- Owner, 2026-11-03: "All of the connectors in pr117 need to be added to magnus"; the connectors spec uses a provider's own hosted server where it is adequate. Supabase's (`https://mcp.supabase.com/mcp`) is: projects, tables, migrations, logs, security and performance advice, edge functions and queries.
- Read only (`?read_only=true`): every query runs as Supabase's read-only database user, and its tools that change anything (migrations, deploying functions, creating, pausing and restoring projects, branches, feedback) are not listed. Reason: least access; its writes run SQL and migrations against production databases, and pause or delete what an organisation's apps run on. Widening it is a new manifest version, the owner's call. Supabase's metadata keeps the parameter in the resource it names, so Magnus's check that the metadata is for this address holds (checked live).
- One project (`?project_ref=`) is not set: the project is each person's, and a catalog manifest is the same for everyone, while a field to type it in is what the spec excludes ("no connector shows the user a form"). A person chooses the organisation on Supabase's own page when they sign in, and Magnus asks which project in the conversation (`list_projects`). One project per person needs a setting the Library does not have; open with the owner.
- Magnus registers itself there (`registration = "dcr"`), checked live 2026-11-03: the server's 401 names its protected-resource metadata, whose authorization server `https://api.supabase.com` offers registration (`/platform/oauth/apps/register`) and PKCE S256 (no CIMD). Its clients are confidential: a registration for `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/callback` asking for no secret was accepted (201) and answered with a client secret, which Magnus keeps with the sign-in and sends at the token exchange (`client_secret_post`, which Supabase takes). The sign-in address built for it, with the manifest's scopes, was answered with a redirect to Supabase's authorization page (an unknown client gets 400). Supabase names no revocation endpoint, so Disconnect deletes the sign-in here and names the organisation's apps page.
- Scopes, what its read-only tools call (Supabase's Management API, each endpoint's `x-oauth-scope`, read 2026-11-03), of the thirteen its metadata offers: `organizations:read`, `projects:read`, `database:read` (migrations, types, advisors), `database:write` (every query, read only included, and so listing tables and extensions, goes through `POST /database/query`, which takes it; the server runs them as the read-only user), `analytics:read` (logs), `secrets:read` (the project's keys), `edge_functions:read`, `environment:read` (branches). Whether Supabase narrows a registered client's grant to the scopes asked for is not documented; its manual fallback app needs all of them.
- Tools: the 19 it lists read only with its default feature groups, from Supabase's tool reference (`supabase.com/docs/guides/getting-started/mcp`) and its source (supabase-community/supabase-mcp at f36f74a, read 2026-11-03): its list needs a sign-in, so it was not read from the live server, and no test checks it for drift until a test account signs in. Storage is off by default and named by no manifest. Classes, the owner's to review (ruling 5):
  - read (18): `search_docs`, `list_organizations`, `get_organization`, `list_projects`, `get_project`, `get_cost`, `confirm_cost`, `list_tables`, `list_extensions`, `list_migrations`, `query_logs`, `get_advisors`, `get_project_url`, `get_publishable_keys`, `generate_typescript_types`, `list_edge_functions`, `get_edge_function`, `list_branches`.
  - irreversible (1): `execute_sql`, read only as it is: a query can call a database function or extension with effects outside the database (one that makes a web request, for one), which a read-only transaction does not stop, and it reads whatever the person's role can; the person sees the query each time.
  - `apply_migration`, `deploy_edge_function`, `create_project`, `pause_project`, `restore_project`, the branch operations and feedback are not listed in read-only mode, so no manifest names them.
- Audience public: nothing Magnus sends is kept, but a query can reach any address through an extension. Label private+untrusted: what comes back is the organisation's data, written by its apps' users.
- Test: `supabase_is_in_every_library_read_only_and_magnus_registers_itself_at_its_sign_in` (supabase.rs, against Supabase, through `magnus_scratch::catalog`, as Sentry's): in carol's Library ready, her sign-in registers Magnus for her computer's address with exactly the manifest's access and PKCE, for the read-only server, and Supabase answers with its authorization page, not an error. Red with the appliance built without the manifest ("supabase is not in carol's Library"), green with it. `every_manifest_is_well_formed` covers the manifest.

## Cloudflare in the catalog (2026-11-03, `catalog-cloudflare`)

- Owner, 2026-11-03: "All of the connectors in pr117 need to be added to magnus"; the connectors spec uses a provider's own hosted server where it is adequate. Cloudflare's (`https://mcp.cloudflare.com/mcp`) is: its whole API (over 2,500 endpoints: DNS, Workers, R2, Zero Trust and the rest) and its documentation, through three tools. Its product servers (`docs.`, `browser.`, `containers.` and others) are not added: Cloudflare says the main one covers them.
- Magnus registers itself there (`registration = "dcr"`), checked live 2026-11-03: the server's 401 names its protected-resource metadata and the scopes it requires (`user:read account:read`); its authorization server `https://mcp.cloudflare.com` offers registration (`/register`), PKCE S256, clients with no secret, and CIMD. A registration for `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/callback` with no secret was accepted (201), and the sign-in address built for it was answered with Cloudflare's consent page ("Authorize Magnus | Cloudflare").
- Scopes `user:read offline_access account:read`: the ones its server requires (cloudflare/mcp `REQUIRED_SCOPES`, at 4938f2f), its least. Asking for no more leaves Cloudflare's page on its minimal set; the person can pick more there ("Read only", "Full access"). A request that needs more is refused by Cloudflare with a step-up challenge, and Magnus asks the person to sign in again approving it, as for any app.
- Tools, from its source (cloudflare/mcp at 4938f2f, Code Mode, its default; read 2026-11-03): its list needs a sign-in, so it was not read from the live server, and no test checks it for drift until a test account signs in. Classes, the owner's to review (ruling 5):
  - read (2): `search` (runs code against Cloudflare's published API description only, not the account), `docs` (searches Cloudflare's documentation).
  - irreversible (1): `execute`. It runs code Magnus writes against any Cloudflare endpoint the sign-in reaches, deletes and deploys included, so one tool name says nothing of what a call does; each call asks the person, who sees the code. Not prohibited: it is the only tool that reaches the account at all, and prohibiting it would leave a card that can only read documentation; the sign-in's scopes, minimal unless the person chose more on Cloudflare's page, bound what any call can do. If the owner wants per-request judgement instead, Cloudflare offers each endpoint as its own tool (`?codemode=false`, about 2,600 tools), which would need each classed.
- Audience public: `execute` can change DNS, Workers and sites that anyone reaches. Label private+untrusted: what comes back is the account's configuration and data, some written by others.
- Test: `cloudflare_is_in_every_library_and_magnus_registers_itself_at_its_sign_in` (cloudflare.rs, against Cloudflare, through `magnus_scratch::catalog`, as Sentry's): in carol's Library ready, her sign-in registers Magnus for her computer's address with exactly the manifest's access and PKCE, and Cloudflare answers with its consent page, not an error. Red with the appliance built without the manifest ("cloudflare is not in carol's Library"), green with it. `every_manifest_is_well_formed` covers the manifest.

## The console tests' step-up window outlasts a sign-in (2026-11-04, `step-up-window`)

- The admin tests `admin_security`, `admin_operations`, `admin_people`, `admin_knowledge` and `admin_users` failed now and then in CI (#123, #125, #126) with `step_up_required` on the first change made right after a fresh sign-in. Each was rerun to green.
- The cause is in the tests, not the console. Measured with a log of each step-up check's age:
  - The console's check was right every time: a sign-in typed a password at the provider (`auth_time` after the sign-in began), so the session counted as stepped up from its callback.
  - The tests ran with a 20-second step-up window. The browser sign-in (`desktop/tests/admin-sign-in.mjs`) then waits for the console to draw and closes Chrome before it hands back the cookie. That took 0.6 to 23 seconds after the callback, at a load of 12.
  - At 23 seconds the session's step-up had lapsed before the test used it, and the console refused, rightly.
- The fix: these tests use a 60-second window. They still sleep past it before checking that a lapsed step-up is refused, so each takes about 40 seconds longer; they run beside the other suites.
- `admin_shell` (20 seconds) is unchanged. Its step-up is used inside the same browser page right after the password, with no hand-back to wait for.
- No test can fail without this change on demand, since it is a matter of timing. Before it, admin_operations failed once in three runs here, at line 126, with the check log showing the session's step-up 23 seconds old.

## A brokered connector's changed definitions are its new manifest version's (2026-11-03, `brokered-tools-repin`)

- Found on the owner's appliance (event log, 2026-11-04 00:35 UTC): when the brokered Gmail first listed its tools, each of the 21 it shares by name with the old hosted server differed from its pin, so each got a `ToolChanged` and an owner `tool_change` card, and stayed disabled until re-approved. Only the four new names were offered, so there was no draft to send: "missing tools needed to send emails".
- Calendar was disabled the same way (owner, 2026-11-04): all nine of the brokered Calendar's tools got a `ToolChanged` at 00:35 UTC, since each shares its name with the hosted server's. The fix is by transport, so it covers Calendar, and every later brokered connector replacing a hosted one (Drive, Chat), with no change of its own.
- The guard is for a tool "that appears or changes after its manifest was signed" (connectors spec, Remote MCP connectors), and a changed tool reaches the agent "by the next manifest version". A brokered connector's definitions come from the image its manifest names by digest, checked before every boot (`brokered-image`, `brokered-runtime`): they cannot change after the manifest was signed without the digest changing, so a definition that differs from its pin is the next manifest version's. In `Kernel::pin`, for `transport = "brokered"`, it is pinned as a first sight (`ToolPinned`, approved false), with no `ToolChanged` and no `tool_change` card; the owner still reviews its description, as any first sight's (the description reaches the model only once approved).
- A `tool_change` card still open for that tool is resolved by the kernel in the same step (`ItemResolved`, the one event that puts away any card, decision `superseded`, saying why), so the owner's 21 stale cards clear on the first listing after this deploys.
- A remote server can change its definitions at any time, so for it the guard is unchanged (`inv1_boundary.rs` `a_changed_tool_definition_is_disabled_and_hidden`, and the remote half of the test below).
- Test: `gmail_s_tools_from_its_hosted_server_are_offered_from_its_image` (gmail.rs). Gmail is first a remote server (a FakeMcp, as the invariants' apps) whose `create_draft` is pinned, then changes there: it is no longer offered and a change card opens. Then Gmail becomes the brokered one, its image built from this checkout: `create_draft` is offered and the draft reaches Gmail's API; no change card is open, the remote's is resolved `superseded`, and the image's description is put to the owner for review. Without the re-pin it fails (the draft never reaches Gmail); without resolving the card it fails (a change card is still open).

## Replace the hosted Chat connector (2026-11-04)

- The owner requested replacement after direct probes showed the hosted MCP tool list returns 403 with the existing unexpired credential and 200 without it. Port the existing Chat implementation onto current master, retaining its brokered tool re-pinning fix and the Google client's uncertain-write handling. Keep the stable `google-chat` identity and per-person credential; the manifest changes from remote to brokered and advances to 2.0.0 as the connector spec requires.
- A read-only request to `chat.googleapis.com/v1/spaces?pageSize=1` with the existing credential returns HTTP 400 `FAILED_PRECONDITION`: "Google Chat is turned off. To use Chat API, turn on Google Chat." This is a separate account setup blocker; replacement does not enable the user's Google service. No real messages were sent, edited or deleted.
- Replacement review found `list_members` silently dropped further pages. It now accepts `page_token` and exposes `next_page_token`; the all-tools VM regression failed with "the second membership page was not requested" before this fix. The same test proves editing and deleting each wait for their own approval.

- Chat replacement delivery (2026-11-04): merge master `9998e01` after #133 replaces the one-open-PR gate; proceed with a separate Chat PR under the six-agent-PR cap. The earlier queue blocker no longer applies.

- Review of Chat replacement #134: Google allows space managers to delete other members' messages ([deletion guide](https://developers.google.com/workspace/chat/delete-messages)), so provider authorization alone does not enforce the catalog's own-message promise. Before DELETE, fetch the account from Google's published UserInfo endpoint with the same current access token, read the message, and require its human sender's canonical Google ID to match.
