Missing identity, missing sender or a mismatch sends no DELETE. No model-supplied identity is accepted. `openid` and `openidconnect.googleapis.com` are added solely for this check; email/profile access is not requested. Google's [OpenID documentation](https://developers.google.com/identity/openid-connect/openid-connect) identifies `sub` as the Google ID; [Chat User](https://developers.google.com/workspace/chat/api/reference/rest/v1/User) documents canonical user IDs. The new VM regression fails before this fix because another member's message reaches DELETE, even after approval. The positive own-message deletion and unknown-outcome checks remain covered; unit cases reject incomplete identity and bot senders. The rebuilt image is pinned in the manifest.
- `migrate` sets the kernel role's attributes (no superuser, createdb, createrole, replication or bypassrls) whether it creates the role or finds it, then refuses if the role can still UPDATE, DELETE or TRUNCATE `public.events` through any grant or membership (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: a role made beforehand kept what it had (only its password was set), so a superuser or `pg_write_all_data` role could change the log, which the decision says the database makes append-only; the invariant test always makes a fresh role, so it could not catch this. The build box's appliance role passes the check. Test: `migrate_leaves_the_kernel_role_unable_to_change_the_log` (log_review.rs).

- The adapter protocol's messages that can carry a credential (`Envelope`, `Request`, `CompleteParams`, `Event`) print (`{:?}`) as their JSON with every credential, and a `credential` event's value, replaced by `[redacted]` (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: the kernel prints messages it did not expect (`unexpected answer: {other:?}`) into errors, tracing, `SecretRevoked.detail` and the admin console, and a refreshed credential arriving at the wrong moment would have been written there, against invariant 2. Test: `printing_a_message_never_shows_its_credential` (adapter-proto).
- The desktop app's API proxy judges a path as it will be sent (review, 2026-11-04): a path that dot-segment normalisation would change, or one holding an encoded dot, slash or backslash (`%2e`, `%2f`, `%5c`) or a backslash, is refused before the sign-in, sign-out and devices prefixes are checked. Reason: `/v1/./sign-in/device` passed the string check and was then normalised by the URL parser to `/v1/sign-in/device`, which hands the session token to the page; the proxy exists so the page never sees it. Test: `the_page_cannot_reach_beyond_the_api_or_take_a_session` (desktop lib.rs).
- A request to the embedding server times out after `[knowledge.embeddings] timeout_s` (default 30 seconds, as long as the identity provider's requests may take), and then counts as failed, so search falls back to Postgres full-text (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: the client had no timeout, so a server that took the connection and never answered stalled `knowledge_search`, `knowledge_add` and the knowledge API for ever; the decision says search is full-text "when it fails", and a hang never failed. Test: `a_silent_embedding_server_does_not_stall_search` (knowledge.rs).

## A task's summary read in conversation carries what its task read (2026-11-04, `chat-controls-taint`)

- Found by the review of master against the specs (2026-11-04). In conversation, `list_tasks` and `list_schedules` return what each run said it did (its summary, or a schedule's latest run's). Those words are the run's: a task that read untrusted mail and private data writes them from both. But the two tools were labelled as the principal's own words (`tools.rs`), so a turn that listed them stayed clean, and its next web lookup could carry the summary out with no Rule-of-Two ask (invariant 4).
- Now a list is labelled as each run whose summary it returned, and names the apps those runs read private data from (`listed_taint`, `controls.rs`), as `knowledge_search` and `project_read` are labelled by what they return. A row without words (another person's while their data is private, or a run that has said nothing yet) adds nothing. The run's state is read after the list, and taint only grows, so a run that reads more in between can only make the label stricter.
- Test: `a_listed_task_summary_taints_the_turn_that_reads_it` (chat_controls.rs): Bob's task reads his untrusted, private mail and ends with a summary of it; in conversation Magnus lists his tasks, then looks up a web address. The turn is untrusted and private and waits for Bob's approval, and the address is never fetched. On master the turn is only untrusted (from the lookup itself), completes, and the address is fetched.
- The identity provider's HTTP clients (`oidc.rs`, sign-in and the status client) follow no redirects (review, 2026-11-04). Reason: reqwest's default follows https→http, and a 307 or 308 replays the POST, so a token endpoint answering with a redirect would have the person's password and code, or the status client's secret, resent wherever it pointed; PROGRESS claimed HTTPS enforcement including redirects, which only `web.rs` and the desktop client had. Keycloak answers discovery and the token endpoint directly (checked on the build box's Keycloak: 200, 401, no redirect). Test: `a_redirecting_token_endpoint_never_gets_the_password_resent` (oidc.rs).
- `migrate` moves the content of events written whole (before the content store existed, or by a release installed without `migrate`) into `public.content`, leaving the record with its salted hash as the kernel writes events now (review, 2026-11-04). Each event moves in one statement (insert its content row, then rewrite its body), so a crash leaves it whole or moved; only kinds in `events::CONTENT` whose record has no `content` hash are read, so a later `migrate` changes nothing and deleted content is not looked for. This is the one rewrite of a log row, done by the administrator, never the kernel, whose role cannot update the log. Reason: content must be deletable (invariant 3), and line 235's "step 4's migration moves such content out" was never built. Test: `migrate_moves_content_written_into_the_log_to_the_content_store` (log_review.rs).

## A tool that stays in the person's own account does not count as sending (2026-11-04, `reach-in-manifests`)

- The owner's "Magnus Approvals and Shared Computer Spec" (2026-11-04, https://claude.ai/artifact/G9NKQjsx2igX33Soo6qRBK) is built in its eleven steps, each its own pull request; this is step 1. The owner approved, in the spec, adding its destination tests to `tests/invariants/` (each with the step that makes it pass) and the risk it accepts: wrong content can reach an already allowed recipient without a card. The owner's ruling of the same day replaces "Keep asking each time" (2026-11-02), which they called AI bureaucracy; the steps that bring grants (3 onwards) record what changes there.
- Why email asked every time: every catalog connector's content is private and untrusted, so after one read the Rule of Two asked before every write, including a label, the trash or a draft, which reach no one.
- Now each tool may declare its reach (`[reach]` in a manifest, `reach` on a configured tool): `self`, it stays in the person's own account. Neither the Rule of Two nor the audience rule counts a `self` tool as sending, as for a write to Magnus's memory. Policy still applies: `delete_draft` is irreversible and asks by policy. A tool that declares nothing is unknown and asks as before. `self` fits only a write: a read reaches no one to begin with, money asks every time (a tool reclassified as money stops counting as `self`), and any other word is refused when the configuration loads. A signed manifest's reach counts as the owner's classification, as its classes do (ruling 7). An owner's reclassification of a tool keeps its reach.
- Gmail (manifest 1.2.0) declares `self` for exactly the spec's list: drafts written, replied, forwarded, updated and deleted; labels; trash and spam, both ways. `send_draft` is not in `[reach]`: who it reaches is the draft's To, Cc and Bcc, read at the gate (`[destinations.send_draft]`, step 2, already on master). Both steps are on this branch, so the manifest is 1.2.0 rather than either step's 1.1.0. The other catalog connectors declare nothing until each is checked tool by tool; a Drive file, a calendar event or a chat message can reach other people.
- Tests: `having_read_mail_gmail_labels_and_drafts_without_asking_and_asks_only_before_the_send` (gmail.rs), the spec's "Tests that change": having read her mail, Magnus labels the thread and writes the draft with no card, and asks once, before the send, which reads the draft; the task stays untrusted and private. On master the label asks first. `a_tool_reaches_self_only_as_a_write` (config.rs) and the catalog's `every_manifest_is_well_formed`: a reach is `self` on a write the manifest classes, never on a read, money or a prohibited tool.
- The kernel binds its control socket before it serves the network (`control::bind`, then the serving spawned), and a failure to bind is logged as before (2026-11-05). Reason: bound inside a spawned task, the socket could still be the previous process's when the appliance first answered, so the console, or a test, connecting just after a restart got "connection refused" (`admin_audit` in #159's CI). No new test: the race is timing-dependent and does not fail reliably on an unloaded machine; `admin_audit`'s restart test is the one that caught it.
- The browser snapshot never prints the value of a secret field (2026-11-05): an input of type `password`, or any field whose `autocomplete` names `current-password`, `new-password`, `one-time-code`, `cc-number`, `cc-csc` or `cc-exp*`, shows as `= (hidden)` when filled, and its value is never used as its label (`browser/sidecar.js`). Reason: the snapshot is text the model reads, and it printed every filled field's value, so a password or code a person typed reached the model; the Approvals and Shared Computer spec's "Password values never enter a snapshot" rests on this. The field keeps its ref so it can still be targeted, and the marker says it is filled. Screenshots are unchanged: Chromium draws password fields masked; a one-time-code or card field the page itself draws in clear is shown as the page shows it. Test: `browser_snapshot_never_shows_a_secret_field_value` (tools.rs), which on master printed the password, code and card number twice each, as label and value.
- The kill switch closes the browser: once every running run is suspended, the sidecar closes every run's context with the browser itself (`close_all`), whether or not the records were written, and the next request launches a new one, so a run continued later starts blank (2026-11-05). Reason: a suspension kept the run's context, so its pages' scripts went on under the switch, the waiting runs' as well (found reviewing the approvals and shared computer spec). Closed after the suspensions, so no run opens a page after; a context being made when it closes is closed too. Erasing a person cancels their runs, which already closes theirs; turning an account off stops no runs, so it is not changed here. Tests: `the_kill_switch_closes_every_runs_pages` (`kill_switch.rs`), a page's script calling home stops for both a running and a waiting run; `closing_the_browser_closes_every_runs_pages_and_a_later_request_starts_blank` (`tools.rs`).

## Test consoles listen below the ephemeral range (2026-11-05, #182)

- The admin console tests take a console's port below the system's ephemeral range (20000-31999, from a random start, checked free), not a port 0 bind released for later (2026-11-05). Reason: a port the system handed out could be taken again, by any of the tests' outgoing connections or port-0 binds, before the console bound it, and the test then talked to whatever held it: `InvalidCertificate(BadSignature)` from another test's console (`admin_operations`, #164's CI). Below the range only another console picking the same port can take it, about one chance in 12,000. `admin_on_appliance` uses the same helper. A test-harness fix; no product change. This is the substance of closed #166, re-applied from current master; that pull request is not reopened. Test: `a_console_port_is_below_the_ephemeral_range` (admin.rs). On the old helper it fails: port 0 returned 41055, inside the ephemeral range (32768-60999).

## One registered client per authorization server (2026-11-05, #208)

- Magnus keeps the client it registers at an app's authorization server (RFC 7591) in the vault (`registered_client <registration endpoint> <redirect>`) and uses it again for every later sign-in there, by anyone on the appliance (2026-11-05, #208). Reason: gitlab.com lets one address register about ten clients an hour, and Magnus registered on every sign-in, so an office connecting GitLab more than ten times an hour was refused, and every CI run spent one.
- The kept redirect leaves out the port of a loopback IP address (127.0.0.1, [::1]), so one client serves every sign-in from the person's computer, whose port changes each time (2026-11-05, #208). Reason: RFC 8252 §7.3 has the provider accept any port there. GitLab.com's Doorkeeper 5.9.9 (`URIChecker.matches?`) and Cloudflare's workers-oauth-provider (`isValidRedirectUri`, which Cloudflare and Sentry run on) match it so. `localhost` is a name and stays exact. An anonymous request cannot show this on gitlab.com: its sign-in sends every address to its login page first, so the check was its source.
- Before reusing a kept client, Magnus asks the provider's sign-in for the address once. A 400 or 401 whose answer names the client (GitLab.com answers 401 invalid_client before anyone signs in; Cloudflare's library answers invalid_request "Invalid client_id") means the provider dropped the client: Magnus registers again, once, and keeps the new one. A 429, a 403 or a server error says nothing about the client, and the kept one stays, spending no registration (review of #215). A token exchange answering invalid_client forgets the kept client, so the next sign-in registers (2026-11-05, #208). Reason: a provider can delete a client, and a kept one must not lock everyone out of the app.
- The scratch OAuth provider matches loopback redirects as those providers do (2026-11-05, #208). Reason: it stood in for them with an exact match, which no provider Magnus registers with does. The scratch provider also answers an unknown client as GitLab.com does (401 invalid_client). Tests: `connectors.rs` `sign_ins_by_anyone_from_any_port_register_magnus_once` (fails on master: three registrations), `a_client_the_provider_dropped_is_registered_again` and `a_busy_provider_does_not_cost_a_registration`.

## A draft being sent is leased (2026-11-06, #177)

- A state-changing action leases the record it changes on its `id` or `record_id`, and also on the id field the app's manifest names in `[destinations]` (Gmail's `draft_id`), from the gate to the end of its run (2026-11-06, #177). Reason: the gate reads the draft to show and hash it, and sends it later by id; `update_draft` stays in the person's account and never asks, so another run could change the draft between the gate and the send. The field comes from the manifest, so no tool name is written into the kernel. The person editing the draft in Gmail is not fenced by this; the approval hash is, once it covers every part (the second half of #177). Test: `draft_lock.rs` `a_draft_being_sent_is_changed_by_no_other_run` (fails on master: the other run retitles the draft mid-send).
## An approval holds for every part of the email (2026-11-06, #177)

- A connector's `[destinations]` may name `bound` fields: the approval hash holds for them, and the card does not show them (2026-11-06, #177). Gmail binds `/message/digest`, which `get_draft` computes over every MIME part's type, file name and bytes, an attachment's bytes fetched by its id (its id changes on every read; its bytes do not). Reason: the hash covered the plain-text body, else the HTML, and attachments by name, type and size, so a draft changed only in its HTML part or an attachment's contents was sent on the old approval. The digest is unshown because a hash on a card tells the person nothing, and the HTML itself on the card would show the same text twice. Gmail's raw form (`format=raw`) was not hashed: whether Gmail returns an unchanged draft's raw form identically on every read cannot be checked without the test account, and if it did not, every send would ask forever. Gmail manifest 1.3.0. Test: `gmail.rs` `a_change_to_a_part_the_card_does_not_show_asks_again` (fails on master: the email changed in its HTML part is sent on the old approval).
